%---------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\section*[Intro]{Introduction}

In this article, we propose a proof of certain conjectures of Langlands, Shelstad, and Waldspurger, better known as the fundamental lemma for Lie algebras and the nonstandard fundamental lemma. For further details in the following statements, we refer to Sections \ref{LS} and \ref{Waldspurger}.

\begin{theorem}
Let $k$ be a finite field with $q$ elements, $\calO$ a complete discrete valuation ring with residue field $k$, and $F$ its fraction field. Let $G$ be a reductive group scheme over $\calO$ whose Weyl group order is not divisible by the characteristic of $k$. Let $(\kappa,\rho_\kappa)$ be an endoscopic datum for $G$ over $\calO$, and let $H$ be the associated endoscopic group scheme.

Then one has the equality between the $\kappa$-orbital integral and the stable orbital integral
$$
\Delta_G(a){\bf O}_a^\kappa(1_{\frakg},{\rm d}t)=\Delta_H(a_H){\bf SO}_{a_H}(1_{\frakh},{\rm d}t)
$$
attached to the corresponding regular semisimple stable conjugacy classes $a$ in $\frakg(F)$ and $a_H$ in $\frakh(F)$, to the characteristic functions $1_{\frakg}$ and $1_{\frakh}$ of the compact subsets $\frakg(\calO)$ and $\frakh(\calO)$ in $\frakg(F)$ and $\frakh(F)$ respectively, and where we have set
$$
\Delta_G(a)=q^{-{\rm val}(\discrim_G(a))/ 2}\quad \text{and}\quad \Delta_H(a_H)=q^{-{\rm val}(\discrim_H(a_H))/ 2},
$$
with $\discrim_G$ and $\discrim_H$ denoting the discriminant functions of $G$ and $H$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}
Let $G_1$ and $G_2$ be two reductive group schemes over $\calO$ having isogenous root data and whose Weyl group orders are not divisible by the characteristic of $k$. Then, one has the following equality between the stable orbital integrals
$$
{\bf SO}_{a_1}(1_{\frakg_1},{\rm d}t)= {\bf SO}_{a_2}(1_{\frakg_2},{\rm d}t)
$$
attached to the corresponding regular semisimple stable conjugacy classes $a_1$ in $\frakg_1(F)$ and $a_2$ in $\frakg_2(F)$, and to the characteristic functions $1_{\frakg_1}$ and $1_{\frakg_2}$ of the compact sets $\frakg_1(\calO)$ and $\frakg_2(\calO)$ in $\frakg_1(F)$ and $\frakg_2(F)$ respectively.
\end{theorem}

We prove these theorems in the equal-characteristic case. According to Waldspurger, the case of mixed characteristic follows (see \cite{W}).

The main applications of the fundamental lemma are found in the realization of certain particular cases of Langlands' functoriality principle via the comparison of trace formulas and in the construction of Galois representations attached to automorphic forms through the computation of the cohomology of Shimura varieties. For applications to the comparison of trace formulas, we refer to the work of Arthur \cite{A}, and for those to Shimura varieties, to Kottwitz \cite{K-Shim} and to Harris's forthcoming book.

\subsubsection*{Known Cases and Reductions}
The fundamental lemma has been established in a great number of particular cases. Its archimedean analogue was completely solved by Shelstad in \cite{Sh}. This case inspired Langlands and Shelstad to formulate their conjecture for a nonarchimedean field. The case of the group $\SL(2)$ was treated by Labesse and Langlands in \cite{LL}. The case of the three-variable unitary group was resolved by Rogawski in \cite{Rog}. Cases related to the twisted $\Sp(4)$ and $\GL(4)$ have been solved by Hales, Schr\"oder, and Weissauer via explicit computations (see \cite{Hales-Sp(4)}, \cite{Sch} and \cite{We}). More recently, Whitehouse continued these computations to prove the weighted twisted fundamental lemma in this case (see \cite{Wh}).

The fundamental lemma for stable base change was established by Clozel \cite{C} and Labesse \cite{Lab}, starting from the case of the unit element of the Hecke algebra proved by Kottwitz \cite{K-unit}. Previously, the case of $\GL(2)$ was established by Saito and Shintani \cf \cite{Saito} and by Langlands \cf \cite{L-GL(2)}, and the case of $\GL(3)$ by Kottwitz \cf \cite{K-GL(3)}.

Another important case is that of $\SL(n)$, which was resolved by Waldspurger in \cite{W-SL}. The case of $\SL(3)$ with an elliptic torus was established earlier by Kottwitz (see \cite{K-SL(3)}) and the case of $\SL(n)$ with an elliptic torus by Kazhdan (see \cite{Ka}).

Recently, together with Laumon, we proved the fundamental lemma for the Lie algebras of unitary groups in the equal-characteristic case. Our method is geometric and applies only to local fields of equal characteristic. As already mentioned, the mixed-characteristic case then follows from Waldspurger's work \cite{W}. The change of characteristic was also established by Cluckers and Loeser \cite{CL2} in a more general framework but with less precision regarding the bound on the residual characteristic.

In a series of works, notably \cite{W-Trans} and \cite{W-tordu}, Waldspurger showed that the fundamental lemma for groups as well as the transfer is deduced from the ordinary fundamental lemma for Lie algebras. Similarly, the twisted fundamental lemma is deduced from the combination of the ordinary lemma for Lie algebras and what he calls the nonstandard lemma. In the remainder of this article, we restrict ourselves to the ordinary fundamental lemma for Lie algebras and its nonstandard variant over a local field of equal characteristic.

\subsubsection*{Local Geometric Approach}
Kazhdan and Lusztig introduced in \cite{KL} the affine Springer fibers, which are geometric incarnations of orbital integrals. That work provides basic information on the geometry of affine Springer fibers, which we recall in Chapter \ref{section : Fibres de Springer affines} of the present article.

In the appendix to \cite{KL}, Bernstein and Kazhdan constructed an affine Springer fiber for the group $\Sp(6)$ whose point count is not a polynomial in $q$. In fact, the associated motive of this affine Springer fiber contains the motive of a hyperelliptic curve. This example suggests that it is unlikely that one can obtain an explicit formula for the orbital integrals.

The interpretation of the $\kappa$-orbital integrals in terms of the quotients of affine Springer fibers was established in the work of Goresky, Kottwitz, and MacPherson \cite{GKM}. They also introduced in \cite{GKM} the use of equivariant cohomology in the study of affine Springer fibers. This strategy is very well adapted to the particular case of elements belonging to an unramified torus since in that case one has an action of a large torus on the affine Springer fiber with only isolated fixed points. They also discovered a remarkable relation between the equivariant cohomology of an affine Springer fiber for $G$ and the corresponding fiber for the endoscopic group $H$ in this unramified setting. This relation also depends on a purity conjecture for the cohomology of these affine Springer fibers. This conjecture was verified for elements with equal root valuations in \cite{GKM-equivaluation}.

In \cite{L} and \cite{L-LFU}, Laumon introduced a method of deforming Springer fibers in the case of unitary groups based on the deformation theory of plane curves. His strategy is to introduce a plane curve of geometric genus zero having a singularity prescribed by the local situation and then to deform this plane curve. He also observed that in the unitary case, there exists a one-dimensional torus acting on the affine Springer fibers of $U(n)$ associated with a stable class coming from $U(n_1)\times U(n_2)$, whose fixed point set is the corresponding affine Springer fiber for $U(n_1)\times U(n_2)$. By computing the equivariant cohomology in family, he was able to prove the fundamental lemma in the unitary case, even for possibly ramified elements. His proof again relies on the purity conjecture for affine Springer fibers formulated by Goresky, Kottwitz, and MacPherson.

This purity conjecture seemed to me to be the main obstacle in the local geometric approach. It plays an essential role in degenerating certain spectral sequences and allows one to apply the Atiyah-Borel-Segal localization theorem.

In fact, there is another obstacle, at least as serious, to using equivariant cohomology for general affine Springer fibers. For very ramified elements of groups other than the unitary groups, there is no torus action on the corresponding affine Springer fiber. In this case, even if one has the purity conjecture, it is not clear that the strategies of \cite{GKM} and \cite{L-LFU} can be applied.

\subsubsection*{Global Geometric Approach}
In \cite{H}, Hitchin showed that the cotangent bundle of the moduli space of stable bundles over a compact Riemann surface is a completely integrable Hamiltonian system. To do this, he interpreted this cotangent bundle as the moduli space of principal bundles equipped with a Higgs field. The Hamiltonians are then given by the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial of the Higgs field. In this way, he defined the famous Hitchin fibration $f:\calM\rightarrow\calA$, where $\calM$ is the aforementioned cotangent bundle, $\calA$ is the affine space parametrizing the characteristic polynomials with coefficients in the global sections of suitable powers of the canonical bundle of $X$, and where $f$ is a morphism whose generic fiber is essentially an abelian variety.

From our point of view, the Hitchin bundles are global analogues of affine Springer fibers. Moreover, it is important in our approach to allow the Higgs bundles to have values in an arbitrary line bundle of sufficiently large degree instead of the canonical bundle. In this generality, the moduli space of Higgs bundles $\calM$ is no longer endowed with a symplectic form but still admits a Hitchin fibration $f:\calM\rightarrow \calA$.

We observed in \cite{N} that a formal point count of $\calM$ over a finite field yields an expression nearly identical to the geometric side of the trace formula for the Lie algebra. In \cite{N}, we proposed to interpret the stabilization process of the Langlands-Kottwitz trace formula in terms of the $\ell$-adic cohomology of the Hitchin fibration. This interpretation leads to a formulation of a global variant of the fundamental lemma in terms of the $\ell$-adic cohomology of the Hitchin fibration (see \cite{Dat} and \cite{N-ICM}). The geometric interpretation of the stabilization process with the Hitchin fibration, as well as the formulation of this global variant of the fundamental lemma, is more complex than the local analogue with affine Springer fibers. On the other hand, one enjoys a much richer geometry.

The geometric interpretation of the stabilization process is based on the action of a Picard stack $\calP\rightarrow \calA$ on $\calM$, which is in some sense the stack of natural symmetries of the Hitchin fibration. The point count with the help of $\calP$, carried out in Section 9 of \cite{N}, is revisited in a more systematic way in the final Chapter \ref{section : comptage} of the present article. The construction of $\calP$ is based on that of the regular centralizer, which we recall in Chapter \ref{section : centralisateur regulier}. An important observation in the point count is that for every $a\in\calM(k)$, the quotient $[\calM_a/\calP_a]$ can be expressed as a product of local quotients of the affine Springer fibers $\calM_v(a)$ by their natural symmetry groups $\calP_v(J_a)$ (see \ref{produit} and \cite[4.6]{N}). This product formula appears implicitly throughout the article.

The algebraic stack $\calM$ is not of finite type. However, there exists an anisotropic open subset $\calA^\ani$ of $\calA$, constructed in Chapter \ref{section : anisotrope}, over which the restricted fibration $f^\ani:\calM^\ani \rightarrow \calA^\ani$ is a proper morphism. It is known that $\calM^\ani$ is smooth over the base field $k$. According to Deligne \cite{Weil2}, the derived direct image $f^\ani_* \Ql$ is a pure complex - that is, the perverse cohomology sheaves
% [journal, Introduction] the journal uses no K^n shorthand here.
$${}^p\rmH^n(f^\ani_*\Ql)$$
are pure perverse sheaves. By the decomposition theorem, these become semisimple after base change to $\calA^\ani\otimes_k \bar k$. In order to prove the fundamental lemma, it is essential to understand the geometrically simple factors in this decomposition.

The action of $\calP$ on $\calM$ induces an action of $\calP^\ani$ on the perverse sheaves ${}^p\rmH^n(f^\ani_*\Ql)$ and decomposes them into isotypic components
$${}^p\rmH^n(f^\ani_*\Ql)=\bigoplus_{[\kappa]}{}^p\rmH^n(f^\ani_*\Ql)_{[\kappa]}.$$
Here the index $[\kappa]$ runs over the set of semisimple conjugacy classes in the dual group $\hat G$, only finitely many of which have a non-trivial contribution. We denote by ${}^p\rmH^n(f^\ani_*\Ql)_{\rm st}$ the direct factor corresponding to $\kappa=1$. The appearance of the dual group here is a consequence of the computation of the sheaf of connected components of the fibers of $\calP$ via the Tate-Nakayama isomorphism, which was initiated in \cite[6]{N} and completed in Sections \ref{subsection : pi_0(P_a)} and \ref{subsection : description pi_0(P)} of the present article. In \cite{N} and \cite{N-ICM}, we showed that this decomposition exactly corresponds to the endoscopic decomposition on the geometric side of the trace formula established by Langlands and Kottwitz (cf. \cite{Langlands} and \cite{K-EST}).

% [journal, Introduction] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION. This is the paragraph in which
% the point of view changes to the etale cover Atilde^ani and the FINER decomposition
% indexed by kappa in the maximal torus Tautilde -- the setup used from chapter 5 onward.
% The arXiv paragraph it replaces (on unramified endoscopic groups attached to kappa) has
% no journal counterpart.
In this work we change our point of view slightly. Instead of working over $\calA^\ani$, we pass to an \'etale cover $\tilde\calA^\ani$ depending on the choice of a point $\infty\in X$, just as in \cite{LN}. Over this \'etale open set we have a finer decomposition
% [journal, Introduction] the journal prints the index of this direct sum as [kappa],
% carried over from the previous display; the sentence that follows says kappa runs over a
% finite subset of Tautilde, so the index must be kappa. Corrected here.
$${}^p\rmH^n(\tilde f^\ani_*\Ql)=\bigoplus_{\kappa}{}^p\rmH^n(\tilde f^\ani_*\Ql)_\kappa,$$
where $\kappa$ runs over a finite subset of the maximal torus $\hat\bbT$ of the dual group $\hat G$. We no longer stick exactly to the trace formula, but in return we rid ourselves of a number of incidental difficulties. Our geometric stabilization theorem \ref{stabilisation sur tilde A geom} consists in expressing the piece ${}^p\rmH^n(\tilde f^\ani_*\Ql)_\kappa$ in terms of endoscopic groups. In fact one expresses it as a direct sum of the stable pieces --- that is, those corresponding to $\kappa=1$ --- but for the groups $H$ associated with pointed endoscopic data \cf \ref{donnee endoscopique pointee}. There is an arithmetic variant \ref{stabilisation sur tilde A} of \ref{stabilisation sur tilde A geom}, of which the fundamental lemma of Langlands-Shelstad is a consequence.

In \cite{N} we showed that ${}^p\rmH^n(\tilde f^\ani_*\Ql)_\kappa$ is supported on the union of the images of the morphisms $\tilde\calA^\ani_H\rightarrow\tilde\calA^\ani$, or rather a variant of this. We take up those arguments again in \ref{subsection : immersion fermee}. As we observed in \cite{N} and \cite{LN}, this statement can replace the purity conjecture of Goresky, Kottwitz and MacPherson in the context of the Hitchin fibration. Moreover, it is very tempting to conjecture that all the simple factors of ${}^p\rmH^n(\tilde f^\ani_*\Ql)_\kappa$ have as support the image of one of the closed immersions $\tilde\calA^\ani_H\rightarrow\tilde\calA^\ani$. Note that the geometric stabilization theorem follows from this support conjecture, since in fact it is not difficult to establish it over a dense open subset of $\tilde\calA^\ani_H$.

In the unitary case, Laumon and I used the Atiyah-Borel-Segal exact sequence in equivariant cohomology to prove an ad hoc variant of the support statement above. My later attempts to generalize this equivariant method to other groups were hampered by the absence of a torus action on certain Hitchin fibers associated to highly ramified elements.

In this work we prove, for all groups, a weak form of this support conjecture. In Chapter \ref{support} we show that one can determine all the supports in the case of a $\delta$-regular abelian fibration \cf \ref{support general}. Since it is not possible at present to prove that the Hitchin fibration is $\delta$-regular --- see however \cite[p.~4]{N-preparation} --- we use certain intermediate statements \ref{inegalite delta} and \ref{support general kappa}, combined with what is available in positive characteristic in the direction of $\delta$-regularity \ref{codimension} and an intensive point count in Chapter \ref{section : comptage}, to conclude the proof of \ref{stabilisation sur tilde A}.

The delta inequality \ref{inegalite delta}, whose proof occupies Chapter \ref{support}, allows one to bound from below the codimension of the supports in the case of an abelian fibration. It is in fact an elaborate variant of the inequality on the codimension of supports due to Goresky and MacPherson, which we recall in \ref{subsection : inegalite GM}.

Let us now review the organization of the article. The first chapter contains the statement of the fundamental lemma for Lie algebras and its nonstandard variant. The heart of the article is the next-to-last chapter \ref{support}, where we prove the inequalities \ref{inegalite delta} and \ref{support general kappa}, from which a support theorem \ref{support faible} will be deduced. The final chapter \ref{section : comptage} contains the point counting argument that allows one to deduce the fundamental lemma from the support theorem \ref{support faible}. The other chapters are of a preparatory nature. Chapter \ref{section : Fibres de Springer affines} contains a study of the geometry of affine Springer fibers. The following chapter \ref{section : Fibration de Hitchin} presents a parallel study of the geometry of the Hitchin fibration. Our favorite tool in these studies is the action of the natural symmetries based on the construction of the regular centralizer recalled in Chapter \ref{section : centralisateur regulier}. In Chapter \ref{section : stratification}, various stratifications of the Hitchin base are defined. In the next chapter \ref{section : anisotrope}, the anisotropic open subset of the Hitchin base is defined over which the fibration has all the good properties so that one may formulate the geometric stabilization theorem \ref{stabilisation sur tilde A}, which will be proved in the final two chapters \ref{support} and \ref{section : comptage}. At the beginning of each chapter, the reader will find a summary of its content and the notations used.
% [journal, Introduction] the arXiv sentences promising an appendix on Goresky-MacPherson
% (now 7.3) and a table of contents at the END of the article have no journal counterpart:
% the journal carries its table of contents at the front.

\section{The Langlands-Shelstad and Waldspurger Conjectures}

In this chapter, we recall the statement of the fundamental lemma for Lie algebras conjectured by Langlands and Shelstad (see \ref{LS}), as well as the nonstandard variant conjectured by Waldspurger (see \ref{Waldspurger}). In the final paragraph of this chapter, we recall where the Langlands-Shelstad fundamental lemma enters in the stabilization process on the geometric side of the trace formula. Our study of the Hitchin fibration, which will be carried out later in this article, is directly inspired by this stabilization process. We have tried to keep the presentation in this chapter as elementary as possible.

\subsection{Chevalley Morphism}
\label{subsection : Chevalley}

Let $k$ be a field and $\bbG$ a split reductive group over $k$. Our definition is that of \cite{SGA3}: reductive implies affine, smooth and connected. We fix a split maximal torus $\bbT$ and a Borel subgroup $\bbB$ containing $\bbT$. Let $N_G(\bbT)$ be the normalizer of $\bbT$ and $\bbW=N_G(\bbT)/\bbT$ be the Weyl group. We denote by $\bbg$ the Lie algebra of $\bbG$ and $k[\bbg]$
the algebra of regular functions on $\bbg$. Similarly, we denote by
$\bbt=\Spec(k[\bbt])$ the Lie algebra of $\bbT$. Let $r$ be the rank of
$\bbG$, which by definition is the dimension of the maximal torus $\bbT$.

% [journal 1.1] RESTORED. The arXiv text replaced the standing hypothesis of the whole
% paper (p = 0 or p > 2h) by its weaker consequence "p does not divide |W|", and dropped
% the paragraph defining roots, heights and h from which it is stated.
The action of $\bbT$ on the Lie algebra $\bbg$ defines a set of roots $\Phi$, on which the Weyl group $\bbW$ acts. The choice of the Borel subgroup $\bbB$ defines a set $\Delta$ of simple roots. Every root can be written uniquely in the form $\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}n_\alpha\alpha$ with $n_\alpha\in\ZZ$; the integer $\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}n_\alpha$ is then called the {\em height} of that root. We write $h-1$ for the maximal height of a root; if the root system is simple, $h$ is its Coxeter number. {\em Throughout this work we shall assume that the characteristic $p$ is either zero or greater than $2h$.} Since the order of the Weyl group is a product of natural numbers not exceeding $h$, this hypothesis implies in particular that $p$ does not divide the order of the Weyl group. It also implies that $p$ is a good prime in the sense of \cite[1.14]{Ca}, and hence that the Killing form is non-degenerate in the semisimple case.

The group $\bbG$ acts on its Lie algebra via the adjoint action. The algebra of $\bbG$-invariant functions on $\bbg$ is identified by the restriction from $\bbg$ to $\bbt$ with the algebra of
$\bbW$-invariant functions on $\bbt$.

% [journal 1.1] RESTORED: the journal names where the theorem is proved (semisimple adjoint
% case) and states that the general case is deduced under the hypothesis p > 2.
Here is the statement of the Chevalley restriction theorem, a proof of which may be found, in the semisimple adjoint case, in \cite[3.17]{S-S}. The general case is deduced from it under the hypothesis $p>2$ \cf \cite[0.8]{Levy}.

% [journal 1.1.1] The journal states only the isomorphism; the polynomial-algebra
% assertion is carried by the preceding sentence (Shephard-Todd-Chevalley), so the
% arXiv version's second sentence was a duplication. Trimmed to the journal's statement.
\begin{theoreme}\label{Chevalley}
By restriction from $\bbg$ to $\bbt$, we have an algebra isomorphism
$k[\bbg]^\bbG =k[\bbt]^\bbW$.
\end{theoreme}


We denote $\bbc=\Spec(k[\bbt]^\bbW)=\Spec(k[\bbg]^\bbG)$. The action of $\GG_m$ by homothety on $\bbg$ commutes with the adjoint action of $\bbG$, and therefore induces an action on $\bbc$.

The inclusion $k[\bbt]^\bbW\subset k[\bbt]$ defines a morphism
$\pi:\bbt\rightarrow\bbc$. It is a finite and flat morphism that realizes
$\bbc$ as the quotient in the sense of invariants of $\bbt$ by the action of
$\bbW$. Moreover, there exists a non-empty open subset $\bbc^\rs$ of $\bbc$
above which $\pi$ is a finite \'etale Galois morphism with Galois group
$\bbW$. This morphism is compatible with the action of $\GG_m$ by
homothety on $\bbt$ and the action of $\GG_m$ on $\bbc$ by exponents.

We denote $ \chi:\bbg\rta\bbc$ the characteristic Chevalley morphism
which is deduced from the inclusion of algebras $k[\bbg]^\bbG\subset k[\bbg]$. This
morphism is compatible with the action of $\GG_m$ by homothety on
$\bbg$ and the action of $\GG_m$ on $\bbc$ by exponents. By
analogy with the case of matrices, we will call $\chi(x)$ the characteristic
polynomial of $x$ and $\bbc$ the space of characteristic
polynomials.

In addition to the $\GG_m$-equivariance property, $\chi$ is $\bbG$-invariant
by construction. This gives rise to morphisms between
algebraic stacks
$$[\chi]:[\bbg/\bbG]\rta \bbc\ \mbox{ and }\ [\chi/\GG_m]:
[\bbg/\bbG\times\GG_m]\rta [\bbc/\GG_m].$$


\subsection{Kostant Section}\label{Section de Kostant}

% [journal 1.2] RESTORED: the entire opening paragraph was absent, including the hypothesis
% p > 2h under which Kostant's construction generalises, the Veldkamp attribution, and
% Deligne's remark that Veldkamp's argument appears incomplete.
The morphism $\chi:\bbg\rightarrow\bbc$ admits sections. In \cite{Kos}, Kostant constructed such a section in a uniform way. Although he assumes that the base field has characteristic zero, his construction generalizes to positive characteristic $p>2h$. In \cite{Veldkamp}, Veldkamp sought to construct the Kostant section under the weaker hypothesis that $p$ does not divide the order of the Weyl group; but, as Deligne pointed out to me, Veldkamp's argument appears to be incomplete.

Fix a pinning of $\GG$ which consists of the choice of a maximal
split torus $\bbT$, a Borel subgroup $\bbB$ containing $\bbT$ with
unipotent radical $\bbU$ and a vector $\bbx_{+}\in \Lie(\bbU)$ of the form
$\bbx_+=\sum_{\alpha\in \Delta}\bbx_\alpha$ where $\Delta$ is the set
of simple roots and where $\bbx_\alpha$ is a non-zero vector of the
eigenspace $\Lie(\bbU)_\alpha$ of $\Lie(\bbU)$ corresponding to
the eigenvalue $\alpha$ for the action of $\bbT$.

There then exists a unique $\mathfrak{sl}_2$-triple $(h,\bbx_+,\bbx_-)$ in
$\bbg$ with the semi-simple element $h\in \bbt\cap \Lie(\bbG^{\rm der})$.
% [journal 1.2] RESTORED: the justification of the sl_2-triple was absent -- the height
% argument, Jacobson-Morozov under p > 2h, and complete reducibility.
Indeed, arguing on the height of the roots, one shows that ${\rm ad}(\bbx_+)^{2h-1}=0$, as in \cite[5.5.2]{Ca}. One then has a theorem of Jacobson-Morozov type \cite[5.3.2]{Ca} under the hypothesis $p>2h$. One even knows that the principal representation of $\mathfrak{sl}_2$ on $\bbg$ is completely reducible under the hypothesis $p>2h$, by \cite[5.4.8]{Ca}.

The following statement was proved by Kostant in characteristic zero \cite[Theorem 0.10]{Kos}.

\begin{lemme}\label{Kostant}
Let $\bbg^{{\bbx}_+}$ be the centralizer of ${\bbx}_+$ in ${\bbg}$. The
restriction of the characteristic morphism $\chi:\bbg\rta\bbc$ to the
affine subspace ${\bbx}_-+\bbg^{{\bbx}_+}$ of the vector space $\bbg$ is an
isomorphism.
\end{lemme}

% [journal 1.2] RESTORED: the commentary explaining why Kostant's argument extends to
% characteristic p was absent.
He first observes that $\Lie(\bbB)=[\bbx_-,\Lie(\bbU)]\oplus\bbg^{\bbx_+}$. This equality follows from the complete reducibility of the principal representation of $\mathfrak{sl}_2$ in $\bbg$, which is valid under the hypothesis $p>2h$. His argument \cite[Prop.~19]{Kos} for deducing the lemma from this observation is in fact independent of the characteristic. It has also been presented on the last page of \cite{BD}.

The inverse of this isomorphism
\begin{equation}
\epsilon:\bbc\rta{\bbx}_-+\bbg^{{\bbx}_+}\hookrightarrow \bbg
\end{equation}
defines a section of the Chevalley morphism $\chi:\bbg\rightarrow\bbc$
called the Kostant section. In fact, Kostant constructed an entire
family of sections of the Chevalley morphism. For classical groups,
it is also possible to construct explicit sections $\bbc\rightarrow \bbg$
using linear algebra without using the pinning \cf
\cite{compagnon}. These sections, which generalize the companion matrix
in the linear case, are probably more adapted to explicit calculations
of affine Springer fibers and Hitchin fibers.

% [journal 1.2] MOVED FROM 1.1. The arXiv version placed the exponents/G_m-coordinates
% discussion, the definition of g^reg and the lemma below at the end of 1.1; in the
% journal they close 1.2, and the lemma is Lemme 1.2.3. Left in 1.1 the lemma printed
% as 1.1.2 instead of 1.2.3.
Let us return to the section constructed by Kostant. It follows from \ref{Kostant} that $\bbc$ is isomorphic to an affine space. The action of $\GG_m$ on $\bbG$ induces an action on $\bbc$. Using the complete reducibility of the principal $\mathfrak{sl}_2$-representation in $\bbg$, one may choose coordinates $a_1,\ldots,a_r$ of $\bbc$ so that this action is written
$$t(a_1,\ldots,a_r)=(t^{e_1}a_1,\ldots,t^{e_r}a_r).$$
The integers $e_1-1,\ldots,e_r-1$ are the exponents of the root system, see \cite{Bourbaki}.

Let $\bbg^\reg$ be the open subset of $\bbg$ consisting of the points $x\in\bbg$ whose centralizer $I_x$ has dimension $r$. It was shown in \cite[Lemma 10]{Kos} that the image of the Kostant section is contained in the open subset $\bbg^\reg$. Kostant proves that if $x,x'\in\bbg^\reg(\bar k)$ have the same characteristic polynomial $\chi(x)=\chi(x')$, then they are conjugate \cf \cite[Th.~2]{Kos}. Combining this with the existence of the section, he obtains the following statement.

\begin{lemme}\label{Kostant reg}
The restriction of $\chi$ to $\bbg^\reg$ is a smooth morphism. Its geometric fibers are homogeneous spaces under the action of $G$.
\end{lemme}


\subsection{Outer Twisting}\label{Torsion exterieure}

For arithmetic applications, it is necessary to consider the quasi-split forms of the group $\GG$. Fix an pinning $(\bbT,\bbB,\bbx_+)$ of $\GG$, as in \ref{Section de Kostant}. Let $\Out(\GG)$ denote the group of automorphisms of $\GG$ that fix this pinning. This is a discrete group which may possibly be infinite. It acts on the set of roots $\Phi$, leaving the subset of simple roots stable. It also acts on the Weyl group $\bbW$ in a compatible way-that is, there is an action of the semidirect product $\bbW\rtimes \Out(\GG)$ on $\bbT$ and on the set $\Phi$ of roots.

\begin{definition}
A quasi-split form of $\GG$ over a $k$-scheme $X$ is the data of an $\Out(\GG)$-torsor $\rho_G$ over $X$ (with respect to the \'{e}tale topology).
\end{definition}

\begin{numero}
The datum of $\rho_G$ allows one to twist $\GG$ to obtain a smooth reductive $X$-group $G=\rho_G\wedge^{\Out(\GG)} \GG$ which is equipped with a pinning defined over $X$, that is, a triple $(T,B,\bfx_+)$, where $B$ is a closed smooth subgroup scheme of $G$ over $X$, $T$ is a subtorus of $B$, and ${\bf x_+}$ is a global section of $\Lie(B)$ such that, fiber by fiber, $(T,B,\bfx_+)$ is isomorphic to the pinning $(\bbT,\bbB,\bbx_+)$. Conversely, any smooth reductive $X$-group equipped with a pinning $(T,B,\bfx_+)$ and locally isomorphic to $\GG$ for the \'{e}tale topology defines a torsor under the group $\Out(\GG)$. We will abuse language by saying that $G$ is a quasi-split form, omitting mention of the attached pinning.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
Let $\rho_G$ be an $\Out(\GG)$-torsor over $X$, and let $G$ be the corresponding quasi-split form with pinning $(T,B,\bfx_+)$. The structures discussed in the previous two paragraphs carry over to the quasi-split form. Let $\frakg=\Lie(G)$ and $\frakt=\Lie(T)$. The action of $\bbW\rtimes\Out(\GG)$ on $\bbt$ induces an action of $\Out(\GG)$ on $\bbc=\bbt/\bbW$. Thus, we define the space of characteristic polynomials of $\frakg=\Lie(G)$ as the $X$-scheme
$$\frakc=\rho_G\wedge^{\Out(\GG)} \bbc.$$
It is equipped with a morphism
$$\chi:\frakg\rightarrow \frakc$$
which is deduced from the Chevalley morphism $\chi:\bbg\rightarrow\bbc$. Since $\Out(\GG)$ fixes the $\mathfrak{sl}_2$-triple $(h,\bbx_+,\bbx_-)$, the Kostant section $\epsilon:\bbc\rightarrow \bbg$ is $\Out(\GG)$-equivariant. By twisting, we obtain an $X$-morphism
\begin{equation}\label{section de Kostant tordue}
\epsilon:\frakc\rightarrow \frakg
\end{equation}
which is a section of the Chevalley morphism $\chi:\frakg\rightarrow \frakc$, and which we will also call the Kostant section. Moreover, there is a finite flat morphism
$$\pi:\frakt\rightarrow \frakc$$
obtained from $\pi:\bbt\rightarrow\bbc$. The finite \'{e}tale $X$-group scheme
$$W=\rho_G \wedge^{\Out(\GG)}\bbW$$
acts on $\frakt$. Since $\bbc$ is the invariant quotient of $\bbt$ by $\bbW$, $\frakc$ is also the invariant quotient of $\frakt$ by $W$. Over the open subset
$$\frakc^\rs=\rho_G\wedge^{\Out(\GG)} \bbc,$$
the morphism $\pi:\frakt^\rs\rightarrow \frakc^\rs$ is a $W$-torsor under the finite \'{e}tale group scheme.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}\label{reduction}\label{diagramme reduction}\label{pi_rho}
To study endoscopic groups, it is necessary to consider reductions of the $\Out(\GG)$-torsor $\rho_G$. A reduction of $\rho_G$ is a torsor $\rho:X_\rho\rightarrow X$ under a discrete group $\Theta_\rho$, together with a homomorphism ${\bf o}_\GG:\Theta_\rho\rightarrow \Out(\GG)$ such that
$$\rho\wedge^{\Theta_\rho}\Out(\GG)=\rho_G.$$
Then one has $\frakt=X_\rho\wedge^{\Theta_\rho} \bbt$ and $\frakc=X_\rho\wedge^{\Theta_\rho} \bbc$, and a cartesian diagram
$$\xymatrix{
 X_\rho \times\bbt \ar[d]_{} \ar[r]^{\pi}    & X_\rho \times \bbc\ar[d]^{}  \\
 \frakt \ar[r]_{\pi} & \frakc           }$$
in which both vertical arrows are $\Theta_\rho$-torsors. The semidirect product $\bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho$ acts on $X_\rho\times \bbt$ and acts freely on the open subset $X_\rho\times \bbt^\rs$. The diagonal arrow in the above diagram,
$$\pi_\rho:X_\rho\times \bbt \rightarrow \frakc,$$
is then a finite flat $\bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho$-invariant morphism that realizes $\frakc$ as the invariant quotient of $X_\rho\times \bbt$ by $\bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho$. Over the open subset $\frakc^\rs$, $\pi_\rho$ is a finite \'{e}tale Galois morphism with Galois group $\bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho$. It is often convenient in calculations to replace the $W$-torsor $\frakt^\rs\rightarrow \frakc^\rs$ by the torsor $X_\rho\times \bbt^\rs\rightarrow \frakc^\rs$ under the constant group $\bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho$.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}\label{deploiement}
% [journal 1.3.5] RESTORED: the standing hypothesis on X, required for the
% fundamental-group description, was dropped.
In what follows we shall assume that $X$ is connected and normal. It is often convenient to pass from the language of torsors to the more concrete language of representations of the profinite fundamental group. Let $x$ be a geometric point of $X$ and $\pi_1(X,x)$ the pointed fundamental group of $X$. Let $x_{\rm Out}$ be a geometric point of $\rho_G$ above $x$. The choice of this point defines a homomorphism
$$\rho_G^\bullet:\pi_1(X,x)\rightarrow \Out(\GG).$$
A pointed reduction $\rho\wedge^{\Theta_\rho}\Out(\GG)=\rho_G$ (see \ref{reduction}) is given by a geometric point $x_\rho$ of $X_\rho$ lying above $x_{\rm Out}$. This is equivalent to giving a homomorphism $\rho^\bullet:\pi_1(X,x)\rightarrow \Theta_\rho$ in the commutative diagram
$$\xymatrix{
 \pi_1(X,x)\ar[dr]_{\rho_G^\bullet} \ar[r]^{\rho^\bullet}   & \Theta_\rho \ar[d]^{{\bf o}_G}  \\
 &           \Out(\GG)}$$
where ${\bf o}_\GG:\Theta_\rho\rightarrow \Out(\GG)$ is the reduction homomorphism.
\end{numero}

\subsection{Regular Semisimple Centralizer}\label{subsection : Centralisateur rs}

Let $\frakg^\rs$ denote the inverse image of the open subset $\frakc^\rs$ under the morphism $\chi:\frakg\rightarrow\frakc$. For any $a\in\frakc^\rs(\bar k)$, it is well known that the fiber $\chi^{-1}(a)$ consists of a single orbit under the adjoint action of $G$. For any $\gamma\in \frakg^\rs(\bar k)$, the centralizer $I_\gamma$ of $\gamma$ is a maximal torus of $G\otimes_k \bar k$.

\begin{numero}
Let $a\in \frakc^\rs(\bar k)$ and let $\gamma,\gamma'\in \chi^{-1}(a)$. Then there exists an element $g\in G(\bar k)$ which transports $\gamma$ to $\gamma'$, i.e. such that $\ad(g)\gamma=\gamma'$. The inner automorphism $\ad(g)$ then defines an isomorphism $\ad(g):I_\gamma \isom I_{\gamma'}$. Moreover, if $g$ and $g'$ are two elements of $G(\bar k)$ transporting $\gamma$ to $\gamma'$, then $g$ and $g'$ differ by an element of $I_\gamma$. Since $I_\gamma$ is a torus, hence commutative, the two isomorphisms
$$\ad(g)\quad \text{and}\quad \ad(g'):I_\gamma \isom I_{\gamma'}$$
are identical. This shows that the tori $I_\gamma$ and $I_{\gamma'}$ are canonically isomorphic. This therefore defines a torus that depends only on $a$ and is canonically isomorphic to $I_\gamma$ for every $\gamma\in\chi^{-1}(a)$. This torus can be described directly in terms of $a$ with arbitrary coefficients as follows.

Let $S$ be an $X$-scheme and $a\in\frakc^\rs(S)$ an $S$-point of $\frakc^\rs$. We call the {\em cameral cover} associated to $a$ the $W$-torsor $\pi_a:\tilde S_a \rightarrow S$ obtained by forming the cartesian diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
 \tilde S_a \ar[d]_{\pi_a} \ar[r]   & \frakt^\rs \ar[d]^{\pi}   \\
  S \ar[r]_{a} & \frakc^\rs           }
$$
Set
\begin{equation}\label{pi a J a}
J_a=\pi_a\wedge^{W} T.
\end{equation}
% [journal 1.4] MISTRANSLATION. French: "Le lemme SUIVANT est un cas particulier d'un
% resultat de Donagi et Gaitsgory QUI ETAIT probablement bien connu." Two errors: it is
% the FOLLOWING lemma (the sentence points forward), and "probably well known" qualifies
% the result of Donagi and Gaitsgory, not the fact that this is a particular case.
The following lemma is a particular case of a result of Donagi and Gaitsgory that was probably well known. We shall recall the general statement in paragraph \ref{desciption galoisienne de J}.
\end{numero}

\begin{lemma}\label{J a}
Let $S$ be an $X$-scheme and $a\in\frakc^\rs(S)$ an $S$-point of $\frakc^\rs$. Let $x$ be an $S$-point of $\frakg^\rs(S)$ such that $\chi(x)=a$, and let $I_x$ be the pullback of the centralizer over $\frakg$. Then there is a canonical isomorphism $J_a=I_x$.
\end{lemma}

% [journal 1.4.4 - 1.4.5] SUPERSEDED. The arXiv version gave a variant in terms of a
% reduction rho of the Out(G)-torsor and a (W x| Theta_rho)-torsor. The journal replaces
% it by a POINTED variant, twisting the constant torus by the pi_1(S,s)-action. The old
% text is kept alongside at ch1/OLD_1_4_4_theta_rho.tex.
\begin{numero}
Let us consider a pointed variant of the construction above. Choose a geometric point $s$ of $S$ lying over $x$, and $\tilde s$ of $\tilde S_a$ lying over $s$. As in \ref{deploiement}, we have a commutative diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
 & \bbW\rtimes \Out(\GG) \ar[d] \\
\pi_1(S,s) \ar[ru]^{\pi_a^\bullet} \ar[r] & \Out(\GG)
}
$$
The torus $J_a$ is then obtained by twisting the constant torus $\bbT$ by the action of $\pi_1(S,s)$ on $\bbT$ deduced from the homomorphism
$$\pi_a^\bullet:\pi_1(S,s)\rightarrow \bbW\rtimes\Out(\GG),$$
that is to say
\begin{equation}\label{pi a J a 2}
J_a=S_\bullet\wedge^{\pi_1(S,s),\pi_a^\bullet}\bbT,
\end{equation}
where $(S_\bullet,s_\bullet)$ is the universal pro-finite \'etale Galois cover of $(S,s)$.
\end{numero}

% [journal 1.5] the French title is "Classes de conjugaison dans une classe stable";
% inserting "Stable" made the title name the wrong objects.
\subsection{Conjugacy Classes within a Stable Class}

In this paragraph, $G$ will be a quasi-split form of $\GG$ over a field $F$ containing $k$. By a stable semisimple regular conjugacy class in $\frakg$ over $F$, we mean an element $a\in\frakc^\rs(F)$. Langlands's original definition of stable conjugacy classes is more complicated, but once restricted to semisimple regular elements in the Lie algebra, it coincides with ours. Since we restrict ourselves to stable semisimple regular classes, henceforth by a stable conjugacy class we mean semisimple regular, unless explicitly mentioned otherwise.

\begin{numero}
Let $a\in\frakc^\rs(F)$ be a stable conjugacy class. Let $\gamma_0=\epsilon(a)\in\frakg(F)$ be the image of $a$ under the Kostant section. The centralizer $I_{\gamma_0}$ of $\gamma_0$ is a torus defined over $F$, canonically isomorphic to the torus $J_a$ defined in the previous paragraph (see \ref{J a}). Let $\gamma$ be another $F$-point in $\chi^{-1}(a)$. Since, as elements of $\frakg(\overline F)$, $\gamma_0$ and $\gamma$ are conjugate (i.e. there exists $g\in G(\overline F)$ such that $\gamma=\ad(g) \gamma_0$), it follows that for every $\sigma\in \Gal(\overline F/F)$, one has $g^{-1}\sigma(g)\in I_{\gamma_0}(\overline F)$. The map $\sigma\mapsto g^{-1}\sigma(g)$ then defines an element
$$\inv(\gamma_0,\gamma)\in \rmH^1(F,I_{\gamma_0})$$
which depends only on the $G(F)$-conjugacy class of $\gamma$ and not on the choice of the transporting element $g$. Its image in $\rmH^1(F,G)$ is trivial by construction. According to Langlands, the map $\gamma\mapsto \inv(\gamma_0,\gamma)$ establishes a bijection between the set of $G(F)$-conjugacy classes in the set of $F$-points of $\chi^{-1}(a)$ and the fiber of the map
$$\rmH^1(F,I_{\gamma_0})\rta \rmH^1(F,G)$$
over the neutral element (see \cite{Langlands}). This fiber will be denoted
$${\rm ker}(\rmH^1(F,I_{\gamma_0})\rta \rmH^1(F,G)).$$
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
Instead of $\frakg(F)$, it is often necessary to consider the groupoid $[\frakg/G](F)$ of pairs $(E,\phi)$ consisting of a $G$-torsor $E$ over $F$ and an $F$-point $\phi$ of $\ad(E)=E\wedge^G \frakg$. The Chevalley morphism defines a functor $[\chi]$ from $[\frakg/G](F)$ to the set $\frakc(F)$. Let $a\in\frakc^\rs(F)$. Consider the groupoid of $F$-points of $[\chi]^{-1}(a)$. The objects of $[\chi]^{-1}(a)(F)$ are locally isomorphic for the \'{e}tale topology. Moreover, there is a base point $(E_0,\gamma_0)$ of the groupoid, where $E_0$ is the trivial $G$-torsor and $\gamma_0\in \frakg(F)$ is the element $\gamma_0=\epsilon(a)$ defined by the Kostant section. For any $F$-point $(E,\phi)$ of $[\chi]^{-1}(a)$, one obtains an invariant
$$\inv((E_0,\gamma_0),(E,\phi))\in \rmH^1(F,I_{\gamma_0}).$$
The map $(E,\phi)\mapsto \inv((E_0,\gamma_0),(E,\phi))$ defines a bijection from the set of isomorphism classes of $[\chi]^{-1}(a)(F)$ onto $\rmH^1(F,I_{\gamma_0})$.

For a given $F$-point $(E,\phi)$ of $[\chi]^{-1}(a)$ and its invariant $\inv((E_0,\gamma_0),(E,\phi))$, the isomorphism class of $E$ corresponds to the image of this invariant in $\rmH^1(F,G)$ under the map
$$\rmH^1(F,I_{\gamma_0})\rightarrow \rmH^1(F,G).$$
Thus, we recover the bijection mentioned above between the $G(F)$-conjugacy classes in $\chi^{-1}(a)(F)$ and the subset of $\rmH^1(F,I_{\gamma_0})$ consisting of the elements whose image in $\rmH^1(F,G)$ is trivial.
\end{numero}

\subsection{The Tate-Nakayama Duality}\label{subsection : Tate Nakayama}

The discussion in the previous paragraph takes a very explicit form in the case of a non-archimedean local field thanks to the Tate-Nakayama duality. Let $F_v$ be a non-archimedean local field, $\calO_v$ its ring of integers, and $v$ the valuation. Let $F_v^{\rm sep}$ be a separable closure of $F_v$. Denote by $\Gamma_v$ the Galois group $\Gal(F_v^{\rm sep}/F_v)$. Write $X=\Spec(F_v)$ and let $x$ be the chosen geometric point.

\begin{numero}
Let $G$ be the quasi-split form over $F_v$ of $\GG$ associated to a homomorphism $\rho_G^\bullet:\Gamma_v\rightarrow \Out(\GG)$. Let $\hat \bbG$ be the dual complex of $\bbG$. By definition, it is equipped with a pinning $(\hat\bbT,\hat\bbB,\hat\bbx)$ whose associated root datum is obtained from that of $\GG$ by interchanging roots and coroots. In particular, $\Out(\GG)=\Out(\hat \GG)$. Thus, we have an action $\rho_G^\bullet$ of $\Gamma_v$ on $\hat \GG$ that fixes the pinning.

% [journal 1.6.1] Kottwitz's assertions (finiteness, natural abelian structure) and the
% word 'Pontryagin' were dropped, leaving the starred notation unexplained.
According to Kottwitz (see \cite{K-CTT} and \cite{K-EST}), $\rmH^1(F,G)$ is finite and carries a natural abelian group structure. Moreover, its Pontryagin dual is given by
$$\rmH^1(F,G)^*=\pi_0((Z_{\hat\GG})^{\rho_G^\bullet(\Gamma_v)})$$
where $(Z_{\hat\GG})^{\rho_G^\bullet(\Gamma_v)}$ is the subgroup of fixed points in the center ${Z_{\hat \GG}}$ of $\hat \GG$ under the action of $\rho_G^\bullet(\Gamma_v)$. In particular, $\rmH^1(F_v,G)^*$ is a finitely generated abelian group.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}\label{x_rho a}
Let us now assume the situation of \ref{deploiement}. In particular, we have a finite \'{e}tale Galois cover $\rho:X_\rho\rightarrow X$ with Galois group $\Theta_\rho$, and a point $x_\rho\in X_\rho$ above $x$. For every $a\in\frakc^\rs(F_v)$, we have a $\bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho$-torsor $\pi_{\rho,a}:\widetilde X_{\rho,a} \rightarrow X$ coming from \ref{pi_rho}. Choose a point $x_{\rho,a}$ of $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}$ above $x_\rho$.

We then have a homomorphism $\pi_{\rho,a}^\bullet: \Gamma_v\rightarrow \bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho$ making the diagram
% [journal 1.6] the journal does not number this display; numbering it shifted (1.6.3) to (1.6.4). Unnumbered.
$$\xymatrix{
 \Gamma_v \ar[dr]_{\rho_G^\bullet} \ar[r]^{\pi_{\rho,a}^\bullet\,\,\,\,}   & \bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho\ar[d]^{}  \\
 & \Theta_\rho          }$$
commute.

Let $\gamma_0=\epsilon(a)$ be the element obtained by applying the Kostant section to $a$. According to Lemma \ref{J a}, we have
$$I_{\gamma_0}=J_a=\pi_{\rho,a}\wedge^{\bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho}\bbT.$$ By the local Tate-Nakayama duality (see \cite[1.1]{K-EST}), one then obtains
\begin{equation}\label{T-N}
\rmH^1(F_v,J_a)^*=\pi_0(\hat \bbT^{\pi_{\rho,a}^\bullet(\Gamma_v)}).
\end{equation}
In other words, the group of complex characters of $\rmH^1(F_v,J_a)$ coincides with the group of connected components of the fixed point group $\hat\bbT^{\pi_{\rho,a}^\bullet(\Gamma_v)}$. Here $\hat \bbT$ denotes the complex dual torus of $\bbT$, defined by interchanging the character group with the cocharacter group.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
The inclusion $\iota:\hat \bbT \hookrightarrow \hat \bbG$ is $\Gamma$-equivariant up to conjugation; that is, for any $t\in \hat\bbT$ and any $\sigma\in \Gamma_v$, the elements $\rho^\bullet(\sigma)(\iota(t))$ and $\iota(\pi_a^\bullet(\sigma)(t))$ are conjugate in $\hat\GG$. This yields the inclusion
$$
(Z_{\hat \GG})^{\rho_G^\bullet(\Gamma_v)}\subset \hat \bbT^{\pi_a^\bullet(\Gamma_v)}
$$
which induces a homomorphism between the groups of connected components
$$
\pi_0((Z_{\hat \GG})^{\rho_G^\bullet(\Gamma_v)})\rightarrow \pi_0(\hat \bbT^{\pi_a^\bullet(\Gamma_v)}).
$$
By duality, one recovers the map $\rmH^1(F_v,I_{\gamma_0})\rta \rmH^1(F_v,G)$ defined in the previous paragraph.
\end{numero}

\subsection{$\kappa$-Orbital Integrals}\label{subsection : integrales orbitales}

Retain the notations of the previous paragraph. In addition, assume that $G$ is given as a quasi-split form of $\GG$ over $\calO_v$. The locally compact group $G(F_v)$ is then equipped with a maximal compact open subgroup $G(\calO_v)$. Let ${\rm d}g_v$ denote the Haar measure on $G(F_v)$ normalized so that $G(\calO_v)$ has volume one.

For $a\in \frakc^\rs(F_v)$, fix a Haar measure ${\rm d}t_v$ on the torus $J_a(F_v)$. For any $\gamma\in \frakg(F_v)$ with $\chi(\gamma)=a$, the canonical isomorphism $J_a=I_\gamma$ allows one to transport the Haar measure ${\rm d}t_v$ from $J_a(F_v)$ to a Haar measure on $I_\gamma(F_v)$.

For any such $\gamma$, and for any locally constant compactly supported function $f$ on $\frakg(F_v)$, one may then define the orbital integral
$$
{\bf O}_\gamma(f,{\rm d}t_v)=\int_{I_\gamma(F_v) \backslash G(F_v)}
f(\ad(g_v)^{-1}\gamma) \frac{{\rm d}g_v}{ {\rm d}t_v}.
$$

\begin{definition}\label{kappa integrale}
Let $\kappa$ be an element of $\hat \bbT^{\pi_a^\bullet(\Gamma_v)}$. For any locally constant compactly supported function $f$ on $\frakg(F_v)$, we define the $\kappa$-orbital integral of $f$ on the stable conjugacy class $a\in\frakc(F_v)$ by the formula
$$
{\bf O}_a^\kappa(f,{\rm d}t_v)=\sum_{\gamma} \langle
\inv(\gamma_0,\gamma), \kappa \rangle\, {\bf O}_\gamma(f,{\rm d}t_v)
$$
where $\gamma$ runs over the set of $G(F_v)$-conjugacy classes within the stable class of characteristic $a$, where the base point $\gamma_0=\epsilon(a)$ is defined by the Kostant section, and where ${\rm d}t_v$ is a Haar measure on the torus $J_a(F_v)$.
\end{definition}

Note for future reference that the definition of the $\kappa$-orbital integral depends on the choice of a geometric point $x_{\rho,a}$ in $\tilde X_{\rho,a}$; without such a point one cannot relate the cohomology group $\rmH^1(F_v,J_a)$ with the dual torus $\hat\bbT$, and therefore express the Tate-Nakayama duality in the form \ref{T-N}.

\subsection{Endoscopic Groups}\label{subsection : Groupes endoscopiques}

By construction, the dual group $\hat\bbG$ is equipped with a pinning $(\hat\bbT,\hat\bbB,\hat\bbx_+)$. Let $\kappa$ be an element of the maximal torus $\hat\bbT$ in this pinning. The neutral component of the centralizer of $\kappa$ in $\hat\GG$ is a reductive subgroup, which we denote by $\hat\bbH$. The pinning of $\hat\bbG$ induces on $\hat\bbH$ a pinning with the same maximal torus $\hat\bbT$. Let $\bbH$ be the split reductive group over $k$ equipped with a pinning whose dual complex is $\hat\bbH$ (with its pinning). Then one has $\Out(\bbH)=\Out(\hat\bbH)$.

Consider the centralizer $(\hat\GG\rtimes \Out(\GG))_\kappa$ of $\kappa$ in the semidirect product $\hat\GG\rtimes \Out(\GG)$. There is an exact sequence
$$1\rightarrow \hat\bbH \rightarrow
(\hat\GG\rtimes \Out(\GG))_\kappa \rightarrow
\pi_0(\kappa)\rightarrow 1,$$
where $\pi_0(\kappa)$ is the group of connected components of $(\hat\GG\rtimes \Out(\GG))_\kappa$. One then has canonical homomorphisms
$$
\xymatrix{
 & \pi_0(\kappa)\ar[dl]_{{\bf o}_\bbH(\kappa)} \ar[dr]^{{\bf
o}_\bbG(\kappa)}   &   \\
 \Out(\bbH) & & \Out(\bbG)           }
$$

\begin{definition}\label{donnee endoscopique}
Let $G$ be a quasi-split form of $\GG$ over $X$, given by an $\Out(\GG)$-torsor $\rho_G$. An endoscopic datum for $G$ over $X$ is a pair $(\kappa,\rho_\kappa)$ with $\kappa$ as above and where $\rho_\kappa$ is a $\pi_0(\kappa)$-torsor which induces $\rho_G$ via the change of structure groups ${\bf o}_\bbG(\kappa)$.

The endoscopic group associated with the endoscopic datum $(\kappa,\rho_\kappa)$ is the quasi-split form $H$ over $X$ of $\bbH$ given by the $\Out(\bbH)$-torsor $\rho_H$ obtained from $\rho_\kappa$ by the change of structure group ${\bf o}_\bbH(\kappa)$.
\end{definition}

There is a pointed variant of the notion of endoscopic datum that is useful. Let $X$ be a scheme with a geometric point $x$. Let $G$ be a connected reductive $X$-group, a quasi-split form of the constant group $\GG$, defined by a homomorphism
$$\rho_G^\bullet:\pi_1(X,x)\rightarrow\Out(\GG).$$

\begin{definition}\label{donnee endoscopique pointee}
A pointed endoscopic datum for $G$ over $X$ is a pair $(\kappa,\rho_\kappa^\bullet)$ where $\kappa\in \hat\bbT$ and
$$\rho_\kappa^\bullet:\pi_1(X,x)\rightarrow\pi_0(\kappa)$$
is a homomorphism lying over $\rho_G^\bullet$.
\end{definition}

Given a pointed endoscopic datum, one can form a commutative diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
& \pi_1(X,x) \ar[ddl]_{\rho_H^\bullet} \ar[ddr]^{\rho_G^\bullet} \ar[d]_{\rho_\kappa^\bullet} &\\
 & \pi_0(\kappa)\ar[dl]^{{\bf o}_\bbH(\kappa)} \ar[dr]_{{\bf
o}_\bbG(\kappa)}   &   \\
 \Out(\bbH) & & \Out(\bbG)       }
$$
The endoscopic group $H$ associated with the pointed endoscopic datum $(\kappa,\rho_\kappa^{\bullet})$ can then be formed using the homomorphism $\rho_H^\bullet$.

\subsection{Transfer of Stable Conjugacy Classes}\label{subsection : Transfert des classes}

Let $(\kappa,\rho_\kappa)$ be an endoscopic datum as in \ref{donnee endoscopique}, and let $H$ be the associated endoscopic group. We now define the transfer of stable conjugacy classes from $H$ to $G$ by constructing a morphism $\nu:\frakc_H\rightarrow \frakc$.

By construction, one has a simultaneous reduction of the torsors $\rho_G$ and $\rho_H$ to the torsor
$$\rho_\kappa:X_{\rho_\kappa}\rightarrow X$$
under the group $\pi_0(\kappa)$. Then one may realize $\frakc$ as the invariant quotient of $X_{\rho_\kappa}\times \bbt$ under the action of $\bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$ (see \ref{pi_rho}). Similarly, one may realize $\frakc_H$ as the invariant quotient of $X_{\rho_\kappa}\times \bbt$ under the action of $\bbW_\HH\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$. To define the morphism $\frakc_H\rightarrow\frakc$, it suffices to define a homomorphism
$$\bbW_\bbH\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)\rightarrow \bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$$
compatible with the actions on $X_{\rho_\kappa}\times \bbt$, which leads us to the following lemma.

\begin{lemma}\label{homomorphisme produit semi-direct}
Let $\bbW_\HH\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$ be the semidirect product defined by ${\bf o}_\HH(\kappa):\pi_0(\kappa)\rightarrow \Out(\HH)$. Let $\bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$ be the semidirect product defined by ${\bf o}_\GG(\kappa):\pi_0(\kappa)\rightarrow \Out(\GG)$. There exists a canonical homomorphism
$$\bbW_\HH\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa) \rightarrow \bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$$
which induces the obvious homomorphism on the normal subgroups $\bbW_\HH \subset \bbW$, induces the identity on the quotient $\pi_0(\kappa)$, and is compatible with the actions of $\bbW_\HH\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$ and $\bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$ on $\bbt$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Recall that the centralizer $(\bbW\rtimes \Out(\GG))_\kappa$ of $\kappa$ in $\bbW\rtimes\Out(\GG)$ is canonically isomorphic to the semidirect product $\bbW_\HH \rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$ (see \cite[Lemma 10.1]{N}). One deduces from this an homomorphism $\theta:\pi_0(\kappa) \rightarrow \bbW\rtimes\Out(\GG)$ such that one has a group homomorphism
$$\bbW_\HH \rtimes \pi_0(\kappa) \rightarrow \bbW\rtimes^{\theta} \pi_0(\kappa),$$
where the latter semidirect product is formed using the action of $\pi_0(\kappa)$ on $\bbW$ defined by the homomorphism $\theta:\pi_0(\kappa)\rightarrow \bbW\rtimes \Out(\GG)$ and the natural action of $\bbW\rtimes \Out(\GG)$ on $\bbW$. This homomorphism is visibly compatible with the actions on $\bbt$.

It remains to construct an isomorphism between the semidirect products
$$\bbW\rtimes^{\theta} \pi_0(\kappa) \rightarrow \bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa),$$
where the latter is formed using the homomorphism ${\bf o}_\GG(\kappa):\pi_0(\kappa)\rightarrow \Out(\GG)$. For every $\alpha\in \pi_0(\kappa)$, the element $\theta(\alpha)\in \bbW\rtimes\Out(\GG)$ can be written uniquely as $\theta(\alpha)=w(\alpha){\bf o}_\GG(\alpha)$ with $w(\alpha)\in\bbW$. This allows us to define a homomorphism
$$\pi_0(\kappa)\rightarrow \bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$$
by the formula $\alpha\mapsto w(\alpha) \alpha$. This homomorphism induces an isomorphism $\bbW\rtimes^{\theta} \pi_0(\kappa) \rightarrow \bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$ which makes the diagram
$$ \xymatrix{
\bbW\rtimes^{\theta} \pi_0(\kappa) \ar[dr]_{} \ar[rr]^{} &  &  \bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa) \ar[dl]^{}  \\
 &\bbW\rtimes \Out(\GG) &           }
$$
commute. In particular, this isomorphism is compatible with the actions on $\bbt$.
\end{proof}

Over the regular semisimple open $\frakc^\rs$ of $\frakc$, there is a finite \'{e}tale morphism
$$\nu^\rs:\frakc_H^{G-\rs}\rightarrow \frakc^\rs$$
where we denote by $\frakc_H^{G-\rs}$ the inverse image of $\frakc^\rs$ in $\frakc_H$. This morphism realizes the transfer of stable conjugacy classes of $H$ that are semisimple and $G$-regular to the stable conjugacy classes of $G$ that are semisimple regular.

\begin{lemma}\label{J a J a_H}
Let $a_H\in \frakc_H^{G-\rs}(S)$ be an $S$-point for a scheme $S$, and let $a\in\frakc^\rs(S)$ be its image. Then there is a canonical isomorphism between the torus $J_a$ defined by formula \ref{pi a J a} and the torus $J_{H,a_H}$ defined by the same formula applied to $H$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Taking the inverse image of \ref{pi_rho} by $a$, one obtains a $\bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$-torsor $\pi_{\rho_\kappa,a}$ over $S$. Similarly, one has a $\bbW_H\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$-torsor $\pi_{\rho_\kappa,a_H}$ over $S$. By the very construction of the morphism $\nu:\frakc_H\rightarrow \frakc$, there is a morphism
$$\pi_{\rho_\kappa,a_H} \rightarrow \pi_{\rho_\kappa,a}$$
compatible with the actions of $\bbW_H\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$ and $\bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$. Consequently, the second torsor is obtained from the first by the change of structure groups $\bbW_H\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa) \rightarrow \bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$ (see \ref{homomorphisme produit semi-direct}). It now suffices to apply formula \ref{pi a J a 2}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remarque}\label{x_a dans pi_{a_H}}
Now, let $S=\Spec(F_v)$ where $F_v$ is a local field as in \ref{subsection : Tate Nakayama}. By choosing an $F_v^{\rm sep}$-point $x_{\rho_\kappa,a}$ in the torsor $\pi_{\rho_\kappa,a_H}$, one obtains a homomorphism $\pi_{\rho_\kappa,a_H}^\bullet:\Gamma_v \rightarrow \bbW_H\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$. Then, by Tate-Nakayama duality \ref{T-N},
$$\rmH^1(F_v,J_a)^*=\rmH^1(F_v,J_{H,a_H})^*=
\hat \bbT^{\pi_{\rho_\kappa,a_H}^\bullet(\Gamma_v)}.
$$
By construction, $\kappa\in \hat\bbT^{\bbW_H\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)}$, so that one may define the $\kappa$-orbital integral ${\bf O}_a^\kappa(f,{\rm d}t_v)$ as in \ref{kappa integrale}.
\end{remarque}

\subsection{Discriminant and Resultant}

Let $\Phi$ be the root system associated with the split group $\GG$. For each root $\alpha\in \Phi$, let ${\rm d}\alpha\in k[\bbt]$ denote the derivative of the character $\alpha:\bbT\rightarrow\GG_m$. Form the discriminant
$$\underline\discrim_\GG=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi} {\rm d}\alpha \in k[\bbt],$$
which is clearly an invariant element under $\bbW$ in this polynomial algebra. It thus defines a function on the space of characteristic polynomials $\bbc=\Spec(k[\bbt]^\bbW)$. Let $\discrim_\GG$ be the divisor on $\bbc$ defined by this function. We recall the well-known statement.

\begin{lemma}\label{diviseur discriminant}
The divisor $\discrim_\GG$ is a reduced divisor on $\bbc$ which is stable under the action of $\Out(\GG)$. The open complement of this divisor is the regular semisimple open $\bbc^\rs$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The pullback of $\discrim_\GG$ is the divisor of the hyperplanes in $\bbt$ defined by the roots, each hyperplane occurring with multiplicity $2$ since $\alpha$ and $-\alpha$ define the same hyperplane. The group $\bbW$ acts freely on the complement of this divisor in $\bbt$, so that the morphism $\pi_\bbt:\bbt\rightarrow\bbc$ is \'{e}tale over this open set and clearly ramified along this divisor. Moreover, $\Out(\GG)$ acts on the set of roots, hence it leaves this divisor stable.

It remains to prove that $\discrim_\GG$ is a reduced divisor. Since it is a complete intersection, it suffices to show that a dense open subset of $\discrim_\GG$ is reduced. % [journal 1.10] the journal removes from the divisor the IMAGE of those points of t.
One may therefore remove from $\discrim_\GG$ the image of the points of $\bbt$ belonging to more than two root hyperplanes. One then reduces to the case of a group of semisimple rank one, where the assertion can be verified by hand.
\end{proof}

Let $X$ be a $k$-scheme and let $G$ be a quasi-split form of $\GG$ over $X$ given by an $\Out(\GG)$-torsor $\rho_G$. By twisting $\discrim_\GG$ by $\rho_G$, one obtains a reduced divisor $\discrim_G$ on $\frakc$ whose open complement is $\frakc^\rs$. Let $(\kappa,\rho_\kappa)$ be an endoscopic datum (see \ref{donnee endoscopique}) for $G$. One has a divisor $\discrim_\bbH$ on $\bbc_\HH$ and by twisting, a divisor $\discrim_H$ on $\frakc_H$.

% [journal 1.10.2 - 1.10.3] RESTORED. The translation stated a different lemma (existence
% and uniqueness of the divisor) and WEAKENED the conclusion: the journal proves that
% prod d.alpha is W_H-INVARIANT, i.e. that the sign is +1, whereas the translation asserted
% only that it "transforms by a sign". The Coxeter-length proof was absent, as was the
% author's attribution of the statement to two of the anonymous referees. Old text kept at
% ch1_OLD_1_10_2.tex.
The following statement was pointed out to me by two of the anonymous referees.

\begin{lemma}\label{produit dalpha invariant}
Choose a subset $\Psi\subset\Phi-\Phi_\bbH$ such that for every pair of opposite roots $\pm\alpha\in\Phi-\Phi_\bbH$ the cardinality of $\{\pm\alpha\}\cap\Psi$ equals one. Then the function $\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi}{\rm d}\alpha\in k[\bbt]$ is invariant under the action of $\bbW_\bbH$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
An element $w\in\bbW_\bbH$ acts on $\Phi-\Phi_\bbH$ by sending a pair of opposite roots $\{\pm\alpha\}\subset\Phi-\Phi_\bbH$ to a generally different pair of opposite roots in the same set. It follows that there is a sign $\epsilon(w)$ such that $w(\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi}{\rm d}\alpha)=\epsilon(w)\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi}{\rm d}\alpha$, and that this sign does not depend on the choice of the set $\Psi$. It remains to show that this sign equals one.

Choose a subset $\Psi_\bbG\subset\Phi$ such that for every pair of opposite roots $\pm\alpha\in\Phi$ the cardinality of $\{\pm\alpha\}\cap\Psi_\bbG$ equals one, and set $\Psi_\bbH=\Psi_\bbG\cap\Phi_\bbH$. We may also assume that $\Psi_\bbG=\Psi_\bbH\cup\Psi$. For every $w\in\bbW$ there is a sign $\epsilon_\bbG(w)$ such that $w(\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi_\bbG}{\rm d}\alpha)=\epsilon_\bbG(w)\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi_\bbG}{\rm d}\alpha$, and this sign does not depend on the choice of $\Psi_\bbG$. Taking for $\Psi_\bbG$ the set of positive roots, one sees that $\epsilon_\bbG(w)=(-1)^{\ell_\bbG(w)}$, where $\ell_\bbG(w)$ is the usual length of $w$ in the Coxeter group $\bbW_\bbG$. If $w\in\bbW_\bbH$, one likewise has $w(\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi_\bbH}{\rm d}\alpha)=\epsilon_\bbH(w)\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi_\bbH}{\rm d}\alpha$ with $\epsilon_\bbH(w)=(-1)^{\ell_\bbH(w)}$.

To show that $\epsilon(w)=1$, it remains to prove the equality
$$(-1)^{\ell_\bbG(w)}=(-1)^{\ell_\bbH(w)}.$$
Note that both these signs may be interpreted as the determinant of the reflection representation. The equality of signs follows from the fact that the reflection representation of $\bbW_\bbH$ is obtained from that of $\bbW_\bbG$ by restriction.
\end{proof}

% [journal 1.10.3] the journal DEFINES R^G_H by the invariant function of the lemma above.
Let $\resultant_\bbH^\bbG$ be the effective divisor of $\bbc_\HH$ defined by the invariant function $\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi}{\rm d}\alpha\in k[\bbt]^{\bbW_\bbH}$ of the preceding lemma. One then has the equality of divisors
$$\nu^*\discrim_\bbG=\discrim_\bbH+2\resultant_\bbH^\bbG.$$

\begin{numero}\label{discrimnant resultant}
By twisting $\resultant_\bbH^\bbG$ by $\rho_{\kappa}$, one obtains an effective divisor $\resultant_H^G$ on $\frakc_H$ satisfying the relation
$$
\nu^* \discrim_G=\discrim_H+2 \resultant_H^G.
$$
\end{numero}

\subsection{The Fundamental Lemma for Lie Algebras}

We are now in a position to state the fundamental lemma for Lie algebras, conjectured by Langlands and Shelstad and by Waldspurger.

Let $F_v$ be a non-archimedean local field, $\calO_v$ its ring of integers, and let $v:F_v^\times\rightarrow \ZZ$ be the discrete valuation. Let $\FF_q$ denote the residue field of $\calO_v$. Let $X_v=\Spec(\calO_v)$. Let $F_v^{\rm sep}$ be a separable closure of $F_v$. This defines a geometric point $x$ of $X_v$.

Let $G$ be a quasi-split form of $\GG$ over $\calO_v$ defined by a homomorphism $\rho_G^\bullet:\pi_1(X_v,x)\rightarrow \Out(\GG)$. Consider a pointed endoscopic datum
$(\kappa,\rho_\kappa^\bullet)$ consisting of an element $\kappa\in\hat\bbT$ and a homomorphism $\rho_\kappa^\bullet:\pi_1(X_v,x)\rightarrow \pi_0(\kappa)$ lying over $\rho_G^\bullet$ (see \ref{donnee endoscopique pointee}). Then there is a reductive group scheme $H$ over $X_v$ and a morphism of $X_v$-schemes $\frakc_H\rightarrow \frakc$.

Let $a_H\in\frakc_H(\calO_v)$ with image $a\in\frakc(\calO_v)\cap \frakc^\rs(F_v)$. Choose a Haar measure ${\rm d}t_v$ on the torus $J_a(F_v)$. By Lemma \ref{J a J a_H}, we have an isomorphism between the tori $J_a$ and $J_{H,a_H}$, so that one may transport the Haar measure ${\rm d}t_v$ to $J_{H,a_H}(F_v)$.

By choosing an $F_v^{\rm sep}$-point $x_a$ as in \ref{x_a dans pi_{a_H}}, one can define the $\kappa$-orbital integral ${\bf O}_a^\kappa$ of any locally constant compactly supported function on $\frakg(F_v)$. Let $1_{\frakg_v}$ be the characteristic function of $\frakg(\calO_v)$ in $\frakg(F_v)$ and $1_{\frakh_v}$ the characteristic function of $\frakh(\calO_v)$ in $\frakh(F_v)$.

\begin{theoreme}\label{LS}
With the above notations, one has the equality
$$
{\bf O}_a^\kappa(1_{\frakg_v},{\rm d}t_v)=q^{r_{H,v}^G(a_H)}\,{\bf SO}_{a_H}(1_{\frakh_v},{\rm d}t_v)
$$
where $r_{H,v}^G(a_H)=\deg_v(a_H^* \resultant_H^G)$.
\end{theoreme}

This theorem is the Lie algebra variant of the original conjecture of Langlands and Shelstad, which concerns Lie groups over a non-archimedean local field. This variant was formulated by Waldspurger, who showed that it implies the original conjecture for groups. He also proved that the case where $F$ is a local field of positive characteristic not dividing the order of $\bbW$ implies the case where $F$ is a local field of characteristic zero with residue characteristic not dividing the order of $\bbW$ (see \cite{W}). We restrict ourselves here to the first case.

The original Langlands-Shelstad statement is somewhat more complicated, notably due to the presence of a sign in the transfer factor. In \cite{K-FdT}, Kottwitz established the link between the Langlands-Shelstad transfer factor and the Kostant section, which allows us to state the Langlands-Shelstad conjecture in this simpler form. In the case of classical groups, Waldspurger gave a more explicit form of this conjecture in \cite{W-pav}. Hales also wrote an expository paper (see \cite{Hales}) on the statement of the Langlands-Shelstad conjecture.

\begin{numero}
Note that the statement above extends trivially to the case where one starts with an element $a_H\in \frakc^{G-\rs}(F_v)$ that is not in $\frakc_H(\calO_v)$. In this case, we also have $a\notin\frakc(\calO_v)$. This follows from the valuative criterion of properness applied to the finite morphism $\nu_H:\frakc_H\rightarrow\frakc$. In such cases, it is evident that the orbital integrals ${\bf O}_a^\kappa(1_{\frakg_v},{\rm d}t_v)$ and ${\bf SO}_{a_H}(1_{\frakh_v},{\rm d}t_v)$ vanish.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
Finally, note that the equality of divisors
$$a_*\discrim_G=a_H^* \discrim_H+ 2 a_H^* \resultant_G^H$$
which follows from \ref{discrimnant resultant} implies
$$\Delta_H(a_H) \Delta_G(a)^{-1}= q^{\deg_v(a_H^* \resultant_G^H)}$$
with $\Delta_H(a_H)=q^{-{\rm deg}(a_H^* \discrim_H)/ 2}$ and
$\Delta_G(a)=q^{-{\rm deg}(a^* \discrim_G)/ 2}$. This allows one to rewrite formula \ref{LS} in the more usual form
$$\Delta_G(a){\bf O}_a^\kappa(1_{\frakg_v},{\rm d}t_v)=\Delta_H(a_H){\bf SO}_{a_H}(1_{\frakh_v},{\rm d}t_v).
$$
\end{numero}

\subsection{The Nonstandard Fundamental Lemma}

In \cite{W-tordu}, Waldspurger formulated a variant of the Langlands-Shelstad conjecture which he calls the nonstandard fundamental lemma. In this paragraph, we recall this conjecture.

Let $\bbG_1$ and $\bbG_2$ be two split reductive groups over $k$ with pinnings. For each $i\in\{1,2\}$, we have in particular a maximal torus $\bbT_i$ of $\bbG_i$, the set of roots $\Phi_i\subset \bbX^*(\bbT_i)$, the set of simple roots $\Delta_i\subset \Phi_i$, and the set of coroots $\Phi_i^\vee\subset \bbX_*(\bbT_i)$. The quintuple
$$(\bbX^*(\bbT_i),\bbX_*(\bbT_i),\Phi_i,\Phi_i^\vee,\Delta_i)$$
is the root datum associated with the pinned group $\bbG_i$, which determines it up to unique isomorphism.

\begin{definition}\label{isogenie donnees radicielles}
An isogeny of root data between $\bbG_1$ and $\bbG_2$ consists of a pair of isomorphisms of $\QQ$-vector spaces
$$\psi^*:\bbX^*(\bbT_2)\otimes\QQ \longrightarrow \bbX^*(\bbT_1)\otimes\QQ$$
and
$$
\psi_*:\bbX_*(\bbT_1)\otimes\QQ \longrightarrow
\bbX_*(\bbT_2)\otimes\QQ,
$$
which are dual to each other and such that $\psi^*$ sends bijectively the $\QQ$-lines of the form $\QQ \alpha_2$, for $\alpha_2\in \Phi_2$, onto the $\QQ$-lines of the form $\QQ \alpha_1$, for $\alpha_1\in \Phi_1$, sending the lines of the simple roots onto the lines of the simple roots, and similarly for $\psi_*$ with the $\QQ$-lines generated by the coroots.
\end{definition}

\begin{exemple}
Two semisimple groups having the same adjoint group have isogenous root data. Indeed, in this case there is a canonical isomorphism $\bbX^*(\bbT_1)\otimes \QQ\rightarrow \bbX^*(\bbT_2)\otimes\QQ$ which respects the set of roots and the set of simple roots, and whose dual isomorphism respects the coroots.
\end{exemple}

\begin{exemple}
The most interesting example is that of two reductive groups dual to each other in the sense of Langlands. By definition, the root datum of the dual group $\hat\bbG$ is obtained from that of $\bbG$ by interchanging the character group with the cocharacter group, and the set of roots with the set of coroots. Consider the direct sum decomposition of $\QQ$-vector spaces
\begin{eqnarray*}
\bbX^*(\bbT_i)\otimes\QQ&=&\QQ\Phi \oplus \bbX^*(Z_\GG)\otimes\QQ,\\
\bbX_*(\bbT_i)\otimes\QQ&=&\QQ\Phi^\vee \oplus \bbX_*(Z_\GG)\otimes\QQ,
\end{eqnarray*}
where $\QQ\Phi$ is the subspace of $\bbX^*(\bbT_i)\otimes\QQ$ spanned by the roots and $\bbX^*(Z_\GG)$ is the character group of the center of $\GG$. The linear map $\alpha\mapsto \alpha^\vee$ induces an isomorphism of vector spaces $\QQ\Phi\rightarrow \QQ\Phi^\vee$ that sends the lines of the form $\QQ \beta$ for a certain root $\beta$ onto the lines of the form $\QQ {\beta'}^\vee$ for a certain coroot $\beta'$. The dual linear map has the same property. The interesting cases are those of $B_n\leftrightarrow C_n$, $F_4$, and $G_2$, where there exist roots of different lengths. For a more detailed discussion, see \cite[page 14]{W-tordu}.
\end{exemple}

\begin{numero}\label{apparies}
Since the reflection associated to a root $\alpha$ depends only on the $\QQ$-line spanned by $\alpha$, the isomorphisms $\psi^*$ and $\psi_*$ induce an isomorphism between the Weyl groups $\bbW_1 \isom \bbW_2$ of the two reductive groups $\bbG_1$ and $\bbG_2$ that are paired.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
Let $\bbG_1$ and $\bbG_2$ be two split groups whose root data are isogenous. Let $\Out_{12}$ denote the group of automorphisms of $\bbX_*(\bbT_1)\otimes\QQ$ that stabilize $\Phi_1$, $\Delta_1$ but also $\bbX^*(\bbT_2)$, $\Phi_2$, $\Delta_2$ (viewed as subsets of $\bbX_*(\bbT_1)\otimes\QQ$), and similarly for the dual automorphisms of $\bbX_*(T_1)\otimes\QQ$. For any $k$-scheme $X$, for any $\Out_{12}$-torsor $\rho_{12}$, one can twist $\bbG_1$ and $\bbG_2$, with their pinnings, by $\rho_{12}$ to obtain quasi-split forms $G_1$ and $G_2$. The pairs of quasi-split forms obtained by this procedure are called {\em paired}.
\end{numero}

Since we have the isomorphism $\psi^*$ between the $\QQ$-vector spaces
$$\bbX^*(\bbT_1)\otimes\QQ\simeq\bbX^*(\bbT_2)\otimes\QQ,$$
one may compare the positions of the two lattices $\bbX^*(\bbT_1)$ and
$\bbX^*(\bbT_2)$ viewed as lattices in a common $\QQ$-vector space. A prime number $p$ is said to be {\em good} relative to $\psi^*$ if $p$ does not divide the integers
$$|\bbX^*(\bbT_1)/(\bbX^*(\bbT_1) \cap \bbX^*(\bbT_2))|
\quad \text{and} \quad |\bbX^*(\bbT_2)/(\bbX^*(\bbT_2) \cap \bbX^*(\bbT_1))|.
$$
If $k$ is a field of characteristic good relative to $\psi^*$, then $\psi^*$ induces an isomorphism $\bbX^*(\bbT_1)\otimes k \isom \bbX^*(\bbT_2)\otimes k$.

\begin{lemma}\label{c_G_1=c_G_2}
Let $G_1$ and $G_2$ be paired groups over a base $X$ with good residual characteristics. Let $T_1$ and $T_2$ be the maximal tori of the pinnings of $G_1$ and $G_2$, and let $\frakt_1, \frakt_2$ be their Lie algebras. Then there exists a canonical isomorphism $\frakt_1\rightarrow \frakt_2$ and a compatible isomorphism
$$\nu:\frakc_{G_1}\isom \frakc_{G_2}.$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We have
$$\bbt_i=\Spec({\rm Sym}_{\calO_X}(\bbX^*(\bbT_i)\otimes \calO_X)),$$
where ${\rm Sym}_{\calO_X}(\bbX^*(\bbT_i)\otimes \calO_X)$ is the symmetric $\calO_X$-algebra associated with the free $\calO_X$-module $\bbX^*(\bbT_i)\otimes \calO_X$. If the residue characteristics of $X$ are good, $\psi^*$ induces an isomorphism of free $\calO_X$-modules
$$\bbX^*(\bbT_2)\otimes \calO_X \longrightarrow \bbX^*(\bbT_1)\otimes \calO_X,$$
and thus an isomorphism $\bbt_1 \isom \bbt_2$. We already saw that $(\psi^*,\psi_*)$ induces an isomorphism $\bbW_1\isom \bbW_2$ which is clearly compatible with their actions on $\bbt_1\isom \bbt_2$. This yields an isomorphism between
$$\bbc_1=\Spec({\rm Sym}_{\calO_X}(\bbX^*(\bbT_i)\otimes \calO_X))^{\bbW_{1}}$$
and
 $$\bbc_2=\Spec({\rm Sym}_{\calO_X}(\bbX^*(\bbT_i)\otimes
\calO_X))^{\bbW_{2}}.$$
By applying the outer twisting by $\rho_{12}$, one obtains the isomorphism $\frakc_1 =\frakc_2$, as desired.
\end{proof}

Now assume the situation of the previous lemma. Let $X$ be the disk $\Spec(\calO_v)$ where $\calO_v=k[[\epsilon_v]]$ with $k$ a finite field of characteristic good relative to $\psi^*$. Let $a_1\in \frakc_{G_1}(\calO_v)$ and $a_2\in\frakc_{G_2}(\calO_v)$ be such that $\nu(a_1)=a_2$. The isogeny $\bbT_1\rightarrow \bbT_2$ given by $\psi^*$ induces, by twisting, an isogeny of the tori $J_{a_1}\rta J_{a_2}$ by Lemma \ref{J a}. Moreover, the isogeny induces an isomorphism between the Lie algebras of these tori under the assumption that the characteristic is good relative to $\psi$. One can thus transport Haar measures between $J_{a_1}(F_v)$ and $J_{a_2}(F_v)$ (and vice versa). We will denote by the same symbol ${\rm d}t_v$ these compatible Haar measures.

% [journal 1.12.7] the hypothesis on the residual characteristic was dropped entirely,
% turning a conditional statement into an unconditional one.
\begin{theoreme}\label{Waldspurger}
Suppose that the residual characteristic is greater than twice the Coxeter number of $G_1$ and of $G_2$. Then we have the following equality between stable orbital integrals:
$$
{\bf SO}_{a_1}(1_{G_1},{\rm d}t_v)= {\bf SO}_{a_2}(1_{G_2},{\rm d}t_v),
$$
where $1_{G_i}$ is the characteristic function of the compact subset $\frakg_i(\calO_v)$ in $\frakg_i(F_v)$.
\end{theoreme}

This equality was conjectured by Waldspurger, who calls it the nonstandard fundamental lemma. In \cite{W-tordu}, he showed that the combination of the standard fundamental lemma \ref{LS} and the nonstandard lemma above implies the twisted fundamental lemma.

\subsection{Global Stabilization Formula}\label{Formule globale de stabilisation}

We now return to the Langlands-Shelstad conjecture. The fundamental lemma consists of an identity of local orbital integrals. However, it is necessary to place it in its original global context, namely the stabilization of the trace formula. We now review the structure of the anisotropic part over a global field of positive characteristic. This review will later serve as a guide to study the structure of the cohomology of the Hitchin fibration.

Let $k=\FF_q$ and let $F$ be the field of rational functions on a smooth, projective, geometrically connected curve $X$ over $k$. For every closed point $v\in |X|$, denote by $F_v$ the completion of $F$ with respect to the valuation defined by $v$, and by $\calO_v$ its ring of integers. For simplicity, in this discussion we assume that $G$ is a split semisimple group.

For each class $\xi\in\rmH^1(F,G)$, denote by $G^\xi$ the inner form of $G$ defined by the image of $\xi$ in $\rmH^1(F,G^{\rm ad})$. We will call such a form $G^\xi$ a strongly inner form. Instead of considering the trace formula for $G$, one considers the sum of the trace formulas over the strongly inner forms that are locally trivial. The stabilization of the sum becomes simpler and, moreover, admits a direct geometric interpretation. This stabilization process, due to Langlands and Kottwitz (see \cite{Langlands} and \cite{K-EST}), is presented here following the exposition in \cite{N-ICM}.

Consider the sum
\begin{equation}\label{1}
\sum_{\xi\in {\rm ker}^1(F,G)} \sum_{\gamma\in {\mathfrak g}^{\xi,\ani}(F)/{\sim}} \bfO_\gamma(1_D),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{itemize}
    \item ${\rm ker}^1(F,G)$ is the set of isomorphism classes of $G$-torsors over $F$ whose image in $\rmH^1(F_v,G)$ is trivial for all $v\in|X|$.
    \item ${\mathfrak g}^\xi$ is the form of ${\mathfrak g}$ over $F$ defined by $\xi$.
    \item $\gamma$ runs over the set of conjugacy classes of semisimple regular elements in ${\mathfrak g}^\xi(F)$ whose centralizer in $\frakg^\xi(F\otimes_{k} \bar k)$ is an anisotropic torus.
    \item $\bfO_\gamma(1_D)$ is the global orbital integral
    $$
    \bfO_\gamma(1_D)=\int_{G_\gamma^\xi(F)\backslash G({\mathbb A})}
    1_{D}({\rm ad}(g)^{-1}\gamma) dg,
    $$
    of the function
    $$1_D=\bigotimes_{v\in |X|} 1_{D_v}:\frakg({\mathbb A})\longrightarrow \CC,$$
    where $1_{D_v}$ is the characteristic function of the compact open subset $\varepsilon^{-d_v}{\mathfrak g}({\mathcal O}_v)$ of ${\mathfrak g}(F_v)$ (the integers $d_v$ being even and zero except for finitely many places). The integral converges for the anisotropic classes $\gamma$.
    \item $dg$ is the Haar measure on $G({\mathbb A})$ normalized so that $G({\mathcal O}_{{\mathbb A}})$ has volume one.
\end{itemize}

Consider the Chevalley characteristic morphism $\chi:\frakg\rightarrow \frakc$, as well as its twisted forms
$$\chi^\xi :\frakg^\xi \longrightarrow \frakc$$
for $\xi\in\rmH^1(F,G)$. Note that the group of automorphisms of $G$ acts on $\frakc$ via the outer automorphism group, so that twisting by $\xi$ does not affect $\frakc$. Thus every conjugacy class $\gamma$ in $\frakg^\xi(F)$ defines an element $a\in\frakc(F)$. Since the centralizer of a semisimple regular $\gamma$ depends only on $a$, there exists a subset $\frakc^\ani(F)$ of $\frakc(F)$ consisting of those elements $a$ arising from semisimple regular anisotropic classes in $\frakg^{\xi}(F\otimes_{k} \bar k)$. The sum (\ref{1}) can be rewritten as a sum over $a\in\frakc^\ani(F)$:
\begin{equation}\label{2}
\sum_{a\in\frakc^\ani(F)}
\sum_{\xi\in {\rm ker}^1(F,G)}
\sum_{ {\gamma\in {\mathfrak g}^\xi(F)/{\scriptscriptstyle \sim} },\, {\chi(\gamma)=a}   }
\bfO_\gamma(1_D).
\end{equation}

For each element $a\in\frakc^\ani(F)$, the Kostant section (see \ref{Kostant}) produces an element $\gamma_0=\epsilon(a)\in {\mathfrak g}(F)$ with $\chi(\gamma_0)=a$. Denote by $J_a$ the centralizer $I_{\gamma_0}$ of $\gamma_0$. Since we have restricted to the semisimple regular anisotropic part, $J_a$ is an anisotropic torus. The dual torus $\hat J_a$, defined over $\Ql$, is equipped with a finite action of $\Gamma={\rm Gal}(\overline F/F)$ so that the group $\hat J_a^\Gamma$ of fixed points is finite.

For every $\xi\in {\rm ker}^1(F,G)$, the conjugacy classes $\gamma\in {\mathfrak g}^\xi(F)$ with $\chi(\gamma)=a$ are in bijection with the cohomology classes
$$\alpha={\rm inv}(\gamma_0,\gamma)\in {\rm H}^1(F,J_a)$$
whose image in ${\rm H}^1(F,G)$ is the element $\xi$. Thus the set of pairs $(\xi,\gamma)$ in the sum (\ref{2}), with $\xi\in {\rm ker}^1(F,G)$ and $\gamma$ a conjugacy class in ${\mathfrak g}^\xi(F)$ mapping to $a\in \frakc^{\rm ani}(F)$, is in bijection with
$${\rm ker}[{\rm H}^1(F,J_a)\rightarrow \bigoplus_{v\in |X|}
{\rm H}^1(F_v,G)].$$

In order for a collection of conjugacy classes
$(\gamma_v)_{v\in|X|}$ of ${\mathfrak g}(F_v)$ with $\chi(\gamma_v)=a$ to come from a pair $(\xi,\gamma)$ as in (\ref{2}), it is necessary and sufficient that $\gamma_v=\gamma_0$ for almost all $v$, and that
\begin{equation}\label{condition}
\sum_{v\in |X|}\alpha_v\big|_{\hat I_a^\Gamma}=0,
\end{equation}
where $\alpha_v={\rm inv}_v(\gamma_0,\gamma_v)$ (see \cite{K-CTT}). If this holds, the number of pairs $(\xi,\gamma)$ mapping to the collection $(\gamma_v)_{v\in|X|}$ equals the cardinality of the group
$${\rm ker}^1(F,J_a)={\rm ker}[{\rm H}^1(F,J_a)\rightarrow
\bigoplus_{v\in |X|} {\rm H}^1(F_v,J_a)].$$

We now bring in the local orbital integrals. To define these, one needs a Haar measure on the centralizer. For each $a$, choose an invariant volume form on the generic fiber of $J_a$. Such a form exists and is unique up to a scalar. It induces at each place $v$ a Haar measure ${\rm d}t_v$ on $J_a(F_v)$ whose tensor product $\bigotimes_{v\in|X|} {\rm d} t_v$ is independent of the choice of the volume form. This is the Tamagawa measure.
