Thus, the sum (\ref{2}) can be rewritten as
\begin{equation}
\sum_{a\in\frakc^\ani(F)}\left|{\rm ker}^1(F,J_a)\right|\,
\tau(J_a) \sum_{(\gamma_v)_{v\in|X|}} \prod_v
\bfO_{\gamma_v}(1_{D_v},{\rm d}t_v),
\end{equation}
where the $\gamma_v$ are conjugacy classes in ${\mathfrak g}(F_v)$ satisfying condition (\ref{condition}) and
$$\tau(J_a)={\rm vol}(J_a(F)\backslash J_a(\bbA), \bigotimes_{v\in|X|} {\rm d}t_v)$$
is a Tamagawa number. By factoring out $\tau(J_a)$, we obtain a sum of products of local orbital integrals $\prod_v \bfO_{\gamma_v}(1_{D_v},{\rm d}t_v)$ instead of the global orbital integrals $\bfO_\gamma(1_D)$.

% [journal 1.13.4] the journal numbers this display; leaving it unnumbered shifted
% every subsequent equation number in 1.13 down by one relative to the journal.
Using Ono's formula (see \cite{O})
\begin{equation}\label{Ono}
\left|{\rm ker}^1(F,J_a)\right|\, \tau(J_a)
=\left|\pi_0(\hat J_a^\Gamma)\right|,
\end{equation}
the sum (\ref{2}) becomes
\begin{equation}
\sum_{a\in\frakc^\ani(F)}\left|\pi_0(\hat J_a^\Gamma)\right|
\sum_{(\gamma_v)_{v\in|X|}} \prod_v \bfO_{\gamma_v}(1_{D_v},{\rm d}t_v),
\end{equation}
where the $(\gamma_v)$ satisfy condition (\ref{condition}). Note that under the assumption that $J_a$ is anisotropic, the group $\hat J_a^\Gamma$ is finite so that $\pi_0(\hat J_a^\Gamma)=\hat J_a^\Gamma$.

Using Fourier transform on the finite group $\hat J_a^\Gamma$, the sum (\ref{2}) becomes
\begin{equation}\label{kappa}
\sum_{a\in\frakc^\ani(F)} \sum_{\kappa \in \hat J_a^\Gamma}
\bfO^\kappa_a\Bigl(1_D,\bigotimes_{v\in|X|}{\rm d}t_v\Bigr)
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
{\bf O}^\kappa_a\Bigl(1_D,\bigotimes_{v\in|X|}{\rm d}t_v\Bigr)=\prod_{v\in|X|}
\sum_{\substack{\gamma_v\in {\mathfrak g}(F_v)/{\sim}\\ \chi(\gamma_v)=a}}
\langle {\rm inv}_v(\gamma_0,\gamma_v),\kappa\rangle\, {\bf O}_{\gamma_v}(1_{D_v},{\rm d}t_v).
\end{equation}

The next step is to exchange the sums over $a$ and over $\kappa$. By choosing an embedding of $\hat J_a$ into $\hat G$, the element $\kappa$ defines a semisimple conjugacy class $[\kappa]$. The intersection $\hat J_a^{\Gamma}\cap [\kappa]$ of $\hat J_a^{\Gamma}$ with the conjugacy class $[\kappa]$ does not depend on the choice of embedding of $\hat J_a$ into $\hat G$. The sum (\ref{kappa}) then becomes
\begin{equation}\label{[kappa]}
\sum_{[\kappa]\in \hat G/\sim} \sum_{a\in \frakc^{\rm ani}(F)}
\sum_{\kappa\in \hat J_a^\Gamma \cap [\kappa]} {\bf O}^\kappa_a\Bigl(1_D,\bigotimes_{v\in|X|}{\rm d}t_v\Bigr).
\end{equation}

Recall that we assumed $G$ is a semisimple adjoint group. For each conjugacy class $[\kappa]$, choose a representative $\kappa\in\hat G$. Since $\hat G$ is semisimple and simply connected, the centralizer $\hat G_\kappa$ is a connected reductive group. Let $\hat H=\hat G_\kappa$ and let $H$ be the reductive group dual to $\hat H$. Since we are only interested in the anisotropic part, we discard all $H$ which are not semisimple. Suppose then that $H$ is semisimple and consider the morphism
$$\nu_H:\frakc_H^\ani(F)\longrightarrow \frakc^\ani(F).$$
An element $a$ lies in the image of $\nu_{H}$ if and only if $\hat J_a^\Gamma \cap [\kappa]$ is nonempty. In general, there is a canonical bijection between the set $\hat J_a^\Gamma \cap [\kappa]$ and the set of $a_H\in\frakc_H^\ani(F)$ lying over $a$.

Assuming the fundamental lemma, the sum (\ref{2}) becomes
\begin{equation}\label{5}
\sum_{H} \sum_{a_H\in \frakc_H^{\rm ani}(F)} {\bf SO}_a\Bigl(1_D,\bigotimes_{v\in|X|}{\rm d}t_v\Bigr),
\end{equation}
where the first sum runs over the set of equivalence classes of elliptic endoscopic groups of $G$.

As we noted in \cite[1]{N}, counting the $k$-points (with $k$ finite) of the moduli space of Higgs bundles essentially yields the expression (\ref{2}). We have even proposed a geometric interpretation of the stabilization process (\ref{2}) = (\ref{kappa}) as a decomposition of the cohomology of the Hitchin fibration according to the action of its natural symmetries. This is a decomposition into a direct sum of a pure complex over the base of the Hitchin fibration.

The equality (\ref{2}) = (\ref{5}) may then be interpreted as an equality in a Grothendieck group between two pure complexes. Theorem \ref{stabilisation sur tilde A} is a precise variant of this interpretation from which we will deduce the Langlands-Shelstad fundamental lemma \ref{LS}.

\section{Regular Centralizer and Kostant Section}
\label{section : centralisateur regulier}

In this chapter we recall the construction of the regular centralizer and the morphism from the regular centralizer to the centralizer of \cite{N}. We also recall the Galois description of the regular centralizer due to Donagi and Gaitsgory \cite{DG}.

We retain the notations from \ref{Torsion exterieure}. In particular, $\GG$ is a split reductive group over a field $k$ and $G$ is a quasi-split form of $\GG$ over a $k$--scheme $X$. We assume that the characteristic of $k$ does not divide the order of $\bbW$.

\subsection{Regular Centralizer}\label{centralisateur regulier}

Let $I$ be the group scheme of centralizers over $\mathfrak{g}$. The fiber of $I$ over a point $x\in\mathfrak{g}$ is the subgroup of $G$ which centralizes $x$, i.e.,
$$
I_x=\{g\in G \mid \ad(g)x=x\}.
$$
In general, the dimension of $I_x$ depends on $x$, so that $I$ is not flat over $\mathfrak{g}$. However, the restriction $I^\reg$ of $I$ to the open subset $\mathfrak{g}^\reg$ is a smooth group scheme of relative dimension $r$. Since its generic fiber is a torus, it is a smooth commutative group scheme.

The following lemma \cite[3.2]{N} is the starting point for our study of the Hitchin fibration. For the reader's convenience, we briefly recall its proof.

\begin{lemma} \label{J}
There exists a unique smooth commutative group scheme $J$ over $\mathfrak{c}$ endowed with a $G$--equivariant isomorphism
$$
(\chi^* J)|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg} \cong I|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}.
$$
Moreover, this isomorphism extends to a homomorphism $\chi^* J \to I$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $x_1,x_2\in\mathfrak{g}^\reg(\bar{k})$ be such that $\chi(x_1)=\chi(x_2)=a$. Denote by $I_{x_1}$ and $I_{x_2}$ the fibers of $I$ at $x_1$ and $x_2$, respectively. There exists $g\in G(\bar{k})$ such that $\ad(g)x_1=x_2$. Conjugation by $g$ induces an isomorphism $I_{x_1}\to I_{x_2}$, and since $I_{x_1}$ is commutative, this isomorphism is independent of the choice of $g$. This allows one to define the fiber $J_a$ of $J$ at $a$.

The definition of $J$ as an affine group scheme over $\mathfrak{c}$ is obtained by faithfully flat descent. Denote by $I_1^\reg$ and $I_2^\reg$ the pullbacks of $I^\reg=I|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}$ to $\mathfrak{g}^\reg\times_\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{g}^\reg$ via the first and second projections, respectively. The descent datum for $I^\reg$ along the morphism $\chi^\reg:\mathfrak{g}^\reg\to\mathfrak{c}$ consists of an isomorphism $\sigma_{12}:I_2^\reg\to I_1^\reg$ satisfying a cocycle condition. We now construct $\sigma_{12}$, leaving the verification of the cocycle condition to the reader.

The construction of $\sigma_{12}$ also proceeds by descent. Consider the morphism
$$
G\times \mathfrak{g}^\reg \to \mathfrak{g}^\reg\times_\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{g}^\reg,\quad (g,x)\mapsto (x,\ad(g)x).
$$
This morphism is smooth and surjective (hence faithfully flat). Over $G\times \mathfrak{g}^\reg$, there is a canonical isomorphism between $I_1^\reg$ and $I_2^\reg$ provided by the $G$--equivariance of $I$. To descend this isomorphism to $\mathfrak{g}^\reg\times_\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{g}^\reg$, one must verify an identity over the fiber product of $G\times \mathfrak{g}^\reg$ with itself over $\mathfrak{g}^\reg\times_\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{g}^\reg$. After identifying this fiber product with $G\times I_1^\reg$, the required identity follows from the commutativity of the group scheme $I_1^\reg$.

Thus we have constructed a smooth commutative group scheme $J$ over $\mathfrak{c}$ equipped with a $G$--equivariant isomorphism
$$
(\chi^*J)|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}\to I|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}.
$$
This isomorphism extends to a homomorphism of group schemes $\chi^* J\to I$ because $\chi^*J$ is a smooth $k$--scheme, $I$ is an affine $k$--scheme, and the complement $\chi^*J-\chi^*J|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}$ has codimension three in $\chi^*J$.
\end{proof}

We call $J$ the \emph{regular centralizer}. In fact, one may take as a definition $J:=\epsilon^*I$, where $\epsilon$ is the Kostant section from \ref{Section de Kostant}. Note that $J$ is equipped with a $\GG_m$--equivariant structure for the $\GG_m$--action on $\mathfrak{c}$ defined by the exponents. By descent, one obtains a group scheme over $[\mathfrak{c}/\GG_m]$, which we also denote by $J$ (see \cite[3.3]{N}).

\subsection{On the Quotient $[\mathfrak{g}/G]$}

Since the Chevalley morphism $\chi:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{c}$ is $G$--invariant, it factors through the quotient stack $[\mathfrak{g}/G]$, yielding the morphism
$$
[\chi]:[\mathfrak{g}/G]\to \mathfrak{c}.
$$
Recall that $[\mathfrak{g}/G]$ assigns to every $k$--scheme $S$ the groupoid of pairs $(E,\phi)$ where $E$ is a $G$--torsor on $S$ and $\phi$ is a section of the adjoint bundle $\ad(E)$ associated with the adjoint representation of $G$.

Over $\mathfrak{c}$, we have defined a smooth commutative group scheme $J$. Let $\mathbf{B}J$ denote the classifying stack of $J$, which assigns to every $\mathfrak{c}$--scheme $S$ the Picard groupoid of $J$--torsors on $S$. Lemma~\ref{J} shows that there is an action of $\mathbf{B}J$ on $[\mathfrak{g}/G]$ over $\mathfrak{c}$. Indeed, one may twist a pair $(E,\phi)\in [\mathfrak{g}/G](S)$ by a $J$--torsor using the homomorphism $\chi^*J\to I$ from Lemma~\ref{J}.

\begin{proposition}\label{gerbe}
The morphism
$$
[\chi^\reg]:[\mathfrak{g}^\reg/G]\to \mathfrak{c}
$$
is a gerbe banded by the regular centralizer $J$. Moreover, this gerbe is neutral.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
That $[\chi^\reg]$ is a gerbe follows from the fact that the restriction of the homomorphism $\chi^*J\to I$ to $\mathfrak{g}^\reg$ is a $G$--equivariant isomorphism by the construction of $J$. The Kostant section $\epsilon:\mathfrak{c}\to\mathfrak{g}^\reg$, composed with the quotient map $\mathfrak{g}^\reg\to [\mathfrak{g}^\reg/G]$, neutralizes the gerbe. We denote this section by $[\epsilon]:\mathfrak{c}\to [\mathfrak{g}^\reg/G]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{numero}
It is thus natural to view $[\mathfrak{g}/G]$ as a kind of compactification of the Picard stack $\mathbf{B}J$. This mould can be used to construct more concrete geometric situations such as the affine Springer fiber and the Hitchin fibration by evaluating it on different schemes $S$. For affine Springer fibers, one evaluates it on the ring of formal power series (see \ref{section : Fibres de Springer affines}). For the Hitchin fibration, one evaluates it on a smooth projective curve (see \ref{section : Fibration de Hitchin}). For the latter, one must also take into account the $\GG_m$--action which acts by homothety on $\mathfrak{g}$.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
The regular centralizer $J$ is equipped with a $\GG_m$--action that lifts the $\GG_m$--action on $\mathfrak{c}$. The classifying stack $\mathbf{B}J$ over $[\mathfrak{c}/\GG_m]$ acts on $[\mathfrak{g}/G\times\GG_m]$. This latter stack contains the open substack $[\mathfrak{g}^\reg/G\times\GG_m]$. The morphism
\begin{equation}\label{mu}
[\chi^\reg/\GG_m]:[\mathfrak{g}^\reg/G\times\GG_m]\to [\mathfrak{c}/\GG_m]
\end{equation}
is again a gerbe banded by $J$. In general, this gerbe is not neutral. However, it becomes neutral after extracting a square root of the universal line bundle on $\mathbf{B}\GG_m$. Consider the homomorphism $[2]:\GG_m\to\GG_m$ defined by $t\mapsto t^2$. It induces a morphism $\mathbf{B}[2]:\mathbf{B}\GG_m\to\mathbf{B}\GG_m$, which amounts to taking a square root of the universal line bundle on $\mathbf{B}\GG_m$. We denote by a superscript $[2]$ the base change by this morphism. In particular, we have a morphism
$$
[\chi/\GG_m]^{[2]}:[\mathfrak{g}/G\times\GG_m]^{[2]}\to [\mathfrak{c}/\GG_m]^{[2]}.
$$
Indeed, $[\mathfrak{c}/\GG_m]^{[2]}$ is the quotient of $\mathfrak{c}$ by the $\GG_m$--action defined as the square of the usual action by the exponents, and $[\mathfrak{g}/G\times\GG_m]^{[2]}$ is the quotient of $\mathfrak{g}$ by the adjoint action of $G$ and the square of the homothety. Consider the composition of the two homomorphisms
$$
\GG_m\rightarrow T\times\GG_m\rightarrow G\times\GG_m,
$$
where the first is defined by $t\mapsto (2\rho(t),t)$ with $2\rho$ being the sum of the positive coroots. The Kostant section $\epsilon:\mathfrak{c}\to\mathfrak{g}$ is equivariant with respect to this homomorphism and hence induces a section of $[\chi/\GG_m]^{[2]}$. We now rephrase the above in a more convenient form.
\end{numero}

\begin{lemma}\label{epsilon D'}
Let $S$ be a $k$--scheme equipped with a line bundle $D$ and let 
$$h_D:S\to \mathbf{B}\GG_m$$ 
be the morphism associated to the universal line bundle on $\mathbf{B}\GG_m$. Let 
$$a:S\to [\mathfrak{c}/\GG_m]$$ 
be a morphism over $h_D$. Then the Kostant section together with the choice of a square root $D'$ of $D$ defines a section
$$[\epsilon]^{D'}(a):S\to [\mathfrak{g}^\reg/G\times\GG_m].$$
\end{lemma}

\subsection{The Center of $G$ and the Connected Components of $J$}
The content of this paragraph is well known.

\begin{proposition} \label{centre connexe}
If $G$ is a group with connected center, then the regular centralizer $J$ has connected fibers.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
In the case of a regular nilpotent element, this is a theorem of Springer (see \cite[III, 3.7 and 1.14]{S-S} and \cite[Theorem 4.11]{S}). The general case reduces to the nilpotent case by the Jordan decomposition. Let $x\in \mathfrak{g}(\bar{k})$ be a regular element with Jordan decomposition $x=s+n$, where $s\in\mathfrak{g}(\bar{k})$ is semisimple and $n\in\mathfrak{g}(\bar{k})$ is nilpotent with $[s,n]=0$. According to \cite[3, Lemmas 5 and 8]{Kos}, % [journal 2.2] the journal asserts this for the centralizer of the SEMISIMPLE PART s;
% the next step applies Springer's theorem inside Lie(G_s), which needs G_s reductive.
the centralizer $G_s$ of $s$ is a connected reductive subgroup of $G$, and moreover its center is connected. One then applies Springer's theorem to the regular nilpotent element $n$ in $\Lie(G_s)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{pi 0 ZG}
For every $x\in \mathfrak{g}^\reg(\bar{k})$, the canonical homomorphism
$$
Z_G\to I_x
$$
induces a surjective homomorphism
$$
\pi_0(Z_G)\to \pi_0(I_x).
$$
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $G^\ad$ be the adjoint group of $G$ and $I^\ad_x$ the centralizer of $x$ in $G^\ad$. We have the exact sequence
$$
1\to Z_G\to I_x\to I^\ad_x\to 1.
$$
Since $I^\ad_x$ is connected by the previous proposition, the canonical map $\pi_0(Z_G)\to \pi_0(I_x)$ is surjective.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Galois Description of $J$}
\label{desciption galoisienne de J}

Following Donagi and Gaitsgory \cite{DG}, one can describe the group scheme $J$ over $\mathfrak{c}$ via the finite flat cover $\pi:\mathfrak{t}\to\mathfrak{c}$. Our presentation will be slightly different from theirs.

Consider the Weil restriction of the torus $T\times\mathfrak{t}$ from $\mathfrak{t}$ to $\mathfrak{c}$,
$$\Pi:=\prod_{\mathfrak{t}/\mathfrak{c}} (T\times\mathfrak{t})=\pi_*(T\times\mathfrak{t}).$$
As an abelian group scheme over $\mathfrak{c}$, we have
$$
\Pi(S)=\Hom_\mathfrak{t}(S\times_\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{t},T\times\mathfrak{t})
$$
for every $\mathfrak{c}$--scheme $S$. This functor is representable by a commutative group scheme over $\mathfrak{c}$ because the morphism $\mathfrak{t}\to\mathfrak{c}$ is finite and flat. Since the Weil restriction preserves smoothness, $\Pi$ is a smooth commutative group scheme over $\mathfrak{c}$ of relative dimension $r\cdot\sharp\,\bbW$. Over the open subset $\mathfrak{c}^\rs$, the cover $\mathfrak{t}^\rs\to\mathfrak{c}^\rs$ is finite \'etale so that the restriction of $\Pi$ to this open is a torus.

The finite \'etale group scheme $W$ acts simultaneously on $T$ and on $\mathfrak{t}$. The diagonal action of $W$ on $T\times\mathfrak{t}$ induces an action of $W$ on $\Pi$. The fixed points under $W$ define a closed subfunctor $J^1$ of $\Pi$.

\begin{lemma}\label{J1}
The closed subfunctor $J^1$ of $\Pi$ is a smooth commutative group scheme over $\mathfrak{c}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since the order of $W$ is prime to the characteristic, the $W$--fixed points in the smooth group scheme $\Pi$ form a closed subfunctor $J^1$, which is smooth over $k$. Let $x$ be a geometric point of $\Pi$ fixed by $W$, and let $a$ be its image in $\mathfrak{c}$. Let ${\bf T}_x$ be the tangent space of $\Pi$ at $x$, and ${\bf T}_a$ the tangent space of $\mathfrak{c}$ at $a$. Since $\Pi$ is smooth over $\mathfrak{c}$, the map ${\bf T}_x\to {\bf T}_a$ is surjective. The tangent space ${\bf T}_x J^1$ is the subspace of ${\bf T}_x$ fixed by $W$. Because the characteristic of $k$ does not divide $\sharp\,\bbW$, the restriction of ${\bf T}_x\to {\bf T}_a$ to the $W$--fixed part remains surjective. It follows that $J^1$ is smooth over $\mathfrak{c}$.
\end{proof}

The following proposition is a strengthening of \ref{pi a J a}.

\begin{proposition} \label{J J1}
There exists a canonical homomorphism $J\to J^1$ which is an isomorphism over the open subset $\mathfrak{c}^\rs$ of $\mathfrak{c}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We begin by constructing a homomorphism from $J$ into the Weil restriction $\pi_*(T\times\mathfrak{t})$. By adjunction, this is equivalent to constructing a homomorphism
$$\pi^* J\to T\times\mathfrak{t}$$
of group schemes over $\mathfrak{t}$.

To do so, we require the simultaneous Grothendieck-Springer resolution. Let $\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}$ be the scheme of pairs $(x,gB)$ with $x\in\mathfrak{g}$ and $gB\in G/B$ such that $\ad(g)^{-1}(x)\in\Lie(B)$ (here $B$ denotes the Borel subgroup of the fixed pinning of $G$). Denote by $\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}:\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\to\mathfrak{g}$ the projection on $x$. The projection $\Lie(B)\to\mathfrak{t}$ defines a morphism $\tilde{\chi}:\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\to\mathfrak{t}$ which completes the commutative square
$$
\xymatrix{
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}} \ar[d]_{\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}} \ar[r]^{\tilde{\chi}} & \mathfrak{t} \ar[d]^{\pi} \\
\mathfrak{g} \ar[r]_{\chi} & \mathfrak{c}
}
$$
Moreover, when restricted to the open subset $\mathfrak{g}^\reg$, one obtains a cartesian diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg \ar[d]_{\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg} \ar[r]^{\tilde{\chi}^\reg} & \mathfrak{t} \ar[d]^{\pi} \\
\mathfrak{g}^\reg \ar[r]_{\chi^\reg} & \mathfrak{c}
}
$$
By \ref{J}, we have $(\chi^\reg)^*J= I|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}$, so that to construct a homomorphism of $\mathfrak{t}$--group schemes $\pi^* J\to (T\times\mathfrak{t})$, it suffices to construct a homomorphism of group schemes over $\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg$
$$
(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg)^*(I|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}) \to T\times \tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg,
$$
which is $G$--equivariant. We now state a lemma.

\begin{lemma}
For every $(x,gB)\in \tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg(\bar{k})$, we have $I_x\subset \ad(g)B$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The assertion is clear for regular semisimple elements $x$, since for such $x$ the centralizer $I_x$ is a torus that acts on the fiber $\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}(x)$, which is discrete; hence the torus must act trivially.

Now consider the group scheme $H$ over $\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg$ whose fiber over $(x,gB)$ is the subgroup of $I_x$ consisting of elements $h$ with $h\in \ad(g)B$. By construction, this is a closed subgroup scheme of $(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg)^*I$ which agrees with $(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg)^*I|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}$ over the dense open set of pairs $(x,gB)$ with $x$ regular semisimple. Since $(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg)^*I|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}$ is flat over $\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg$, it follows that this closed subgroup scheme equals $(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg)^*I|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}$.
\end{proof}

Consider the group scheme $\underline{B}$ over $G/B$ whose fiber over $gB$ is the subgroup $\ad(g)B\subset G$. Denote by $\underline{B}|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg}$ its base change to $\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg$. The above lemma shows that over $\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg$, we have a homomorphism
$$
I|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg}\to \underline{B}|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg}.
$$
Moreover, there is a homomorphism from $\underline{B}$ into the $G/B$--torus $T\times G/B$. Composing, we obtain a $G$--equivariant homomorphism
$$
I|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg}\to T\times \tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg,
$$
which is an isomorphism over the regular semisimple locus. Over the regular semisimple locus, the $W$--action on $I|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg}$ transfers to the diagonal action on $T\times\mathfrak{g}^\rs$. By adjunction, we obtain a homomorphism
$$
I|_{\mathfrak{g}^\reg}\to (\pi_{\mathfrak{g}})_*(T\times\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg),
$$
which factors through the fixed-point subfunctor under the diagonal $W$--action on $(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}})_*(T\times\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^\reg)$.

By descent, one obtains a homomorphism $J\to J^1$ which is an isomorphism over $\mathfrak{c}^\rs$.
\end{proof}

The previous proposition admits the following variant. Consider a reduction
$$\rho\wedge^{\Theta_\rho} \Out(\GG)=\rho_G$$
of the torsor $\rho_G$ (see \ref{reduction}). Then one has a finite flat cover
$$\pi_{\rho}:X_\rho\times \bbt\to \mathfrak{c}$$
which is generically \'etale Galois with Galois group $\bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho$
(see \ref{pi_rho}). This gives a slightly different description of $J^1$.

\begin{lemma}\label{J pi_rho_G}
$J^1$ is canonically isomorphic to the subfunctor of fixed points in the Weil restriction
$$\prod_{(X_\rho\times \bbt)/\mathfrak{c}} (\bbT\times X_\rho\times \bbt)$$
for the diagonal action of $\bbW\rtimes \Theta_\rho$ on $\bbT\times X_\rho\times \bbt$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since the assertion is local for the \'etale topology on $X$, we may assume that $\rho$ is trivial. In that case, the assertion is immediate.
\end{proof}

Following \cite{DG}, we now define a subfunctor $J'$ of $J^1$ which we will show coincides with the image of $J\to J^1$. This definition will require some preparations. The following construction is local for the \'etale topology on the base $X$, so we may assume $G$ is split. For every root $\alpha\in\Phi$, let $h_\alpha$ be the hyperplane in $\mathfrak{t}$ which is the kernel of the differential ${\mathrm d}\alpha:\mathfrak{t}\to\GG_a$. Let $s_\alpha\in W$ be the reflection with respect to the hyperplane $h_\alpha$. Let $T^{s_\alpha}$ be the subgroup of $T$ of elements fixed by $s_\alpha$. Then one has the inclusion
$$
\alpha(T^{s_\alpha})\subset \{\pm 1\}.
$$
Let $x$ be a geometric point of $\mathfrak{t}$ such that $s_\alpha(x)=x$ and let $a$ be its image in $\mathfrak{c}$. Since $J^1$ is the $W$--invariant part of the functor $\prod_{\mathfrak{t}/\mathfrak{c}} (T\times\mathfrak{t})$, there is a canonical homomorphism from $J^1_a$ into the fiber $T\times\{x\}$. Its image is contained in $T^{s_\alpha}\times\{x\}$. Composing with the root $\alpha:T\to\GG_m$, one obtains a homomorphism
$$
\alpha_x: J^1_a\to\GG_m
$$
whose image is contained in $\{\pm 1\}$.

Let $J^0$ be the open subgroup scheme of $J^1$ consisting of the neutral components of the fibers. By construction, $J^0_a$ is the neutral component of $J^1_a$, so that $J^0_a$ is contained in the kernel of $\alpha_x$.

\begin{definition}\label{J'}
Define $J'$ to be the subfunctor of $J^1$ as follows. For every $\mathfrak{c}$--scheme $S$, the group $J'(S)$ is the subgroup of $J^1(S)$ consisting of morphisms
$$
f: S\times_\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{t} \to T
$$
such that for every geometric point $x$ of $S\times_\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{t}$ with $s_\alpha(x)=x$ for some root $\alpha$, one has
$$
\alpha(f(x))=1.
$$
We have inclusions of functors $J^0\subset J'\subset J^1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
The functor $J'$ from the category of $\mathfrak{c}$--schemes to the category of abelian groups is representable by an open subgroup scheme of $J^1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
It suffices to show that $J'$ is representable by an open subscheme of $J^1$, since being a subgroup functor, the representing scheme will automatically be a group scheme. Consider the open subset $U$ of $T\times\mathfrak{t}$ defined as the complement of the union, over all roots $\alpha\in\Phi$, of the closed subschemes $\alpha^{-1}(-1)\times h_\alpha$. The Weil restriction $\operatorname{Res}_{\mathfrak{t}/\mathfrak{c}}(U)$ is then an open subscheme of $\operatorname{Res}_{\mathfrak{t}/\mathfrak{c}}(T\times\mathfrak{t})$. Hence,
$$
J' = \operatorname{Res}_{\mathfrak{t}/\mathfrak{c}}(U) \cap J^1,
$$
so that $J'$ is an open subgroup scheme of $J^1$.
\end{proof}

The following proposition is a variant of a theorem of Donagi and Gaitsgory \cite[Theorem 11.6]{DG}. We give here a slightly different proof.

\begin{proposition}\label{J=J'}
The homomorphism $J\to J^1$ of Proposition~\ref{J J1} factors through the open subgroup scheme $J'$ defined in Definition~\ref{J'} and induces an isomorphism $J\to J'$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
To show that $J\to J^1$ factors through $J'$, it suffices to prove that for every $a\in\mathfrak{c}(\bar{k})$, the homomorphism $\pi_0(J_a)\to \pi_0(J_a^1)$ factors through $\pi_0(J'_a)$. It is enough to check, fiberwise, that the homomorphism $\pi_0(J_a)\to \pi_0(J_a^1)$ factors through $\pi_0(J'_a)$. Recall that the homomorphism $Z_G\to J_a$ induces a surjective homomorphism $\pi_0(Z_G)\to \pi_0(J_a)$ (see \ref{pi 0 ZG}). Thus, it suffices to verify that the homomorphism $Z_G\to J^1_a$ factors through $J'_a$. But this is evident since the restriction of any root to $Z_G$ is trivial.

Since $J$ and $J'$ are smooth affine group schemes over $\mathfrak{c}$, to show that the homomorphism $J\to J'$ is an isomorphism, it suffices to do so over an open subset of $\mathfrak{c}$ whose complement has codimension two. The inverse image of $\discrim_G$ in $\mathfrak{t}$ is the union of the hyperplanes $h_\alpha$ for the roots. Let $\discrim_G^{\mathrm{sing}}$ be the closed subset of $\discrim_G$ whose inverse image consists of points belonging to at least two hyperplanes $h_\alpha$. It is clear that $\discrim_G^{\mathrm{sing}}$ is a closed subset of codimension two in $\mathfrak{c}$. It suffices to prove that the isomorphism between $J$ and $J'$ on $\mathfrak{c}-\discrim_G$ extends to an isomorphism on $\mathfrak{c}-\discrim_G^{\mathrm{sing}}$.

Let $a\in (\mathfrak{c}-\discrim_G^{\mathrm{sing}})(\bar{k})$. It suffices to show that the homomorphism $J\to J'$ is an isomorphism in an \'etale neighborhood of $a$. If $a\notin \discrim_G$, there is nothing to prove because we have seen that $J=J'=J^1$ over the open $\mathfrak{c}-\discrim_G$. Now suppose $a\in \discrim_G-\discrim_G^{\mathrm{sing}}$. Then one can find $s\in\mathfrak{t}(\bar{k})$ mapping to $a$ such that $s$ is annihilated by a unique root $\alpha$. Let $T_\alpha$ be the kernel of $\alpha:T\to\GG_m$ and $H_\alpha$ the centralizer of $T_\alpha$. Let $n$ be a regular nilpotent element of the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{h}_\alpha$ of $H_\alpha$ (which can be identified with a Lie subalgebra of $\mathfrak{g}$). Then the element $x=s+n$ is a regular element of $\mathfrak{g}$ with image $a\in\mathfrak{c}$. It is also a regular element of $\mathfrak{h}_\alpha$ with image $a_{H_\alpha}$ in the space of characteristic polynomials $\mathfrak{c}_{H_\alpha}$ of $H_\alpha$. One verifies that the morphism $\mathfrak{c}_{H_\alpha}\to\mathfrak{c}$ sends $a_{H_\alpha}$ to $a$ and is \'etale at that point. One can also verify that in a neighborhood of $a_{H_\alpha}$, the inverse images of $J$, $J'$, and $J^1$ to $\mathfrak{c}_{H_\alpha}$ all coincide with the same groups (now associated to $H_\alpha$). Thus the problem reduces to the special case of groups of semisimple rank one.

A group of semisimple rank one is isomorphic to a product of $\SL_2$, $\PGL_2$, or $\GL_2$ with a torus. Therefore, it suffices to restrict to these three groups. By a direct calculation, one verifies that the centralizer $J$ has a nonconnected fiber in the case of $\SL_2$, whereas in the other two cases its fibers are all connected. The same holds for $J'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{isom sur composantes neutres}
The homomorphism $J\to J^1$ from \ref{J J1} induces an isomorphism on the open subgroup schemes of the neutral components of the fibers.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{The Case of Endoscopic Groups}
Consider an endoscopic datum $(\kappa,\rho_\kappa)$ for $G$ and the associated endoscopic group $H$ (see \ref{donnee endoscopique}). There is a finite flat morphism
$$
\nu:\mathfrak{c}_H\to \mathfrak{c}
$$
between the $X$--schemes of characteristic polynomials of $H$ and $G$ (see \ref{subsection : Transfert des classes}). The regular centralizer $J$ on $\mathfrak{c}$ and the regular centralizer $J_H$ of $H$ on $\mathfrak{c}_H$ are related as follows.

\begin{proposition}\label{J JH}
There exists a canonical homomorphism
$$
\mu:\nu^* J \to J_H
$$
which is an isomorphism over the open subset $\mathfrak{c}_H^{G-\rs}=\nu^{-1}(\mathfrak{c}^\rs)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We use the notations of \ref{subsection : Transfert des classes}. In particular, there is a finite flat cover 
$$
X_{\rho_\kappa}\times \bbt\to \mathfrak{c}
$$
which realizes $\mathfrak{c}$ as the invariant quotient of $X_{\rho_\kappa}\times \bbt$ by $\bbW\rtimes\pi_0(\kappa)$. Similarly, $\mathfrak{c}_H$ is the invariant quotient of $X_{\rho_\kappa}\times \bbt$ by $\bbW_\HH\rtimes\pi_0(\kappa)$. Recall that the morphism $\nu:\mathfrak{c}_H\to\mathfrak{c}$ was constructed in \ref{subsection : Transfert des classes} by producing a homomorphism
$$
\bbW_\HH\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa) \to \bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)
$$
compatible with the actions of these two groups on $X_{\rho_\kappa}\times \bbt$.

Consider the group scheme
$$
J^1=\prod_{(X_\rho\times \bbt)/\mathfrak{c}} (T\times \bbt)^{\bbW\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)}
$$
and its analogue for $H$
$$
J_H^1=\prod_{(X_\rho\times \bbt)/\mathfrak{c}_H} (T\times \bbt)^{\bbW_\HH\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)}.
$$
The obvious morphism
$$
X_{\rho_\kappa}\times \bbt \to (X_{\rho_\kappa}\times \bbt)\times_{\mathfrak{c}} \mathfrak{c}_H
$$
which is $\bbW_\HH\rtimes \pi_0(\kappa)$--equivariant, induces a homomorphism $\nu^* J^1\to J_H^1$. One verifies that this homomorphism is an isomorphism over the open subset $\mathfrak{c}_H^{G-\rs}$ (see \ref{J a J a_H}).

According to Corollary~\ref{isom sur composantes neutres}, we have a homomorphism $J\to J^1$ which induces an isomorphism on the open subgroup schemes of the neutral components of the fibers. To show that the homomorphism $\nu^* J^1\to J_H^1$ restricts to a homomorphism $\nu^*J\to J_H$, it suffices to verify that for every $a_H\in \mathfrak{c}_H(\bar{k})$ with image $a\in\mathfrak{c}(\bar{k})$, the induced homomorphism
$$
\pi_0(J^1_a)\to \pi_0(J^1_{H,a_H})
$$
sends the subgroup $\pi_0(J_a)\subset \pi_0(J^1_a)$ into the subgroup $\pi_0(J_{H,a_H})\subset \pi_0(J^1_{H,a_H})$. Since the set of roots of $H$ is a subset of the set of roots for $G$, the conditions that define the subgroup $\pi_0(J_{H,a_H})$ in $\pi_0(J^1_{H,a_H})$ are satisfied by the elements of $\pi_0(J_a)$ (see \ref{J'}). The proposition follows.
\end{proof}

\section{Affine Springer Fibers}
\label{section : Fibres de Springer affines}

By analogy with the Springer fibers in the simultaneous Grothendieck--Springer resolution, Kazhdan and Lusztig introduced the affine Springer fibers and studied their geometric properties. Goresky, Kottwitz, and MacPherson established the link between the affine Springer fibers and stable orbital integrals via the point counting on a certain quotient of the affine Springer fibers. In this chapter, we review the geometric properties of the affine Springer fibers following Kazhdan and Lusztig and provide some complementary results. The point counting will be revisited in Chapter~\ref{section : comptage}.

Below are the notations that will be used in this chapter. Let $k$ be a finite field with $q$ elements and $\bar{k}$ a separable closure of $k$. Let $F_v$ be a local field of equal characteristic, $\calO_v$ its ring of integers whose residue field $k_v$ is a finite extension of $k$. We denote by
$$
X_v=\Spec(\calO_v)
$$
the associated formal disc and by
$$
X_v^\bullet
$$
the punctured formal disc. Let $v$ be the closed point of $X_v$ and $\eta_v$ its generic point.

Set 
$$
\bar{\calO}_v=\calO_v\hat\otimes_k \bar{k} \quad \text{and} \quad \bar{X}_v=\Spec(\bar{\calO}_v).
$$
The set of connected components of $\bar{X}_v$ is in bijection with the set of embeddings of the extension $k_v$ of $k$ into $\bar{k}$:
$$
\bar{X}_v=\bigsqcup_{\bar{v}:k_v\rightarrow \bar{k}} \bar{X}_{\bar{v}}.
$$

By choosing a geometric point $\bar{\eta}_v$ in the generic fiber of $\bar{X}_{\bar{v}}$, one obtains the usual exact sequence
$$
1\rightarrow I_v \rightarrow \Gamma_v \rightarrow \Gal(\bar{k}/\bar{k}_v)
\rightarrow 1,
$$
where $\Gamma_v=\pi_1(\eta_v,\bar{\eta}_v)$ is the Galois group of $F_v$ and 
$$
I_v=\pi_1(\bar{X}_{\bar{v}},\bar{\eta}_v)
$$
its inertia subgroup.

Let $G$ be a quasi-split form of $\GG$ over $X_v$ associated to an
$\Out(\GG)$--torsor $\rho_G$. By choosing a geometric point of $\bar{\eta}_{\Out,v}$ of $\rho_G$ lying over $\bar{\eta}_v$, one obtains a homomorphism
$$
\rho_G^\bullet:\Gamma_v\rightarrow \Out(\GG)
$$
which factors through $\Gal(\bar{k}/k_v)$. Over $\bar{X}_v$, the torsor $\rho_G$ is trivial. The point in $\bar{\eta}_{\Out,v}$ provides a trivialization of $\rho_G$ over $\bar{X}_{\bar{v}}$.

\subsection{Review of the Affine Grassmannian}
\label{subsection : grassmannienne affine}

For every $k$--scheme $S$, denote by $X_v\hat\times S$ the $v$--adic completion of $X_v\times S$, and by $X_v^\bullet\hat\times S$ the open subset obtained by removing $\{v\}\times S$ from $X_v\hat\times S$. The affine Grassmannian is the functor $\calQ_v$ which assigns to every $k$--scheme the groupoid of $G$--torsors $E_v$ on $X_v\hat\times S$ equipped with a trivialization on $X_v^\bullet\hat\times S$. Note that an automorphism of $E_v$ that is trivial on $X_v^\bullet\hat\times S$ is necessarily trivial; hence this groupoid is a discrete category. One may also replace it by the set of isomorphism classes.

According to \cite{BL} and \cite{Fa-loop}, $\calQ_v$ is strictly representable by an ind--scheme over $k$. More precisely, there exists an inductive system of projective $k$--schemes with closed immersions whose inductive limit represents the functor $\calQ_v$. Since $G$ is a smooth group scheme with connected fibers, the set of $k$--points of $\calQ_v$ can be expressed as the quotient
$$
\calQ_v(k)=G(F_v)/G(\calO_v).
$$
When $G=\GL_r$, this set is naturally identified with the set of $\calO_v$--lattices in the $F_v$--vector space $F_v^{\oplus r}$.

The same definition holds when one replaces $k$ by $\bar{k}$ and $X_v$ by $\bar{X}_{\bar{v}}$ for every embedding $\bar{v}:k_v\rightarrow \bar{k}$. Then one obtains the affine Grassmannian $\calG_{\bar{v}}$ defined over $\bar{k}$. One has the formula
$$
\calG\otimes_k \bar{k}=\prod_{\bar{v}:k_v\rightarrow \bar{k}} \calG_{\bar{v}}.
$$

\subsection{Affine Springer Fibers}\label{Springer}
We retain the notations from \ref{Torsion exterieure}. In particular, we have a scheme $\frakc$ over $X_v$ obtained by outer twisting of the space of characteristic polynomials $\bbc$ of $\bbg$, as in Chevalley's theorem \ref{Chevalley}.

Define
$$
\frakc^\heartsuit(\calO_v)=\frakc(\calO_v)\cap \frakc^{\rs}(F_v)
$$
to be the set of $\calO_v$--points of $\frakc$ whose generic fiber is regular semisimple. Recall that we have the Kostant section $\epsilon:\frakc\rightarrow \frakg^\reg$ (see \ref{section de Kostant tordue}). For every $a\in \frakc^\heartsuit(\calO_v)$, one obtains a Kostant point
$$
[\epsilon](a)\in [\frakg^\reg/G],
$$
which consists of the trivial $G$--torsor $E_0$ on $X_v$ and a section
$$
\gamma_0\in \Gamma(X_v,\ad(E_0))
$$
whose characteristic polynomial is $a$.

For each $a\in\frakc^\heartsuit(\calO_v)$, we define the affine Springer fiber $\calM_v(a)$ as follows. The functor $\calM_v(a)$ assigns to every $k$--scheme $S$ the groupoid of pairs $(E,\phi)$, where $E$ is a $G$--torsor on $X_v\hat\times S$ and $\phi$ is a section of $\ad(E)$, equipped with an isomorphism with $(E_0,\gamma_0)$ on $X_v^\bullet\hat\times S$. In particular,
$$
[\chi](E,\phi)=[\chi](E_0,\gamma_0)=a.
$$
Note that by construction the $G$--torsor $E_0$ is trivial, so that $E$ determines a point of the affine Grassmannian.

\begin{proposition}\label{localement de type fini}
The forgetful functor $(E,\phi)\mapsto E$ defines a morphism from the affine Springer fiber $\calM_v(a)$ to the affine Grassmannian $\calQ_v$ that is a closed immersion. In particular, $\calM_v(a)$ is strictly representable by an ind--scheme. Moreover, the reduced subscheme $\calM_v^{\rm red}(a)$ underlying $\calM_v(a)$ is representable by a scheme locally of finite type.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The first assertion is immediate. The second assertion was proved by Kazhdan and Lusztig \cite{KL}.
\end{proof}

Consider the set of $k$--points of $\calM_v(a)$. Let $(E,\phi)$ be an object in $\calM_v(a,k)$. Since the generic fibers of $E$ and $E_0$ are identified, the data of $E$ corresponds to a class $g\in G(F_v)/G(\calO_v)$. Since $\phi$ is identified with $\gamma_0$ on the generic fiber, for $\phi$ to define a section on $X_v$ of $\ad(E)\otimes D$, it is necessary and sufficient that $\ad(g)^{-1}\gamma_0 \in \frakg(\calO_v)$. Hence,
$$
\calM_v(a,k)=\{g\in G(F_v)/G(\calO_v) \mid \ad(g)^{-1}\gamma_0 \in \frakg(\calO_v)\}.
$$

We now consider a trivial variation of the above construction in the presence of a line bundle $D'$ on $X_v$. This variation will be necessary to realize the passage between affine Springer fibers and Hitchin fibers.

Let $D={D'}^{\otimes 2}$ and let 
$$
h_D:X_v\to \mathbf{B}\GG_m
$$
be the morphism to the classifying stack of $\GG_m$ corresponding to the line bundle $D$. Fix a morphism
$$
h_a:X_v\to [\frakc/\GG_m]
$$
which fits into the commutative diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
  X_v \ar[r]^{h_a} \ar[dr]_{h_D} & [\frakc/\GG_m] \ar[d] \\
  & \mathbf{B}\GG_m
}
$$
Let $[\epsilon]^{D'}(a)$ be the Kostant point of $a$ constructed as in Lemma~\ref{epsilon D'}. Denote by $a^\bullet$ and $h_a^\bullet$ the restrictions of $a$ and $h_a$ to $X_v^\bullet$, and denote by $[\epsilon]^{D'}(a^\bullet)$ the associated Kostant point.
 
\begin{definition}\label{fibre de Springer}
The affine Springer fiber $\calM_v(a)$ is defined as the functor that assigns to every $k$--scheme $S$ the set $\calM_v(a,S)$ of isomorphism classes of morphisms
$$
h_{E,\phi}: X_v\hat\times S\to [\frakg/G\times \GG_m]
$$
that fit into the commutative diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
  X_v\hat \times S \ar[r]^{h_{E,\phi}} \ar[dr]_{h_a} & [\frakg/G\times\GG_m] \ar[d]^{[\chi]} \\
  & [\frakc/\GG_m]
}
$$
and which come equipped with an isomorphism between the restriction of $h_{E,\phi}$ to $X_v^\bullet\hat\times S$ and the Kostant point $[\epsilon]^{D'}(a^\bullet)$.
\end{definition}

The same definition holds when one replaces $k$ by $\bar{k}$ and $X_v$ by $\bar{X}_{\bar{v}}$ for every embedding $\bar{v}:k_v\to \bar{k}$.

\subsection{Symmetries of an Affine Springer Fiber}
\label{symetries d'une fibre de Springer}

The regular centralizer allows one to define the symmetry group of an affine Springer fiber. Let $a\in\frakc^\heartsuit(\calO_v)$ and let $h_a:X_v\to \frakc$ be the corresponding morphism. Set
$$
J_a=h_a^*J,
$$
the pullback of the regular centralizer.

Consider the Picard groupoid $\calP_v(J_a)$ over $\Spec(k)$ which assigns to every $k$--scheme $S$ the Picard groupoid $\calP_v(J_a,S)$ of $J_a$--torsors on $X_v\hat\times S$ equipped with a trivialization on $X_v^\bullet\hat\times S$. One may check that for every $k$--scheme $S$, $\calP_v(J_a,S)$ is a discrete Picard category. Moreover, the functor which assigns to $S$ the group of isomorphism classes of $\calP_v(J_a,S)$ is representable by an ind--group scheme over $k$, which we denote by $\calP_v(J_a)$. The group of $k$--points $\calP_v(J_a)(k)$ is canonically identified with the quotient
$$
J_a(F_v)/J_a(\calO_v).
$$
If the fibers of $J_a$ are connected, then the set of $k$--points of $\calP_v(J_a)$ is identified with the quotient $J_a(F_v)/J_a(\calO_v)$.

Lemma~\ref{J} allows one to define an action of $\calP_v$ on $\calM_v$. Indeed, for every $(E,\phi)\in \calM_v(S)$, there is a homomorphism of group sheaves over $X_v\hat\times S$
$$
J_a \longrightarrow \underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(E,\phi)
$$
which is induced by Lemma~\ref{J}. This allows one to twist $(E,\phi)$ by a $J_a$--torsor on $X_v\hat\times S$ that is trivialized on $X_v^\bullet\hat\times S$.

On the level of $k$--points, we can describe this action explicitly. To simplify the exposition, assume that $J_a$ has connected fibers. The action of the group
$$
\calP_v(J_a)(k)=J_a(F_v)/J_a(\calO_v)
$$
on the set
$$
\calM_v(a)(k)=\{g\in G(F_v)/G(\calO_v) \mid \ad(g)^{-1}\gamma_0 \in \frakg(\calO_v)\}
$$
is described as follows. By \ref{J}, there is a canonical isomorphism from $J_a(F_v)$ to the centralizer $G_{\gamma_0}(F_v)$ of $\gamma_0$, which we denote by $j\mapsto \theta(j)$. Then $J_a(F_v)$ acts on the set of $g\in G(F_v)$ such that $\ad(g)^{-1}(\gamma_0)\in \frakg(\calO_v)$ by 
$$
j\cdot g=\theta(j)g.
$$
In order for this to induce an action of $J_a(F_v)/J_a(\calO_v)$ on $\calM_v(a)(k)$, it is necessary and sufficient that for every $g\in \calM_v(a)(k)$, we have
$$
\theta(J_a(\calO_v)) \subset \ad(g) G(\calO_v).
$$
Let $\gamma=\ad(g)^{-1}(\gamma_0)\in \frakg(\calO_v)$. By \ref{J}, the isomorphism
$$
\ad(g)^{-1}\circ\theta : I_a \to G_{\gamma_0}
$$
extends to a homomorphism of group schemes over $X_v$
$$
\ad(g)^{-1}\circ\theta: J_a \longrightarrow I_\gamma,
$$
which in particular implies that
$$
\theta(J_a(\calO_v)) \subset \ad(g)(I_{\gamma_0}(\calO_v)) \subset \ad(g) G(\calO_v).
$$

We now consider the subfunctor $\calM_v^\reg(a)$ of the affine Springer fiber $\calM_v(a)$ consisting of those morphisms $h_{E,\phi}:X_v\to [\frakg/G\times\GG_m]$ that factor through the open substack $[\frakg^\reg/G\times\GG_m]$. Clearly, $\calM_v^\reg(a)$ is an open subset of $\calM_v(a)$.

\begin{lemma}
The open subset $\calM_v^\reg(a)$ is a principal homogeneous space under the action of $\calP_v(J_a)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This is a consequence of Lemma~\ref{gerbe}.
\end{proof}

Let $\bar{v}: k_v \to \bar{k}$ be an embedding. For every $a\in \frakc(\bar\calO_{\bar{v}})\cap \frakc^\rs(\bar F_{\bar{v}})$, one has the symmetry group $\calP_{\bar{v}}(J_a)$ defined over $\bar{k}$ of the affine fiber $\calM_{\bar{v}}(a)$. If $a\in \frakc(\calO_v)\cap \frakc^\rs(F_v)$, then we have the formula
$$
\calP_v(J_a)\otimes_k \bar{k}=\prod_{\bar{v}:k_v\rightarrow \bar{k}} \calP_{\bar{v}}(J_a).
$$
In the rest of this chapter, we will work over $\bar{k}$ and discuss the geometric properties of $\calP_{\bar{v}}(J_a)$. Recall that over $\bar X_{\bar{v}}$, $G$ is split.

\subsection{Projective Quotient of an Affine Springer Fiber}

Let $a\in \frakc(\bar\calO_{\bar{v}})$ whose generic fiber lies in $\frakc^\rs(\bar F_{\bar{v}})$. According to Kazhdan and Lusztig, the functor $\calM_{\bar{v}}(a)$ has an underlying reduced scheme $\calM_{\bar{v}}^{\rm red}(a)$ which is locally of finite type (see Proposition~\ref{localement de type fini} and \cite{KL}). They also showed that $\calM_{\bar{v}}^{\rm red}(a)$, after taking a suitable quotient by a discrete group, is a projective scheme. We now state their result more precisely.

Let $\Lambda$ be the maximal free quotient of $\pi_0(\calP_{\bar{v}}(J_a))$. Choose an arbitrary lifting
$$
\Lambda\to \calP_{\bar{v}}(J_a)
$$
which induces an action of $\Lambda$ on $\calM_{\bar{v}}(a)$ and on $\calM_{\bar{v}}^{\rm red}(a)$.

\begin{proposition}\label{quotient projectif}
The discrete group $\Lambda$ acts freely on $\calM_{\bar{v}}^{\rm red}(a)$, and the quotient $\calM_{\bar{v}}^{\rm red}(a)/\Lambda$ is a projective $\bar{k}$--scheme.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Proposition 1 of \cite[page 138]{KL} shows that $\Lambda$ acts freely on $\calM_{\bar{v}}^{\rm red}(a)$ and that by truncating the reduced Springer fiber $\calM_{\bar{v}}^{\rm red}(a)$ one obtains projective schemes which surject onto the quotient $\calM_{\bar{v}}^{\rm red}(a)/\Lambda$. It follows that the quotient is also a projective scheme.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Approximation}
\label{subsection : approximation}

According to a well-known theorem of Harish-Chandra, the semisimple regular orbital integrals are locally constant. In this section, we will show a geometric variant, stronger than this theorem.

\begin{proposition}\label{HC}
Let $a\in \frakc(\calO_v)\cap \frakc^\rs(F_v)$, and let $\calM_v(a)$ be the affine Springer fiber and $\calP_v(J_a)$ the symmetry group of $\calM_v(a)$. Then there exists an integer $N$ such that for every finite extension $k'$ of $k$, and for every
$$
a'\in\frakc(\calO_v\otimes_k k')
$$
with
$$
a\equiv a' \mod \epsilon_v^N,
$$
the affine Springer fiber $\calM_v(a')$, endowed with the action of $\calP_v(J_{a'})$, is isomorphic to $\calM_v(a)\otimes_k k'$ endowed with the action of $\calP_v(J_a)\otimes_k k'$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Consider the cameral cover $\widetilde X_{a,v}$ of $X_v$ associated to $a$, defined by the cartesian diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
\widetilde X_{a,v} \ar[d]_{\pi_a} \ar[r] & \mathfrak{t} \\
X_v \ar[r]_{a} & \frakc.
}
$$
The morphism $\pi_a$ is finite, flat, and generically \'etale. The cameral cover $\widetilde X_{a,v}$ is endowed with an action of $W$ coming from its action on $\mathfrak{t}$. We also associate to $a'\in\frakc(\calO_v\otimes_k k')$ its cameral cover $\widetilde X_{a',v}\to X_v\otimes_k k'$.

\begin{lemma}
Let $a\in\frakc(\calO_v)\cap \frakc^\rs(F_v)$. Then there exists an integer $N$ such that for every finite extension $k'$ of $k$, and for every
$$
a'\in\frakc(\calO_v\otimes_k k')
$$
with
$$
a\equiv a' \mod \epsilon_v^N,
$$
the cameral cover $\widetilde X_{a',v}$ of $X_v\otimes_k k'$ endowed with the $W$--action is isomorphic to the cameral cover $\widetilde X_{a,v}\otimes_k k'$ endowed with the $W$--action. Moreover, one may require that the isomorphism lifts the evident isomorphism modulo $\epsilon_v^N$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This is a special case of a lemma of Artin--Hironaka \cite[Lemma 3.12]{Artin}. The second assertion is implicit in Artin's proof.
\end{proof}

Assume that the cameral covers $\widetilde X_{a,v}$ and $\widetilde X_{a',v}$ of $X_v\otimes_k k'$ are isomorphic. We now show that $\calM_v(a)\otimes_k k'$, with the action of $\calP_v(J_a)\otimes_k k'$, and $\calM_v(a')$, with the action of $\calP_v(J_{a'})$, are isomorphic. Replacing $k$ by $k'$, we may assume $k=k'$.

To do this, we need a description of the affine Springer fiber in terms of the regular centralizer. Let $\gamma_0=\epsilon(a):X_v\to\frakg$ be the Kostant section associated to $a$. Then there is a canonical isomorphism between $J_a$ and the $X_v$--group scheme $I_{\gamma_0}=\gamma_0^*I$, where $I$ is the group scheme of centralizers over $\frakg$. The obvious homomorphism $I_{\gamma_0}\to G$ induces an injective homomorphism of vector bundles on $X_v$
$$
{\rm Lie}(I_{\gamma_0})\to \frakg.
$$
The following lemma shows that the affine Springer fiber $\calM_v(a)$ depends only on the commutative Lie subalgebra ${\rm Lie}(I_{\gamma_0})$ of $\frakg$.

\begin{lemma}
Let $g\in G(F_v)$. Then $\ad(g)^{-1}(\gamma_0)\in \frakg(\calO_v)$ if and only if $\ad(g)^{-1} {\rm Lie}(I_{\gamma_0}) \subset \frakg(\calO_v)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $\gamma_0\in {\rm Lie}(I_{\gamma_0})$, if $\ad(g)^{-1} {\rm Lie}(I_{\gamma_0})\subset \frakg(\calO_v)$ then immediately $\ad(g)^{-1}(\gamma_0)\in \frakg(\calO_v)$. Conversely, suppose $g\in G(F_v)$ such that $\gamma=\ad(g)^{-1}(\gamma_0) \in \frakg(\calO_v)$. Let $I_\gamma=\gamma^*I$. The fact (see \ref{J}) that the generic isomorphism
$$\ad(g)^{-1}\circ\theta : I_a \to I_{\gamma_0}$$
extends to a homomorphism $J_a\to I_\gamma$ implies, in particular, that
$$\ad(g)^{-1} {\rm Lie}(I_{\gamma_0})\subset \frakg(\calO_v).
$$
\end{proof}

To complete the proof of Proposition~\ref{HC}, it suffices to prove the following lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
Let $a,a'\in \frakc(\calO_v)\cap \frakc^\rs(F_v)$ be such that $a\equiv a'
\mod \epsilon_v$. Suppose there exists an isomorphism between the cameral covers $\widetilde X_{a,v}$ and $\widetilde X_{a',v}$ (with their $W$--action) that lifts the evident isomorphism modulo $\epsilon_v^N$. Let $\gamma_0=\epsilon(a)$ be the Kostant section for $a$ and $\gamma'_0=\epsilon(a')$ the Kostant section for $a'$. Let $I_{\gamma_0}$ and $I_{\gamma'_0}$ be the centralizer group schemes in $G$ of $\gamma_0$ and $\gamma'_0$. Then there exists $g\in G(\calO_v)$ such that
$$
\ad(g)^{-1}I_{\gamma_0}= I_{\gamma'_0}.
$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This lemma is a consequence of a result of Donagi and Gaitsgory \cite[Theorem 11.8]{DG}. For the reader's convenience, we extract the part useful for our purpose. Since the group schemes $I_{\gamma_0}$ and $I_{\gamma'_0}$ are completely determined by the cameral covers $\widetilde X_{a,v}$ and $\widetilde X_{a',v}$ (see \ref{J=J'}), the isomorphism between $\widetilde X_{a,v}$ and $\widetilde X_{a',v}$ induces an isomorphism
$$\iota: I_{\gamma_0}\to I_{\gamma'_0}.$$ 
This isomorphism transports $\gamma_0\in {\rm Lie}(I_{\gamma_0})$ to an element $\iota(\gamma_0)\in {\rm Lie}(I_{\gamma'_0})$. Since the special fibers of $\iota(\gamma_0)$ and $\gamma'_0$ coincide, the section $\iota(\gamma_0):X_v\to\frakg$ factors through the regular locus $\frakg^\reg$. One deduces that
$$
I_{\iota(\gamma_0)}=I_{\gamma'_0}
$$
as subgroup schemes of $G$.

We have two sections
$$
\gamma_0, \iota(\gamma_0): X_v\to \frakg^\reg
$$
which have the same characteristic polynomial $a$ and are congruent modulo $\epsilon_v$. Since the morphism
$$
G\times_X \frakg^\reg \to \frakg^\reg\times_{\frakc}\frakg^\reg
$$
is smooth, there exists $g\in G(\calO_v)$ such that $g\equiv 1 \mod \epsilon_v$ and such that $\ad(g^{-1})(\gamma_0)=\iota(\gamma_0)$. Hence,
$$
\ad(g)^{-1}(I_{\gamma_0})= I_{\iota(\gamma_0)}=I_{\gamma'_0}.
$$
This is what we wanted.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Linear Case}
\label{Cas lineaire local}

We now examine the affine Springer fibers for the linear group, following Laumon \cite{L}. (See also \cite{compagnon} for a similar discussion in the case of classical groups.) % [journal 3.6] the standing hypothesis r < p was dropped here and where it is invoked.
Let $G=\GL(r)$ with $r<p$.

We retain the notations fixed at the beginning of this chapter. A point
$$
a\in\frakc(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v})
$$
is represented by a monic polynomial of degree $r$ in the variable $t$
$$
P(a,t)=t^r-a_1t^{r-1}+\cdots+(-1)^r a_r\in \bar{\calO}_{\bar v}[t].
$$
Form the finite, flat $\bar{\calO}_{\bar v}$--algebra of rank $r$
$$
B=\bar{\calO}_{\bar v}[t]/P(a,t)
$$
and let
$$
E=B\otimes_{\bar{\calO}_{\bar v}} \bar{F}_{\bar v}.
$$
The hypothesis $a\in\frakc^\heartsuit(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v})$ implies that $E$ is a finite \'etale $\bar{F}_{\bar v}$--algebra of dimension $r$. One may write $E$ as a product
$$
E=E_1\times\cdots\times E_s
$$
of $s$ separable extensions of $\bar{F}_{\bar v}$ with $s\le r$.

\begin{numero}
In this situation, the affine Springer fiber may be described in terms of lattices. The $\bar{k}$--points of the affine Springer fiber $\calM_{\bar v}(a)$ are the set of $B$--lattices in $E$, i.e., the $B$--modules $\calL$ contained in the $\bar{F}_{\bar v}$--vector space $E$ which are $\bar{\calO}_{\bar v}$--lattices. The $\bar{k}$--points of the regular part $\calM_{\bar v}^\reg(a)$ consist of the $B$--lattices in $E$ that are free $B$--modules. The $\bar{k}$--points of the symmetry group $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)$ are given by the group $E^\times/B^\times$.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
The normalization $B^\flat$ of $B$ is the ring of integers of $E$. Then there is an exact sequence
$$
1\rightarrow (B^\flat)^\times/B^\times \rightarrow E^\times/B^\times \rightarrow E^\times/(B^\flat)^\times \rightarrow 1,
$$
where $(B^\flat)^\times/B^\times$ is the group of $\bar{k}$--points of the neutral component of the group $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)$. Consequently, the group of connected components $\pi_0(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a))$ is canonically isomorphic to $E^\times/(B^\flat)^\times$, which is a free abelian group equipped with a basis indexed by the set of connected components of $\Spec(B^\flat)$.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
In this case, the dimension of $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)$ equals the Serre $\delta$--invariant
$$
\delta_{\bar v}(a)=\dim_k(B^\flat/B).
$$
Moreover, this integer can be expressed in terms of the discriminant. Let 
$$
d_{\bar v}(a)=\operatorname{val}_{\bar v}(\discrim(a))
$$
be the $\bar{v}$--adic valuation of the discriminant of $a$. Using \cite[III.3 Proposition 5 and III.6 Corollary 1]{Se} and the hypothesis $r<p$, one obtains the formula
$$
\delta_{\bar v}(a)=\frac{d_{\bar v}(a)-c_{\bar v}(a)}{2},
$$
where $c_{\bar v}(a)=r-s$.
\end{numero}

\subsection{Dimension}
\label{dimension}

In \cite{KL}, Kazhdan and Lusztig showed that
$$
\dim(\calM_{\bar v}(a))=\dim(\calM_{\bar v}^\reg(a)).
$$
In fact, one can deduce a more precise statement from their results.

\begin{proposition}\label{dimension du complement}
The complement of the regular open subset $\calM_{\bar v}^\reg(a)$ in the affine Springer fiber $\calM_{\bar v}(a)$ has dimension strictly less than that of $\calM_{\bar v}(a)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
To discuss the dimension, one may neglect the nilpotents in the structure sheaves of $\calM_{\bar v}(a)$. As recalled in Section~\ref{Springer}, $\calM_{\bar v}(a)$ is the subvariety of the affine Grassmannian consisting of those $g\in G(F_{\bar v})/G(\calO_{\bar v})$ such that $\ad(g)^{-1}(\gamma_0)\in \frakg(\calO_{\bar v})$.

Following Kazhdan and Lusztig, consider also the subvariety $\calB_{\bar v}(a)$ of the affine flag variety fixed by $\gamma_0$, i.e., those $g\in G(F_{\bar v})/{\rm Iw}_{\bar v}$ such that $\ad(g)^{-1}\gamma_0\in \Lie({\rm Iw}_{\bar v})$, where ${\rm Iw}_{\bar v}$ is an Iwahori subgroup of $G(\calO_{\bar v})$. In \cite{KL}, Kazhdan and Lusztig proved that the affine Springer fibers for Iwahori subgroups are equidimensional. The equidimensionality of classical Springer fibers was previously proved by Spaltenstein \cite{Sp}.

The morphism $\calB_{\bar v}(a)\to \calM_{\bar v}(a)$ is finite over $\calM_{\bar v}^\reg(a)$. The fibers over points $x\in\calM_{\bar v}(a)\setminus \calM_{\bar v}^\reg(a)$ are nonempty and have dimension at least one. The equidimensionality of $\calB_{\bar v}(a)$ then implies that the irreducible components of $\calM_{\bar v}(a)-\calM_{\bar v}^\reg(a)$ are of strictly smaller dimension than $\dim(\calM_{\bar v}^\reg(a))$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{dim zero}
If $\dim(\calM_v(a))=0$, then the dense open $\calM_v^\reg(a)$ equals $\calM_v(a)$.
\end{corollary}

Kazhdan and Lusztig also conjectured a formula expressing the above dimension in terms of the discriminant and an effective defect term related to monodromy, which was later proved by Bezrukavnikov in \cite{Bz}. Let us recall this formula.

Keep the notations fixed at the beginning of this chapter. Let 
$$a:\bar X_{\bar v} \to \frakc$$ 
be a morphism whose image is not contained in the discriminant divisor $\discrim_G$. By pulling back $\discrim_G$, one obtains an effective Cartier divisor on $\bar X_{\bar v}$ supported on its closed point. Its degree is a natural number that we denote by
$$
d_{\bar v}(a):=\deg_{\bar v}(a^*{\discrim_G}).
$$

By pulling back the finite \'etale cover $\pi:\frakt^\rs\to\frakc^\rs$ via the morphism $a:\bar X_{\bar v}^\bullet\to \frakc^\rs$, one obtains a $W$--torsor $\pi_a$ over $\bar X_{\bar v}^\bullet$. Recall that over $\bar X_{\bar v}$ the quasi-split form splits so that, after base change to $\bar X_{\bar v}$, we have $W=\bbW$. By choosing a geometric point of this torsor over the geometric point $\bar\eta_{\bar v}$ of $\bar X_{\bar v}$, one obtains a homomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{pi_a^bullet}
\pi_a^\bullet: I_v \to \bbW.
\end{equation}
Since the characteristic does not divide the order of $\bbW$, $\pi_a^\bullet$ factors through the tame quotient $I_v^{\rm tame}$ of $I_v$, so that its image is a cyclic subgroup. Denote
\begin{equation}\label{c_v(a)}
c_{\bar v}(a):=\dim(\bbt) - \dim(\bbt^{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)}).
\end{equation}
The following result was proved by Bezrukavnikov in \cite{Bz}.

\begin{proposition}\label{dimension Bezru}
We have the equalities
$$\dim(\calM^\reg_{\bar v}(a))=\dim(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a))=\frac{d_{\bar v}(a)-c_{\bar v}(a)}{2}.$$
\end{proposition}

We now set
$$
\delta_{\bar v}(a)=\dim(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a))
$$
and refer to it as the \emph{local $\delta$--invariant}.

\subsection{N\'eron Model}
\label{subsection : Modele de Neron}

The dimension of $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)$ can be computed in another way using the N\'eron model of $J_a$. In fact, the N\'eron model allows us to fully analyze the structure of $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)$. According to Bosch, L\"{u}tkebohmert, and Raynaud \cite{BLR}, there exists a unique smooth group scheme of finite type $J_a^\flat$ over $\bar X_{\bar v}$ with the same generic fiber as $J_a$ and which is maximal with this property; that is, for every other smooth group scheme $J'$ of finite type over $\bar X_{\bar v}$ with the same generic fiber, there exists a canonical homomorphism $J'\to J_a^\flat$ inducing the identity on the generic fiber. In particular, there is a canonical homomorphism $J_a\to J_a^\flat$. On the level of points, this homomorphism defines the inclusions
$$
J_a(\bar\calO_{\bar v})\subset J_a^\flat(\bar\calO_{\bar v})\subset J_a(\bar F_{\bar v}),
$$
where $J_a^\flat(\bar\calO_{\bar v})$ is the maximal bounded subgroup of $J_a(F_{\bar v})$. We call $J_a^\flat$ the \emph{N\'eron model} of $J_a$. (In the terminology of \cite{BLR}, $J_a^\flat$ is called the finite type N\'eron model, to distinguish it from their N\'eron model which is only locally of finite type.)

Replacing in the definition of $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)$ the group scheme $J_a$ by its N\'eron model $J_a^\flat$, we obtain an ind--group scheme $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^\flat)$ over $\bar{k}$. The homomorphism of group schemes $J_a\to J_a^\flat$ induces a homomorphism of ind--groups
$$\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\to \calP_{\bar v}(J_a^\flat)$$
which gives a decomposition of $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)$.

\begin{lemma}\label{R_v(a)}
The group $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^\flat)$ is homeomorphic to a free abelian group of finite type. The homomorphism
$$
p_{\bar v}:\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\to \calP_{\bar v}(J_a^\flat)
$$
is surjective. Its kernel $\calR_{\bar v}(a)$ is an affine group scheme of finite type over $\bar{k}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $J_a^\flat(\bar\calO_{\bar v})$ is the maximal bounded subgroup of the torus $J_a(\bar F_{\bar v})$, the quotient $J_a(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_a^\flat(\bar\calO_{\bar v})$ is a free abelian group of finite type. The homomorphism
$$
\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)(\bar{k}) = J_a(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_a(\bar\calO_{\bar v}) \to J_a(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_a^\flat(\bar\calO_{\bar v}) = \calP_{\bar v}(J_a^\flat)(\bar{k})
$$
is clearly surjective.

For a sufficiently large integer $N$, $J_a(\calO_{\bar v})$ contains the kernel of the homomorphism $J_a^\flat(\bar\calO_{\bar v}) \to J_a^\flat(\bar\calO_{\bar v}/\varepsilon_{\bar v}^N\bar\calO_{\bar v})$. It follows that $\calR_{\bar v}(a)$ is a quotient of the Weil restriction
$$
\prod_{\Spec(\bar\calO_{\bar v}/\varepsilon_{\bar v}^N\bar\calO_{\bar v})/\Spec(\bar{k})} J_a^\flat\otimes_{\bar\calO_{\bar v}} (\bar\calO_{\bar v}/\varepsilon_{\bar v}^N\bar\calO_{\bar v}),
$$
which is an affine smooth $\bar{k}$--group scheme of finite type. Hence the lemma.
\end{proof}

The N\'eron model can be explicitly constructed using the normalization of the cameral cover. Let $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}$ be the inverse image of $\mathfrak{t}\to\mathfrak{c}$ via the morphism $a:\bar X_{\bar v}\to \mathfrak{c}$. Consider the normalization $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat$ of $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}$, which is a finite flat scheme over $\bar X_{\bar v}$ endowed with a $W$--action. Since the residue field of $\bar X_{\bar v}$ is algebraically closed, $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat$ is a complete regular one-dimensional semi-local scheme.

\begin{proposition}\label{J a flat}
Let $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat=\Spec(\widetilde \calO_{\bar v}^\flat)$ be the normalization of $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}$. Then the N\'eron model $J_a^\flat$ of $J_a$ is the subgroup of fixed points under the diagonal $W$--action in the Weil restriction of the torus $T\times_{\bar X_{\bar v}} \widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat$ from $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat$ to $\bar X_{\bar v}$, that is,
$$
J_a^\flat=\prod_{\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat/\bar X_{\bar v}} \bigl(T\times_{\bar X_{\bar v}} \widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat\bigr)^W.
$$
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Denote by $\pi_a^\flat$ the morphism $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat \to \bar X_{\bar v}$. As in Lemma~\ref{J1}, the subfunctor of fixed points under the diagonal $W$--action on the Weil restriction 
$$\prod_{\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat/\bar X_{\bar v}} \bigl(T\times_{\bar X_{\bar v}} \widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat\bigr)$$ 
is a smooth group scheme of finite type over $\bar X_{\bar v}$. It is more convenient to work with the sheaf $(\pi_{a*}^\flat T)^W$, which represents the Weil restriction above.

According to the Galois description of the regular centralizer (see \ref{desciption galoisienne de J}), the pullback $\pi_a^{\flat*} J_a$ over 
$$\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^{\flat\bullet}=\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat\times_{\bar X_{\bar v}} \bar X_{\bar v}^\bullet$$
is canonically isomorphic to the torus $T\times_{\bar X_{\bar v}^\bullet} \widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^{\flat\bullet}$. Since the N\'eron model of $T\times_{\bar X_{\bar v}^\bullet} \widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^{\flat\bullet}$ is the torus $T\times_{\bar X_{\bar v}} \widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat$, we have a canonical homomorphism
$$
\pi_a^{\flat*} J_a \longrightarrow T\times_{\bar X_{\bar v}} \widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\flat.
$$
By adjunction, one obtains a homomorphism $J_a \longrightarrow \pi_{a*}^\flat T$. The same reasoning applies to any smooth group scheme of finite type having the same generic fiber as $J_a$. Hence, the lemma follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{delta local}
We have the formula
$$
\dim\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)=\dim_{\bar{k}}\Bigl(\bigl(\frakt\otimes_{\bar{\calO}_{\bar v}} \widetilde \calO_{\bar v}^\flat/\widetilde\calO_{\bar v}\bigr)^W\Bigr).
$$
\end{corollary}

This thus gives another formula for the local $\delta$--invariant $\delta_{\bar v}(a)$. Note that the integer $c_{\bar v}(a)$ defined in \eqref{c_v(a)} is equal to the drop in the toric rank of the N\'eron model $J_a^\flat$, that is, the difference between $r$ and the toric rank of the special fiber of $J_a^\flat$.

\subsection{Connected Components}
In this section we describe the group of connected components $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)$.

Let $a:\bar X_{\bar v}\to \frakc$ be a morphism whose image is not contained in the discriminant divisor. Then there exists a smooth group scheme $J_a$ over $\bar X_{\bar v}$ whose generic fiber is a torus. Let $J_a^0$ be the open subgroup scheme of $J_a$ consisting of the neutral components of its fibers. Over $\bar F_{\bar v}$, $J_a$ is connected, so that the homomorphism $J_a^0\to J_a$ induces an isomorphism over $\bar F_{\bar v}$. As a homomorphism of sheaves of abelian groups, it is injective, and its cokernel is supported on the special fiber of $\bar X_{\bar v}$ with fiber
$$
\pi_0(J_{a,\bar v}) = J_a(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v})/J_a^0(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v}).
$$

The inclusions
$$
J_a^0(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v})\subset J_a(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v})\subset J_a(\bar{F}_{\bar v})
$$
induce an exact sequence
$$
1\rightarrow \pi_0(J_{a,\bar v}) \rightarrow J_a(\bar{F}_{\bar v})/J_a^0(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v}) \rightarrow J_a(\bar{F}_{\bar v})/J_a(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v}) \rightarrow 1.
$$
Thus, we have a surjective homomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{P_v(J_a^0)->P_v(J_a)}
\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0) \to \calP_{\bar v}(J_a)
\end{equation}
whose kernel is the finite group $\pi_0(J_{a,\bar v})$. Hence, we obtain an exact sequence
$$
\pi_0(J_{a,\bar v}) \to \pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr) \to \pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr) \to 1.
$$
To determine $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)$, it suffices to describe the group $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr)$ and the image of the map
$$
\pi_0(J_{a,\bar v}) \to \pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr).
$$

As in Tate--Nakayama duality, the group of connected components $\pi_0(\calP_a)$ is more easily expressed via duality. For every finitely generated abelian group $\Lambda$, we denote
$$
\Lambda^* = \Spec(\Ql[\Lambda])
$$
the diagonalizable $\Ql$--group whose group of characters is $\Lambda$, and conversely, for every diagonalizable $\Ql$--group $A$, we denote by $A^*$ its character group, which is a finitely generated abelian group.

To write explicit formulas, fix a trivialization of $\rho_{\Out}$ over $\bar X_v$. This allows one to identify $W$ and $\bbW$. Let $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\bullet$ be the inverse image of the finite \'etale cover $\pi:\frakt^\rs\to\frakc^\rs$ via the morphism $a:\bar X_{\bar v}\to \frakc$. By choosing a geometric point of $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}$ over the geometric point $\bar\eta_v$ of $\bar X_{\bar v}$, one obtains a homomorphism
% [journal 3.9] the journal numbers only the first display in 3.9; this one was unnumbered there. Unnumbered.
$$\pi_a^\bullet: I_v \to \bbW.$$

\begin{proposition}\label{pi 0 local}
With the choice of a geometric point of $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}$ over the geometric point $\bar\eta_v$ of $\bar X_{\bar v}$, there is a canonical isomorphism of diagonalizable groups
$$
\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr)^* = \hat{\bbT}^{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)}.
$$
Similarly, we have an isomorphism
$$
\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)^* = \hat{\bbT}\bigl(\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)\bigr),
$$
where $\hat{\bbT}\bigl(\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)\bigr)$ is the subgroup of $\hat{\bbT}^{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)}$ consisting of those elements $\kappa\in \hat{\bbT}$ such that $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr)$ is contained in the Weyl group of the neutral component $\hat{\bbH}$ of the centralizer of $\kappa$ in $\hat{\bbG}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Consider the group scheme of neutral components $J_a^{\flat,0}$ of the N\'eron model $J_a^\flat$ of $J_a$. (In the terminology of \cite{BLR}, this is the connected N\'eron model.) Since $J_a^0$ has connected fibers, the homomorphism $J_a^0\to J_a^\flat$ factors through $J_a^{\flat,0}$.

\begin{lemma}
The homomorphism $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0) \to \calP_{\bar v}(J_a^{\flat,0})$ induces an isomorphism
$$
\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr) \cong J_a(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_a^{\flat,0}(\bar\calO_{\bar v}).
$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since both $J_a^0$ and $J_a^{\flat,0}$ have connected fibers, the homomorphism $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0) \to \calP_{\bar v}(J_a^{\flat,0})$ induces an isomorphism on the groups of connected components. Topologically, $\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^{\flat,0})$ is the discrete group $J_a(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_a^{\flat,0}(\bar\calO_{\bar v})$.
\end{proof}

For any torus $A$ over $\bar F_{\bar v}$, consider the connected N\'eron model $A^{\flat,0}$ of $A$ over $\bar\calO_{\bar v}$ and the finitely generated abelian group $A(\bar F_{\bar v})/A^{\flat,0}(\bar\calO_{\bar v})$. One may verify (see \cite{Ra}) that the functor $A\mapsto A(\bar F_{\bar v})/A^{\flat,0}(\bar\calO_{\bar v})$ satisfies the axioms of \cite[Lemma 2.2]{K-Iso1}. Following Kottwitz, one obtains a general formula for $A(\bar F_{\bar v})/A^{\flat,0}(\bar\calO_{\bar v})$; in particular, we obtain the following lemma.

\begin{lemma}\label{Kottwitz local}
With the above notation, there is an isomorphism
$$
J_a(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_a^{\flat,0}(\bar\calO_{\bar v}) \cong (\bbX_*)_{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)}.
$$
\end{lemma}

The conjunction of the previous two lemmas yields the isomorphism
$$
\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr) = (\bbX_*)_{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)},
$$
which, by duality, induces the first assertion of the proposition:
$$
\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr)^* = \hat{\bbT}^{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)}.
$$

The proof of the second assertion uses the trick of $z$--extensions. Following \cite[7.5]{K-EST}, there exists an exact sequence
$$
1\rightarrow G\rightarrow G_1\rightarrow C\rightarrow 1
$$
of reductive group schemes over $X$ obtained by outer twisting an exact sequence of split reductive groups
$$
1\rightarrow \GG\rightarrow \GG_1\rightarrow \mathbb{C}\rightarrow 1,
$$
where $\mathbb{C}$ is a torus and $\GG_1$ is a reductive group with connected center. Its dual $\hat{\bbG}_1$ has a simply connected derived subgroup. There exists a maximal torus $\bbT_1$ in $\GG_1$ such that one has an exact sequence
$$
1\rightarrow \bbT\rightarrow \bbT_1\rightarrow \mathbb{C}\rightarrow 1.
$$

Replacing $G$ by $G_1$, denote by $\frakc_1$ the corresponding space of characteristic polynomials. The homomorphism $G\rightarrow G_1$ induces a morphism
$$
\alpha:\frakc\rightarrow \frakc_1.
$$
Over $\frakc_1$, we have the regular centralizer $J_1$, which is a smooth group scheme with connected fibers since the center of $G_1$ is connected. There is an exact sequence of smooth commutative group schemes
$$
1\rightarrow J\rightarrow \alpha^*J_1\rightarrow C\rightarrow 1.
$$
Let $a:\bar X_{\bar v}\rightarrow \frakc$ be such that the restriction $a|_{\bar X_{\bar v}^\bullet}$ has image in $\frakc^\rs$. Denote by $\alpha(a):\bar X_{\bar v}\rightarrow \frakc_1$ the composition of $a$ with $\alpha$. Over $\bar X_{\bar v}$, we have an exact sequence
$$
1\rightarrow J_a\rightarrow J_{1,\alpha(a)}\rightarrow C\rightarrow 1,
$$
with $J_a=a^*J$ and $(J_1)_{\alpha(a)}=\alpha(a)^*J_1$.

\begin{proposition}
The homomorphism 
$$
\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr) \to \pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_{1,\alpha(a)})\bigr)
$$ 
is injective.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $J\to \alpha^*J_1$ is a closed immersion (hence proper), we have
$$
J_a(\bar\calO_{\bar v}) = J_a(\bar F_{\bar v})\cap (J_1)_{\alpha(a)}(\bar\calO_{\bar v}).
$$
It follows that the homomorphism
$$
j_{\alpha(a)}:J_a(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_a(\bar\calO_{\bar v}) \to
J_{1,\alpha(a)}(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_{1,\alpha(a)}(\bar\calO_{\bar v})
$$
is injective. Since $C$ is a torus over $\bar X_{\bar v}$, the group
$$
C(\bar F_{\bar v})/C(\bar\calO_{\bar v})
$$
is a free abelian group of finite type and discrete. The neutral component of
$$
J_{1,\alpha(a)}(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_{1,\alpha(a)}(\bar\calO_{\bar v})
$$
therefore lies in the image of $j_{\alpha(a)}$. Consequently, $j_{\alpha(a)}$ induces an isomorphism between the neutral component of $J_a(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_a(\bar\calO_{\bar v})$ and that of $J_{1,\alpha(a)}(\bar F_{\bar v})/J_{1,\alpha(a)}(\bar\calO_{\bar v})$. It follows that the homomorphism $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)\to \pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_{1,\alpha(a)})\bigr)$ is injective.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
There is a canonical isomorphism between $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)$ and the image of the homomorphism
$$
\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr)\to \pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_{1,\alpha(a)})\bigr).
$$
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In addition to the previous proposition, it suffices to invoke the surjectivity of $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr)\to \pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)$.
\end{proof}

By choosing a geometric point of $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}^\bullet$, one may identify the group $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)$ with the image of the homomorphism
$$
(\bbX_*)_{I_v} \to (\bbX_{1,*})_{I_v},
$$
where $\bbX_{1,*}=\Hom(\GG_m,\bbT_1)$. Note that the equality
$$
\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_{1,\alpha(a)})\bigr) = (\bbX_{1,*})_{I_v}
$$
follows from the connectedness of the fibers of $J_1$ and Lemma~\ref{Kottwitz local}. Dualizing, one obtains an isomorphism between the subgroup $\Spec(\Ql[\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr])$ of $\hat{\bbT}^{I_v}$ and the image of the homomorphism
$$
\hat{\bbT}_1^{I_v}=\Spec(\Ql[(\bbX_{1,*})_{I_v}]) \to \Spec\Bigl(\Ql[(\bbX_*)_{I_v}]\Bigr)=\hat{\bbT}^{I_v}.
$$

It remains now to show that the image of $\hat{\bbT}_1^{I_v}$ in $\hat{\bbT}^{I_v}$ is exactly the subgroup of those elements $\kappa\in \hat{\bbT}$ such that $\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)$ is contained in the Weyl group of the neutral component $\hat{\bbH}$ of the centralizer of $\kappa$ in $\hat{\bbG}$. For every $\kappa\in\hat{\bbT}$ and for every $\kappa_1\in\hat{\bbT}_1$ mapping to $\kappa$, the centralizer $\hat{\bbH}_1$ of $\kappa_1$ in $\hat{\bbG}_1$ is connected and its image in $\hat{\bbG}$ is $\hat{\bbH}$. (Indeed, $\hat{\bbG}_{1}$ has a simply connected derived subgroup so that the centralizer of a semisimple element of $\hat{\bbG}_{1}$ is connected.) The desired result follows.
\end{proof}

We now present another, more explicit description of $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)$, though it is ultimately less convenient.

\begin{proposition}
With the choice of a geometric point of $\widetilde X_{a,\bar v}$ over the geometric point $\bar{\eta}_{v}$ of $\bar X_{\bar v}$, there is a canonical isomorphism between $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)$ and the cokernel of the composite of two maps
$$
\pi_0(Z_{\GG}) \longrightarrow \pi_0\bigl(\bbT^{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)}\bigr) \longrightarrow (\bbX_*)_{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)},
$$
where the first is induced by the evident homomorphism $Z_{\GG}\to \bbT^{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)}$ and the second is the canonical isomorphism of $\pi_0\bigl(\bbT^{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)}\bigr)$ onto the torsion subgroup of $(\bbX_*)_{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
One knows that $\pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\bigr)$ is the cokernel of the homomorphism $\pi_0(J_{a,\bar v}) \to \pi_0\bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a^0)\bigr)$. By \ref{pi 0 ZG}, $\pi_0(J_{a,\bar v})$ and $\pi_0(Z_{\GG})$ have the same image in $(\bbX_*)_{\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)}$. The proposition follows.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Density of the Regular Orbit}
We refer to Section~\ref{dense} for an outline of the proof of the following proposition. It will be deduced from its global analogue which will be proved in detail later. Only the global analogue will be used in the remainder of the paper.

\begin{proposition}\label{densite locale}
The open subset $\calM_v^\reg(a)$ is dense in $\calM_v(a)$.
\end{proposition}

Note that we already know that the closed complement $\calM_v(a)-\calM_v^\reg(a)$ has dimension strictly less than $\dim\bigl(\calM_v^\reg(a)\bigr)$ (see Proposition~\ref{dimension du complement}).

\begin{corollary}
The set of irreducible components of the affine Springer fiber $\calM_v(a)$ is canonically in bijection with the group of connected components of the group $\calP_v(J_a)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, one has an isomorphism $\calP_v(J_a)\to \calM_v^\reg(a)$ by letting $\calP_v(J_a)$ act on the Kostant point.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Case of an Endoscopic Group}

Let $H$ be an endoscopic group of $G$ in the sense of \ref{subsection : Groupes endoscopiques}. Let $a_H\in\frakc_H(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v})$ with image $a\in\frakc(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v})\cap \frakc^\rs(\bar F_{\bar v})$. There is no direct relation between the affine Springer fibers $\calM_{H,v}(a_H)$ and $\calM_v(a)$. However, there is an obvious relation between their symmetry groups.

Thus, we have two $\bar X_{\bar v}$--group schemes $J_a=a^*J$ and $J_{H,a_H}=a_H^*J_H$, which are related by a homomorphism
$$
\mu_{a_H}: J_a \to J_{H,a_H},
$$
which is an isomorphism over the punctured disc $\bar X_{\bar v}^\bullet$. This homomorphism is constructed by pulling back the homomorphism $\mu$ constructed in \ref{J JH} via $a_H$.

Let $\calR_{H,\bar v}^G(a_H)$ be the affine algebraic group over $\bar{k}$ whose group of $\bar{k}$--points is
$$
\calR_{H,\bar v}^G(a_H)(\bar{k})=J_{H,a_H}(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v})/ J_a(\bar{\calO}_{\bar v}).
$$
Then we have the exact sequence
\begin{equation}\label{RPP local}
1\rightarrow \calR_{H,\bar v}^G(a_H)\rightarrow \calP_{\bar v}(J_a) \rightarrow \calP_{\bar v}(J_{H,a_H})\rightarrow 1.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}
We have
$$
\dim\Bigl(\calR_{H,\bar v}^G(a_H)\Bigr)=\dim\Bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\Bigr)-\dim\Bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_{H,a_H})\Bigr)=r_{H,\bar v}^G(a_H),
$$
where 
$$
r_{H,\bar v}^G(a_H)=\deg_{\bar v}\bigl(a_H^*\resultant_G^H\bigr),
$$
with the resultant divisor $\resultant_H^G$ on $\frakc_H$ defined in \ref{discrimnant resultant}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By the exact sequence \eqref{RPP local}, we have
$$
\dim\Bigl(\calR_{H,\bar v}^G(a_H)\Bigr)=\dim\Bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_a)\Bigr)-\dim\Bigl(\calP_{\bar v}(J_{H,a_H})\Bigr).
$$
Since the homomorphism $J_{H,a_H}\rightarrow J_a$ is an isomorphism over the generic fiber, we have
$$
c_{\bar v}(a_H)=c_{\bar v}(a),
$$
where $c_{\bar v}(a_H)$ and $c_{\bar v}(a)$ are the Galois invariants appearing in Bezrukavnikov's dimension formula (see Proposition~\ref{dimension Bezru}). Applying that formula to $a$ and $a_H$, we find
$$
\dim\Bigl(\calR_{H,\bar v}^G(a_H)\Bigr)=\deg_{\bar v}(a^*\discrim_G)-\deg_{\bar v}(a_H^*\discrim_H).
$$
It now suffices to invoke \ref{discrimnant resultant} to conclude.
\end{proof}

Now let $a_H\in\frakc_H(\calO_v)$ with image
$$
a\in\frakc(\calO_v)\cap \frakc^\rs(F_v).
$$
With the same definition as above, one obtains a group $\calR_v(a_H)$ defined over $k$. Then we have the formula
$$
\calR_{H,v}^G(a_H)\otimes_k \bar{k} = \prod_{\bar{v}: k_v \rightarrow \bar{k}} \calR_{\bar{v}}(a)
$$
which implies the dimension formula
$$
\dim\bigl(\calR_{H,v}^G(a_H)\bigr)=\deg(k_v/k)\cdot\deg_v\bigl(a_H^* \resultant^H_G\bigr).
$$

Identifying $\calP_v(J_a)$ with the open subset $\calM_v^\reg(a)$ of $\calM_v(a)$ and $\calP_v(J_{H,a_H})$ with the open subset $\calM_{H,v}(a_H)$, and by taking the closure of the graph of the homomorphism $\mu_{a_H}$ in $\calM_v(a)\times \calM_v(a_H)$, one obtains an interesting correspondence between these two affine Springer fibers. We will not use this correspondence in this work.


\section{The Hitchin Fibration}
\label{section : Fibration de Hitchin}

In his seminal paper \cite{H}, Hitchin observed that the cotangent bundle of the moduli space of stable bundles on a curve forms a completely integrable Hamiltonian system. To achieve this, he explicitly constructed a family of commuting functions with respect to the Poisson bracket, in number equal to half the dimension of this cotangent bundle. These functions define a morphism from the cotangent bundle to an affine space whose generic fiber is essentially an abelian variety. This particularly elegant morphism is the Hitchin fibration.

We will adopt a different viewpoint by considering the fibers of the Hitchin fibration as the global analogue of the affine Springer fibers. In particular, we replace the canonical bundle of the curve with a line bundle of very high degree. In doing so, we lose the symplectic form while retaining a fibration that bears the same overall appearance as the original Hitchin fibration.

As observed in \cite{N}, the point counting over a finite field of this generalized Hitchin fibration formally yields the geometric side of the trace formula for the Lie algebra. This continues to serve as our guide in this chapter for studying the geometric properties of the Hitchin fibration. The point counting will be reviewed in Chapter~\ref{section : comptage}.

Our preferred tool for exploring the geometry of the Hitchin fibration
$$f:\calM\longrightarrow \calA$$
is a Picard stack
$$\calP\longrightarrow \calA$$
