acting on $\calM$. This action is based on the construction of the regular centralizer (see Section~\ref{section : centralisateur regulier}). In particular, one shows that there exists an open subset $\calM^\reg$ of $\calM$ (see~\ref{M reg}) on which $\calP$ acts simply transitively and which is dense in each fiber $\calM_a$ (see~\ref{densite globale}). One can analyze the structure of $\calP_a$ in detail thanks to the cameral curve and the theory of N\'eron models. In particular, this allows one to define an open subset $\calA^\diamondsuit$ over which $\calM$ is essentially an abelian scheme.

We will also perform some useful computations for later use, such as the computation of the dimension (see Section~\ref{subsection : dimension}), that of the group of connected components of $\calP_a$ (see Section~\ref{subsection : pi_0(P_a)}), and that of the group of automorphisms of Higgs bundles (see Section~\ref{subsection : Automorphismes}).

The key point of this chapter is the product formula (see~\ref{produit}) which establishes the relationship between the Hitchin fiber and the affine Springer fibers. This formula was proved in \cite{N}. It plays a crucial role in the chapter on point counting (see~\ref{section : comptage}) and is also implicitly used in the proof of the support theorem in Chapter~\ref{support}.

Finally, we establish a link between the Hitchin fibration for $G$ and that for an endoscopic group. There exists a canonical morphism
$$\nu:\calA_H\longrightarrow \calA,$$
and if $a_H\mapsto a$, then there is a canonical homomorphism
$$\mu:\calP_a \longrightarrow \calP_{H,a_H}.$$
However, there is no direct relation between the Hitchin fibers $\calM_a$ and $\calM_{H,a_H}$ but rather a correspondence deduced from $\mu$ by schematic closure. We will not use this correspondence further in the article, though it is likely worth exploring further.

\medskip

Here are the notations that will be used in this chapter. We fix a smooth, proper, and geometrically connected curve $X$ of genus $g$ over a finite field $k$. Let $\bar{k}$ be an algebraic closure of $k$. We denote
$$\bar{X}=X\otimes_k\bar{k}.$$

We denote by $F$ the field of rational functions on $X$. Let $|X|$ be the set of closed points of $X$. Each element $v\in |X|$ defines a valuation
$$v:F^\times\longrightarrow \ZZ.$$ 
We denote by $F_v$ the completion of $F$ with respect to this valuation, by $\calO_v$ its ring of integers, and by $k_v$ its residue field. We write $X_v=\Spec(\calO_v)$ for the formal disk at $v$, and $X_v^\bullet=\Spec(F_v)$ for the punctured formal disk.

% [journal 4] the chapter's standing hypothesis is on the Coxeter number, not on |W|.
Let $\bbG$ be a Chevalley group whose Coxeter number $h$ satisfies the inequality $2h<p$, where $p$ is the characteristic of $k$. Let $G$ be an $X$-group scheme, a quasi-split reductive form of $\GG$, defined by an $\Out(\GG)$-torsor $\rho_G$ over $X$. The group $G$ is then equipped with a pinning $(T,B,\bfx_+)$. We now follow the notations of Section~\ref{Torsion exterieure}. In particular, we have an $X$-scheme of characteristic polynomials $\frakc$, the Chevalley morphism
$$\chi:\frakg\longrightarrow \frakc,$$
where $\frakg=\Lie(G)$, and a finite flat morphism
$$\pi:\frakt\longrightarrow \frakc,$$
which over the open subset $\frakc^\rs$ is a torsor under the finite \'{e}tale group scheme $W$, obtained by twisting $\bbW$ by $\rho_G$.

We fix a line bundle $D$ on $X$ which is the square
$$D={D'}^{\otimes 2}$$
of another line bundle $D'$. In general, we assume that the degree of $D$ is greater than $2g$, where $g$ is the genus of $X$ (in contrast to \cite{H} where $D$ is the canonical bundle). We will indicate, however, the places where this assumption is truly necessary.

Note that $\GG_m$ acts on $\frakg$ and $\frakt$ by homothety, and acts compatibly on $\frakc$. We denote by $\frakg_D$, $\frakt_D$, and $\frakc_D$ the objects twisted by the $\GG_{m}$-torsor associated with the line bundle $D$.

\subsection{Review of ${\rm Bun}_G$}

We consider the stack ${\rm Bun}_G$ which assigns to every $k$-scheme $S$ the groupoid of $G$-torsors on $X\times S$. The stack ${\rm Bun}_G$ is an algebraic stack in the sense of Artin \cf \cite{L-MR} and \cite[Prop.~1]{Heinloth}. The groupoid of $k$-points of ${\rm Bun}_G$ can be expressed as a disjoint union of double cosets
$${\rm Bun}_G(k)=\bigsqcup_{\xi\in {\rm ker}^1(F,G)}
\left[G^\xi(F)\backslash \check\prod_{v\in |X|} \frac{G(F_v)}{G(\calO_v)}\right]
$$
over the set ${\rm ker}^1(F,G)$ of $G$-torsors over $F$ that are locally trivial. Here, $G^\xi$ denotes the strong inner form of $G$ over $F$ corresponding to the class $\xi\in{\rm ker}^1(F,G)$, and the symbol $\check\prod$ denotes a restricted product.

For every $v\in |X|$, as in Section~\ref{subsection : grassmannienne affine}, one has the affine Grassmannian $\calQ_v$ and a morphism
$$\zeta:\calQ_v\longrightarrow {\rm Bun}_G,$$
which consists of gluing the $G$-torsor on $X_v\hat\times S$, equipped with a trivialization on $X_v^\bullet\hat\times S$, with the trivial $G$-torsor on $(X-v)\times S$. The existence of this formal gluing was proved first by Beauville and Laszlo in the case of vector bundles \cf \cite{BL}, and in the general case by Heinloth \cite[Lem.~5]{Heinloth}. On the level of $k$-points, this morphism is the obvious functor
$$G(F_v)/G(\calO_v) \longrightarrow
\left[G(F)\backslash \check\prod_{v\in |X|} \frac{G(F_v)}{G(\calO_v)}\right],
$$
which sends an element $g_v\in G(F_v)/G(\calO_v)$ to the tuple consisting of $g_v$ and the neutral elements of $G(F_{v'})/G(\calO_{v'})$ for all places $v'\neq v$.

\subsection{Construction of the Fibration}\label{Fibration of Hitchin}
Recall the definition of the Hitchin moduli space.

\begin{definition}\label{definition fibration de Hitchin}
The total Hitchin space is the fibered groupoid $\calM$ which assigns to every $k$-scheme $S$ the groupoid $\calM(S)$ of pairs $(E,\phi)$ consisting of a $G$-torsor $E$ on $X\times S$ and a section
$$\phi\in\rmH^0\Bigl(X\times S,\ad(E)\otimes_{\calO_X} D\Bigr),$$
where $\ad(E)$ is the Lie algebra bundle obtained by twisting $\frakg$, endowed with the adjoint action, by the $G$-torsor $E$.
\end{definition}

\begin{numero}
Equivalently, one may say that $\calM(S)$ is the groupoid of maps $h_{E,\phi}$ fitting into the commutative diagram
$$
X\times S \longrightarrow [\frakg_D/ G].
$$
This stack is a vector bundle over the algebraic stack ${\rm Bun}_G$, which classifies the $G$-torsors on $X$, and hence it is itself an algebraic stack locally of finite type.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
The Chevalley characteristic morphism
$$\chi:\frakg\longrightarrow \frakc$$
induces a morphism
$$[\chi]:[\frakg_D/G]\longrightarrow \frakc_D.$$
Thus, one obtains a morphism
$$f:\calM\longrightarrow \calA,$$
where for any $k$-scheme $S$, $\calA(S)$ is the groupoid of maps $a$ fitting into the commutative diagram
$$
a: X\times S\longrightarrow \frakc_D.
$$
Equivalently, $\calA$ is the space of sections of the vector bundle $\frakc_D$ over $X$. It follows that $\calA$ is a finite-dimensional $k$-vector space.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}\label{Kostant Hitchin}
Given a square root $D'$ of $D$, one obtains a section
$$\epsilon_{D'}:\calA\longrightarrow \calM$$
of the morphism $f:\calM\longrightarrow \calA$ using the construction of \ref{epsilon D'}. This section is essentially the same as the ones that Hitchin constructed more explicitly in the case of classical groups. It is called the \emph{Kostant--Hitchin section}.
\end{numero}
% [journal 4.2.5] RESTORED: this numbered paragraph, with formula (4.2.6), was absent.
\begin{numero}\label{comptage global}
Writing $D$ in the form $D=\calO_X(\sum_{v\in|X|}d_v v)$, the set $\frakc_D(k)$ is identified with a finite subset of $\frakc(F)$. By \cite[1.3]{N}, for $a\in\frakc_D(k)$ regular semisimple and anisotropic, the number of $k$-points of the fiber $\calM_a$ is expressed in terms of sums of global orbital integrals
\begin{equation}\label{comptage 4.2.6}
\sum_{\xi\in{\rm ker}^1(F,G)}\ \sum_{\gamma\in\frakg^\xi(F)/\sim,\ \chi(\gamma)=a}\bfO_\gamma(1_D),
\end{equation}
which is part of the formula \cf \ref{2}. In \cite{N} we constructed a geometric interpretation of the stabilization process of this formula \cf \ref{Formule globale de stabilisation}. This process of counting and stabilization will be examined more systematically in Chapter \ref{section : comptage}.

In the remainder of this chapter we shall recall this geometry and study it in more detail.
\end{numero}

\subsection{Symmetries of a Hitchin Fiber}
\label{subsection : symetries Hitchin}

As with affine Springer fibers, the construction of the natural symmetries of a Hitchin fiber is based on Lemma \ref{J}.

\begin{numero}
For every $S$-point $a$ of $\calA$, there is a morphism 
$$h_a: X \times S \longrightarrow [\frakc/\GG_m].$$ 
We denote by $J_a = h_a^* J$ the pull-back of $J$ from $[\frakc/\GG_m]$, and we consider the Picard groupoid $\calP_a(S)$ of $J_a$-torsors over $X \times S$. As $a$ varies, this construction defines a Picard groupoid $\calP$ fibered over $\calA$.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
The homomorphism $\chi^*J \longrightarrow I$ from Lemma 2.2.1 induces, for every $S$-point $(E,\phi)$ over $a$, a homomorphism
$$J_a \longrightarrow \Aut_{X\times S}(E,\phi) = h_{E,\phi}^* I.$$
Consequently, one may twist $(E,\phi)$ by any $J_a$-torsor. This defines an action of the Picard groupoid $\calP_a(S)$ on the groupoid $\calM_a(S)$. Allowing $a$ to vary, one obtains an action of $\calP$ on $\calM$ relative to the base $\calA$.
\end{numero}

As with the affine Springer fibers, we consider the open subset $\calM^\reg$ of $\calM$ whose points are the morphisms $h_{E,\phi}: X \times S \longrightarrow [\frakg_{D}/G]$ that factor through the open subset $[\frakg^\reg_{D}/G]$.

\begin{proposition}\label{M reg}
$\calM^\reg$ is an open subset of $\calM$ with nonempty fibers over $\calA$. Moreover, it is a torsor under the action of $\calP$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For every $a \in \calA(\bar{k})$, the point $[\epsilon]^{D'}(a)$ constructed in \cf\ref{epsilon D'} lies in the regular open subset. This shows that the morphism $\calM^\reg \rightarrow \calA$ has nonempty fibers. It follows from Lemma \ref{gerbe} that $\calM_a^\reg$ is a torsor under $\calP_a$.
\end{proof}

\begin{numero}
The Kostant--Hitchin section \ref{Kostant Hitchin} 
$$\epsilon_{D'}: \calA \longrightarrow \calM$$ 
factors through the open subset $\calM^\reg$ since the Kostant section 
$$\epsilon: \frakc \longrightarrow \frakg$$ 
factors through $\frakg^\reg$.
\end{numero}

% [journal 4.3.5] MISPLACED in the arXiv version: this proposition sat in "Dimensions"
% (journal 4.13); in the journal it closes 4.3. Moved back.
\begin{proposition}\label{P lisse}
The Picard stack $\mathcal{P}$ is smooth over $\mathcal{A}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $J_a$ is a smooth commutative group scheme, the obstruction to deforming a $J_a$-torsor
lies in the group $\operatorname{H}^2(\bar{X},\operatorname{Lie}(J_a))$. This group vanishes because $\bar{X}$
has dimension one.
\end{proof}


% [journal 4.4] moved: in the arXiv version this subsection came AFTER the cameral-curve
% material; in the journal it is 4.4, immediately after "Symmetries of a Hitchin Fiber".
\subsection{The Linear Case}\label{Cas lineaire}

We now analyze the fibers of $\mathcal{M}$ and $\mathcal{P}$ over a point $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$.
Let us begin with the case of the linear group: let $G=\operatorname{GL}(r)$. In this case one can describe
the Hitchin fibers using spectral curves following Hitchin \cite{H} and Beauville--Narasimhan--Ramanan \cite{BNR}.
A similar treatment works for the classical groups (see \cite{H} and \cite{compagnon}).

\begin{numero}
In the case $G=\operatorname{GL}(r)$, the affine space $\mathcal{A}$ is the vector space
$$
\mathcal{A}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^r \operatorname{H}^0(X, D^{\otimes i}).
$$
A point $a=(a_1,\ldots,a_r)\in\mathcal{A}(\bar{k})$ determines a spectral curve $Y_a$ drawn on the total space $\Sigma_D$ of the line bundle $D$.
This curve is given by the equation
$$
t^r-a_1t^{r-1}+\cdots+(-1)^r a_r=0.
$$
We define an open subset $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit\subset \mathcal{A}$ whose geometric points $a\in \mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$ define a reduced spectral curve.
If $a\in \mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, the Hitchin fiber $\mathcal{M}_a$ is the groupoid $\overline{\operatorname{Pic}}(Y_a)$ of torsion-free $\mathcal{O}_{Y_a}$-modules of rank one (see \cite{BNR}).
The fiber $\mathcal{P}_a$ is the groupoid $\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a)$ of invertible $\mathcal{O}_{Y_a}$-modules.
The group $\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a)$ acts on $\overline{\operatorname{Pic}}(Y_a)$ by tensor product, and $\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a)$ appears as an open subset in $\overline{\operatorname{Pic}}(Y_a)$.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
If the spectral curve $Y_a$ is smooth then there is no difference between $\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a)$ and $\overline{\operatorname{Pic}}(Y_a)$,
i.e., $\mathcal{P}_a$ acts simply transitively on $\mathcal{M}_a$. Moreover, in this case, the structure of $\mathcal{P}_a$ is as simple as possible.
The group of connected components of $\mathcal{P}_a$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}$ via the degree map because $Y_a$ is connected.
The neutral component of $\mathcal{P}_a$ is the quotient of the Jacobian of $Y_a$ (which is an abelian variety) by the trivial $\mathbb{G}_m$-action.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
Let $\xi:Y_a^\flat\rightarrow Y_a$ be the normalization of $Y_a$. The pull-back functor induces a homomorphism
$$
\xi^*:\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a)\rightarrow \operatorname{Pic}(Y_a^\flat)
$$
which in turn induces an isomorphism
$$
\pi_0\bigl(\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a)\bigr)\cong \pi_0\bigl(\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a^\flat)\bigr)=\mathbb{Z}^{\pi_0(Y_a^\flat)}.
$$
The kernel of $\xi^*$ is an affine commutative group of dimension
$$
\delta_a=\dim \operatorname{H}^0\Bigl(Y_a,\frac{\xi_*\mathcal{O}_{Y_a^\flat}}{\mathcal{O}_{Y_a}}\Bigr).
$$
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
By construction, $Y_a$ is a curve drawn on a smooth surface so that in particular it has only planar singularities.
According to Altman, Iarrobino, and Kleiman \cite{AIK}, $\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a)$ is then a dense open subset of $\overline{\operatorname{Pic}}(Y_a)$.
\end{numero}

We now generalize the discussion above to a general reductive group.

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
% [journal 4.5] "Courbe camerale". The arXiv version bundled the cameral cover, the
% connectedness proposition and the A^diamond study differently; the journal splits the
% material into 4.5 / 4.6 / 4.7. This is 4.5.
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Cameral Curve}\label{subsection : courbe camerale}

Consider the cartesian diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
\widetilde{X} \ar[d] \ar[r] & \mathfrak{t}_D \ar[d]^{\pi} \\
X\times \mathcal{A} \ar[r] & \mathfrak{c}_D
}
$$
where the bottom morphism sends a pair $(x,a)$ with $x\in X$ and $a\in\mathcal{A}$ to the point $a(x)\in \mathfrak{c}_D$.
The left morphism, obtained by base change from $\pi$, is a finite flat morphism with an action of $W$.

Taking the fiber at each point $a\in\mathcal{A}(\bar{k})$, we obtain the cameral cover $\pi_a:\widetilde{X}_a\rightarrow \bar X$ of Donagi.
In what follows, we restrict to those parameters $a$ for which $\pi_a$ is generically an
% [journal 4.5] the arXiv text said "such that the cameral curve is reduced"; the journal
% condition is that \pi_a be generically an etale cover. Reducedness is then a conclusion (4.5.1).
\'etale cover. These parameters form an open subset of $\mathcal{A}$ described as follows.

Consider the inverse image $U$ of $\mathfrak{c}^\mathrm{rs}_D\subset \mathfrak{c}_D$ in $X\times\mathcal{A}$.
Since the morphism $U\to\mathcal{A}$ is smooth, its image is an open subset of $\mathcal{A}$ which we denote by $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$.
Its $\bar{k}$-points are described as
$$
\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})=\{a\in\mathcal{A}(\bar{k})\mid a(\bar{X})\not\subset \operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}\}.
$$
If $\deg(D)>2g$, the open subset $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ is nonempty. (See \ref{A diamond non vide} for a stronger statement.)

\begin{lemma}
For every geometric point $a\in \mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, the cover
$$
\pi_a:\widetilde{X}_a\longrightarrow X\otimes_k \bar{k}
$$
is generically a $W$-torsor. Moreover, $\widetilde{X}_a$ is a reduced curve.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By definition of $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$, the intersection $U_a$ of $U$ with the fiber $X\times \{a\}$ is a nonempty open subset of $\bar{X}$. By construction, $\pi_a$ is a $W$-torsor over this dense open. Since $\pi_a$ is a finite flat morphism, $\pi_{a*}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}_a}$ is a torsion-free $\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}}$-module. If it is generically reduced, then it is reduced everywhere.
\end{proof}

% [journal 4.5.2] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION.
\begin{numero}\label{J a J1 a}
For every $a\in\calA^\heartsuit(\bar k)$ we have an injective arrow of sheaves for the \'etale topology
$$J_a\rightarrow J^1_a=\big(\pi_{a,*}(\widetilde X_a\times_X T)\big)^W,$$
deduced from \ref{J J1}, whose kernel is a finite sheaf with finite support that is entirely explicit. This arrow makes it possible to control $J_a$, and hence $\calP_a$, by means of the cameral curve.
\end{numero}

The cameral curve $\widetilde{X}_a$ is equipped with a natural family of finite \'etale covers.
Consider a reduction of the torsor $\rho_G$ as in \cf\ \ref{reduction}, from which we adopt the notation.
In particular, we have a finite \'etale Galois cover $\rho:X_\rho\rightarrow X$ with Galois group $\Theta_\rho$ which induces $\rho_{\Out}$ by the change of structure groups $\Theta_\rho\rightarrow \operatorname{Out}(\mathbb{G})$.
Then there is a cartesian diagram
\begin{equation}\label{diagramme deploiement 2}
\xymatrix{
X_\rho\times\mathbb{t} \ar[d] \ar[r]^{\pi} & X_\rho\times \mathbb{c} \ar[d] \\
\mathfrak{t} \ar[r]_{\pi} & \mathfrak{c}.
}
\end{equation}
For every $a\in\mathcal{A}(\bar{k})$, construct the cover
$\pi_{\rho,a}:\widetilde{X}_{\rho,a}\rightarrow \bar{X}$ by forming the cartesian product
\begin{equation}\label{tilde X rho a}
\xymatrix{
\widetilde{X}_{\rho,a} \ar[d] \ar[r] & X_\rho\times \mathbb{t}_D \ar[d]^{\pi_\rho} \\
\bar{X} \ar[r]_a & \mathfrak{c}_D.
}
\end{equation}
Then one obtains a finite \'etale morphism $\widetilde{X}_{\rho,a}\rightarrow \widetilde{X}_a$ which is Galois with Galois group $\Theta_\rho$.

% [journal 4.5.4] this formula was absent from the arXiv version.
One then has the formula
$$J^1_a=\pi_{\rho,a,*}(T)^{W_\rho}$$
following \ref{J pi_rho_G}.

The above discussion applies also if one has a reduction $\bar{\rho}$ of the $\operatorname{Out}(\mathbb{G})$-torsor $\bar{\rho}_G$ on $\bar{X}$.

% [journal 4.5.5] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION.
\begin{proposition}\label{P Deligne-Mumford}
Suppose that $\deg(D)>2g$. Let $\Theta$ denote the image of the homomorphism $\rho_G^\bullet:\pi_1(\bar X,\infty)\rightarrow\Out(\GG)$. Suppose that $\Theta$ is a finite group of order prime to the characteristic. Then for every $a\in\calA^\infty(\bar k)$ one has
$$\rmH^0(\bar X,J_a)=(Z_\GG)^\Theta$$
and
$$\rmH^0(\bar X,\Lie(J_a))=\Lie(Z_\GG)^\Theta.$$
In particular, if over $\bar X$ the center of $G$ contains no split torus, then $\calP_a$ is a Deligne-Mumford Picard stack.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The key point, that the curve $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}$ is connected, is deferred to \ref{connexe}; let us grant it for the moment. We then have
$$\rmH^0(\bar X,J^1_a)=\bbT^\bbW,$$
which is a finite unramified group under the hypothesis that $Z_\GG$ contains no split tori. The group $\rmH^0(\bar X,J_a)$ is a subgroup of it. Using the explicit description of $J\rightarrow J^1$ \cf \ref{J=J'}, and the fact that the section $a$ meets every irreducible component of the discriminant locus, one can show that
$$\rmH^0(\bar X,J_a)=Z_\GG^\Theta.$$
Since this explicit description will not be used in the sequel, we leave to the reader the task of reconstructing the details.
\end{proof}

% [journal 4.5.6-4.5.7] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION. This is the rigidifier
% referred to from the new 4.12 and from 7.8.
\begin{numero}\label{rigidificateur}
It is not difficult to introduce a rigidifier in order to benefit from representability. Let $\infty\in X$ be a fixed point and let $\calA^\infty$ be the open subset of $\calA$ consisting of the points $a$ which are regular semisimple at $\infty$. For every $a\in\calA^\infty$, write $\calP_a^\infty$ for the Picard stack classifying $J_a$-torsors equipped with a trivialization at $\infty$.
\end{numero}

\begin{proposition}\label{P infty representable}
The functor $\calP^\infty$ is representable by a smooth group scheme locally of finite type over $\calA^\infty$. For every $a\in\calA^\infty$, the torus $J_{a,\infty}$ acts on $\calP^\infty$ by modifying the rigidification, and induces a canonical isomorphism between the quotient stack $[\calP_a^\infty/J_{a,\infty}]$ and $\calP_a$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The first assertion follows from the representability of the Picard scheme of the \'etale cover $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}$ of the cameral curve. The second assertion is immediate.
\end{proof}

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
% [journal 4.6] "Courbe camerale connexe". In the arXiv version this was part of the
% single subsection "The Open Subset A^heartsuit and the Cameral Cover", and the
% connectedness was deduced from the Fulton-Hansen theorem [Fulton-Lazarsfeld, Thm 4.1].
% The journal replaces that by a theorem of DEBARRE \cite[th. 1.4]{Deb} generalising
% Bertini, and rewrites the proof accordingly. Both statement and argument differ.
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Connected Cameral Curve}\label{subsection : courbe camerale connexe}

Under the hypothesis $\deg(D)>2g$ one can show that the cameral curve $\widetilde X_a$ is connected. But what we shall need in the sequel is a slightly stronger connectedness statement. Consider a connected finite \'etale Galois cover $\rho:\bar X_\rho\rightarrow \bar X$ which trivializes the torsor $\rho_G$. We have then constructed a cover
$$\pi_{\rho,a}:\widetilde X_{\rho,a}\rightarrow \bar X$$
as in \ref{tilde X rho a}.

\begin{proposition}\label{connexe}
Suppose that $\deg(D)>2g$. Then for every $a\in\calA^\heartsuit(\bar k)$ the curves $\widetilde X_a$ and $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}$ are connected.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
After possibly replacing $\bar X$ by $\bar X_\rho$, it suffices to prove that the cameral curve $\widetilde X_a$ is connected in the case where $G$ is split. In that case we appeal to the following theorem, due to Debarre \cite[th.~1.4]{Deb}, which generalizes a theorem of Bertini.

\begin{theoreme}\label{Debarre}
Let $M$ be an irreducible variety and let $m:M\rightarrow \bbP$ be a morphism from $M$ into a product of projective spaces $\bbP=\bbP^{n_1}\times\cdots\times\bbP^{n_r}$. In each $\bbP^{n_i}$ we are given a linear subspace $L_i\subset \bbP^{n_i}$ such that for every subset $I\subset\{1,\ldots,r\}$ one has
$$\dim(p_I(m(M)))>\sum_{i\in I}{\rm codim}(L_i),$$
% [journal, p. 64] the journal prints the index set as {1,...,n}; since the product has
% r factors this must read {1,...,r}. Corrected here.
where $p_I$ is the projection of $\bbP$ onto $\prod_{i\in I}\bbP^{n_i}$. Suppose also that $m$ is proper over an open subset $V$ of $\bbP$ and that $L=L_1\times\cdots\times L_r$ is contained in $V$. Then $m^{-1}(L)$ is connected.
\end{theoreme}

Here is how to apply this result to our particular case. Since $G$ is split, $\frakc_D$, as a scheme, is a fibered product over $\bar X$ of line bundles $D^{\otimes e_i}$. Each of these line bundles $D^{\otimes e_i}$ can be compactified into a projective line bundle
$$\bar D^{\otimes e_i}={\rm Proj}_{\bar X}\big({\rm Sym}_{\calO_{\bar X}}(D^{\otimes -e_i}\oplus \calO_{X_\rho})\big),$$
which is a projective $\bar k$-surface. We write $Z_i=\bar D^{e_i}-D^{e_i}$ for the divisor at infinity. The invertible bundle $\calO(1)$ attached to this ${\rm Proj}$ is very ample by the hypothesis $\deg(D)>2g$, and induces a projective embedding
$$\bar D^{\otimes e_i}\rightarrow \bbP^{n_i}$$
of this surface.

The locally closed subscheme $\prod_{i=1}^r D^{\otimes e_i}$ of $\bbP=\prod_{i=1}^r \bbP^{n_i}$ is a closed subscheme of $V$, where
$$V=\prod_{i=1}^r \bbP^{n_i}-\bigcup_{i=1}^r\Big(Z_i\times\prod_{j\not=i}\bar D^{\otimes e_j}\Big).$$
The same holds for $\frakc_D$, which is a closed subscheme of $\prod_{i=1}^r D^{\otimes e_i}$. What plays the role of the irreducible variety $M$ in Debarre's theorem is $\frakt_D$, which is finite over $\frakc_D$, so that $M$ has dimension $r+1$ and the composite morphism $m:M\rightarrow V$ is a proper morphism.

The component $a_i\in\rmH^0(\bar X,D^{\otimes e_i})$ of $a$ defines a hyperplane $L_i$ of $\bbP^{n_i}$, since
$$(a_i,1)\in \rmH^0(\bbP^{n_i},\calO(1))=\rmH^0(\bar X,D^{\otimes e_i})\oplus \rmH^0(\bar X,\calO_{\bar X}),$$
whose intersection with the surface $\bar D^{\otimes e_i}$ does not meet the divisor at infinity $Z_i$; in other words, it is contained in the open surface $D^{\otimes e_i}$. Since $L_i\cap Z_i=\emptyset$, the product $L=\prod_{i=1}^r L_i$ is contained in the open subset $V$.

Since $\widetilde X_a=m^{-1}(L)$, it only remains to check that for every subset $I\subset\{1,\ldots,r\}$ one has
$$\dim(p_I(m(M)))>\sum_{i\in I}{\rm codim}(L_i).$$
But one checks immediately that the dimension of $p_I(m(M))$ equals $\sharp I+1$, which completes the proof.
\end{proof}

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
% [journal 4.7] "Courbe camerale connexe et lisse" (was: "The Open Subset A^diamond").
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Connected and Smooth Cameral Curve}
\label{subsection : A diamond}

Let us begin by studying the open subset $\mathcal{A}^\diamondsuit$ where the fibers of $\mathcal{M}_a$
are as simple as possible. This open set is defined as follows.
A point $a\in \mathcal{A}(\bar{k})$ belongs to this open if the section
$$
h_a:\bar{X}\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}_D
$$
meets the divisor $\operatorname{discrim}_{D}$ transversely. Here $\operatorname{discrim}_{D}$ denotes
the divisor in $\mathfrak{c}_D$ obtained by twisting the divisor $\operatorname{discrim}\subset \mathfrak{c}$
by the $\mathbb{G}_m$-torsor $L_D$ associated to the line bundle $D$.

\begin{proposition} \label{A diamond non vide}
If $\deg(D)>2g$, then the open subset $\mathcal{A}^\diamondsuit$ is nonempty.
\end{proposition}

The proof is completely similar to the Bertini theorem due to Zariski. We begin by proving a lemma.

\begin{lemma}\label{deg(D)>2g}
Assume $\deg(D)>2g$, where $g$ is the genus of $X$. For every $x\in \bar{X}(\bar{k})$
defined by a maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}_x$, the map
$$
\operatorname{H}^0(\bar{X},\mathfrak{c})\longrightarrow \mathfrak{c}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}}}\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}}/\mathfrak{m}_x^2
$$
is surjective. Here the vector bundle $\mathfrak{c}$ is regarded as a locally free $\mathcal{O}_X$-module of rank $r$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By passing to the finite \'etale cover $\rho: X_\rho\rightarrow X$, the bundle $\mathfrak{c}$ becomes isomorphic
to a direct sum
$$
\rho^* \mathfrak{c} =\bigoplus_{i=1}^r \rho^* D^{\otimes e_i},
$$
where the $e_i$ are defined in \ref{Chevalley} and in particular are integers at least $1$.
Since $\mathfrak{c}$ is a direct summand of $\rho_* \rho^* \mathfrak{c}$, it suffices to prove the surjectivity of
$$
\operatorname{H}^0(\bar{X}',\rho^*\mathfrak{c})\longrightarrow \rho^*\mathfrak{c}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}}}\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}}/\mathfrak{m}_x^2.
$$
It is enough to prove this surjectivity for each direct factor $\rho^*D^{\otimes e_i}$.

One may assume that $X_\rho$ is geometrically connected. Let $g'$ be its genus. Then $2g'-2=n(2g-2)$ where $n$ is the degree of $\rho$.
The surjectivity follows from the inequality
$$
\deg(D^{\otimes e_i})>2ng=(2g'-2)+2n.
$$
This proves the lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{A diamond non vide}]
In $\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}$ there is a smooth open subset
$$
{\operatorname{discrim}_{D}}-\operatorname{discrim}_{D}^{\mathrm{sing}},
$$
which is the complement of a closed subset $\operatorname{discrim}_{D}^{\mathrm{sing}}$ of codimension $2$ in $\mathfrak{c}_D$.

Consider the subscheme $Z_1$ of
$$
\Bigl({\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}}-\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}^{\mathrm{sing}}\Bigr)\times \mathcal{A}
$$
consisting of pairs $(c,a)$ such that the section $a(\bar{X})$ passes through the point $c$
and intersects the divisor ${\operatorname{discrim}_{G}}$ at that point with multiplicity at least $2$.
By the lemma above,
$$
\dim(Z_1)\le \dim(\mathcal{A})-1,
$$
so that the projection $Z_1\rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ is not surjective.

Consider the subscheme $Z_2$ of $\operatorname{discrim}_{G}^{\mathrm{sing}}\times \mathcal{A}$ consisting of pairs $(c,a)$
such that the section $a(\bar{X})$ passes through $c$. Again by the lemma,
$$
\dim(Z_2)\le \dim(\mathcal{A})-1,
$$
so that the union of the images of $Z_1$ and $Z_2$ is contained in a proper closed subscheme of $\mathcal{A}$.
Hence, there exists a point $a\in\mathcal{A}^{\heartsuit}$ such that the section $a(\bar{X})$
does not meet the singular locus $\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}^{\mathrm{sing}}$ of the discriminant and meets
the smooth part of this divisor transversely.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{cameral lisse}
A point $a\in\mathcal{A}(\bar{k})$ belongs to the open subset $\mathcal{A}^\diamondsuit(\bar{k})$
if and only if the cameral curve $\widetilde{X}_a$ is smooth.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Suppose that $a\in\mathcal{A}^\diamondsuit(\bar{k})$, \emph{i.e.}, the section $a(\bar{X})\subset \mathfrak{c}_D$
meets the divisor $\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}$ transversely. We show that the inverse image of this section on the
cover $X_\rho\times \mathbb{t}_D$ is smooth. Outside the divisor $\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}$, this cover is \'etale so that there is nothing to verify.
The condition that $a(\bar{X})\subset \mathfrak{c}_D$ meets $\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}$ transversely
implies in particular that it does not meet the singular locus $\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}^{\mathrm{sing}}$ of $\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}$.
A pair $(v,x)\in \mathfrak{t}_D(\bar{k})$ consists of a point $v\in X(\bar{k})$ and a point $x$ in the fiber
of $\mathfrak{t}_D$ over $v$. Over $v$, the group $G$ is split so that one may speak of the root hyperplanes in the fiber of
$\mathfrak{t}_D$ over $v$. If $(v,x)\in \mathfrak{t}_D(\bar{k})$ lies above a point where $a(\bar{X})$ intersects
$$
\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}-\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}^{\mathrm{sing}},
$$
then $x$ belongs to a unique root hyperplane. One may then reduce to the case of a semisimple group of rank one.
In that case a direct calculation shows that the formal completion of $\widetilde{X}_a$ at $(v,x)$ is of the form
$$
\bar{k}[[\epsilon_v]][t]/(t^2-\epsilon_v^m),
$$
where $\epsilon_v$ is a uniformizer of $\bar{X}$ at the point $v$ and $m$ is the intersection multiplicity
of $a(\bar{X})$ with $\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}$ at that point. In the transverse case $m=1$, this implies that
$\widetilde{X}_a$ is smooth at $(\tilde{v},x)$.

Now suppose that $a\notin \mathcal{A}^\diamondsuit(\bar{k})$. If $a(\bar{X})$ meets the smooth part of $\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}$
with multiplicity greater than one, the calculation above shows that $\widetilde{X}_a$ is not smooth.
Now suppose that $a(\bar{X})$ meets the locus $\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}^{\mathrm{sing}}$ at a point $v\in \bar{X}$.
Assume that $\widetilde{X}_a$ is smooth at the point $(v,x)\in \mathfrak{t}_D(\bar{k})$ above $v$.
Then the point $x$ belongs to at least two distinct root hyperplanes so that the local monodromy group $\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)$ (see \ref{pi_a^bullet})
contains two distinct involutions. This is impossible because, under the assumption that the characteristic of $k$
does not divide the order of $\mathbb{W}$, the local monodromy group $\pi_a^\bullet(I_v)$ is cyclic.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{irreductible}
% [journal 4.7] the hypothesis that the cover SPLITS G was dropped, leaving the cover
% in the corollary below unrelated to G.
Let $\bar{X}_{\bar{\rho}}\rightarrow \bar{X}$ be a connected finite \'etale Galois cover which splits $G$.
Suppose that $\deg(D)>2g$. Then for every $a\in \mathcal{A}^\diamondsuit(\bar{k})$, the curve $\widetilde{X}_{\bar{\rho},a}$ is irreducible.
\end{corollary}

% [journal 4.7.5] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION.
\begin{corollary}\label{image pi a diamond}
If $a\in\calA^\diamondsuit(\bar k)$, then the image of $\pi_a^\bullet$ is $\bbW\rtimes\Theta$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{connexe}, $\widetilde{X}_{\rho,a}$ is connected. Since $\widetilde{X}_a$ is smooth,
the \'etale cover $\widetilde{X}_{\rho,a}$ is also smooth. Therefore, it is irreducible.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
A $\bar k$-\emph{abelian stack} is the quotient of a $\bar k$-abelian variety by the trivial action of a diagonalizable group.
\end{definition}

A typical example of an abelian stack is the stack classifying line bundles of degree zero on a smooth,
projective, geometrically connected curve. If this curve is equipped with an action of a finite group whose order
is prime to the characteristic, the associated Prym stacks are abelian stacks.

\begin{proposition}\label{A lozenge}
For every $a\in \mathcal{A}^\diamondsuit(\bar{k})$, the regular locus $\mathcal{M}^\mathrm{reg}_a$ equals the whole fiber $\mathcal{M}_a$,
so that $\mathcal{M}_a$ is a torsor under $\mathcal{P}_a$. The neutral component of $\mathcal{P}_a$ is an abelian stack.
\end{proposition}

We refer to \cite[Proposition~4.2]{N} for the proof of the first assertion. The proof of the second assertion is postponed until the end of Section~\ref{subsection : Modele de Neron global}.

% [journal 4.7.8] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION.
\begin{numero}
For every $a\in\calA^\heartsuit(\bar k)$ we have seen that the inertia group of $\calP_a$ is identified with $Z_\GG$. We shall see in \ref{pi 0 A diamond} that for $a\in\calA^\diamondsuit(\bar k)$ the group of connected components $\pi_0(\calP_a)$ is identified with $Z_{\hat\GG}$. One may observe that if $G_1$ and $G_2$ are dual to one another and if $a\in\calA^\diamondsuit_{G_1}(\bar k)=\calA^\diamondsuit_{G_2}(\bar k)$, then the inertia group of $\calP_{G_1,a}$ is isomorphic to the group of connected components of $\calP_{G_2,a}$, and vice versa.
\end{numero}

\subsection{Global N\'eron Model}\label{subsection : Modele de Neron global}

In this section we fix a point $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$ and denote by $U$
the inverse image of the open set $\mathfrak{c}^\mathrm{rs}_D$ under the morphism $a:\bar X\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}^\mathrm{rs}_D$.

As in the local case (\S\ref{subsection : Modele de Neron}), the structure of the Picard stack $\mathcal{P}_a$ of $J_a$-torsors on $\bar{X}$ can be analyzed using the N\'eron model $J_a^\flat$ of $J_a$.
This is a smooth group scheme of finite type over $\bar{X}$ equipped with a homomorphism $J_a\rightarrow J_a^\flat$
that is an isomorphism over $U$. It is further characterized by the following property: for every smooth group scheme $J'$ of finite type on $\bar{X}$ with a homomorphism $J_a\rightarrow J'$ that is an isomorphism on $U$, there exists a unique homomorphism $J'\rightarrow J_a^\flat$ making the obvious triangle commute.
The existence of this N\'eron model is a result of Bosch, L\"utkebohmert and Raynaud \cite{BLR} (they call it the finite type N\'eron model to distinguish it from the locally finite type local N\'eron model).

This global N\'eron model is obtained from the local N\'eron models (see \S\ref{subsection : Modele de Neron}) as follows.
For every $v\in \bar{X}-U$, let $\bar{X}_v$ be the formal completion of $\bar{X}$ at $v$ and let $\bar{X}_v^\bullet=\bar{X}_v-\{v\}$.
Consider the N\'eron model of the torus $J_a|_{\bar{X}_v^\bullet}$. By gluing these N\'eron models at the various points $v\in \bar{X}-U$ with the torus $J_a|_U$, one obtains a smooth commutative group scheme $J_a^\flat$ over $\bar{X}$ along with a homomorphism $J_a\rightarrow J_a^\flat$.

As in \ref{J a flat}, $J_a^\flat$ can be expressed in terms of the normalization $\widetilde X_a^\flat$ of the cameral curve $\widetilde X_a$. The $W$-action on $\widetilde X_a$ induces an action on $\widetilde X_a^\flat$. Let $\pi_a^\flat:\widetilde X_a^\flat\rightarrow \bar X$ be the projection. The following is the global consequence of the local statement \ref{J a flat}.

\begin{corollary}\label{J a flat global}
$J_a^\flat$ is identified with the subgroup of fixed points under the diagonal action of $W$ in
$$
\prod_{\widetilde X_a^\flat/\bar X} \Bigl(T\times_{\bar X}\widetilde X_a^\flat\Bigr).
$$
\end{corollary}

A useful variant is as follows. Consider a reduction $\bar \rho:\bar X_{\bar \rho}\rightarrow \bar X$ of $\bar\rho_G$
with Galois group $\Theta_{\bar\rho}$. For every $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, there is a finite flat cover
$$
\pi_{\bar\rho,a}:\widetilde X_{\bar\rho,a}\rightarrow \bar X
$$
defined as in (\ref{tilde X rho a}), which is generically \'etale Galois with group $\mathbb{W}\rtimes \Theta_{\bar\rho}$.
Let $\widetilde X_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat$ be the normalization of $\widetilde X_{\bar\rho,a}$, which is also equipped with an action of $\mathbb{W}\rtimes \Theta_{\bar\rho}$.
Denote by $\pi_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat$ its projection to $\bar{X}$. Then one has
$$
J_a^\flat=\prod_{\widetilde X_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat/\bar X}
\Bigl(\mathbb{T}\times \widetilde X_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat\Bigr)^{\mathbb{W}\rtimes \Theta_{\bar\rho}}.
$$

Consider the Picard groupoid $\mathcal{P}_a^\flat$ of $J_a^\flat$-torsors.
The homomorphism of group schemes $J_a\rightarrow J_a^\flat$ induces a homomorphism of Picard groupoids $\mathcal{P}_a\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_a^\flat$.
This homomorphism essentially reflects the general structure of an algebraic group over an algebraically closed field
as an extension of an abelian variety by an affine algebraic group (see \cite{Ros}). The proof below is inspired by an argument of Raynaud \cite{Ray}.

\begin{proposition}\label{P a flat}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The homomorphism $\mathcal{P}_a(\overline{k})\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_a^\flat(\overline{k})$ is essentially surjective.

\item The neutral component $(\mathcal{P}_a^\flat)^0$ of $\mathcal{P}_a^\flat$ is an abelian stack.

\item The kernel $\mathcal{R}_a$ of $\mathcal{P}_a\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_a^\flat$ is a product of affine algebraic groups of finite type $\mathcal{R}_v(a)$, as defined in Lemma~\ref{R_v(a)}.
These are trivial except at finitely many points $v\in |\bar X|$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\begin{enumerate}
\item By the very construction of the N\'eron models, the homomorphism $J_a\rightarrow J_a^\flat$
is injective as a homomorphism between sheaves of abelian groups for the \'etale topology on $\bar X$.
Consider the short exact sequence
\begin{equation}\label{suite courte}
1\rightarrow J_a \rightarrow J_a^\flat \rightarrow J_a^\flat/J_a \rightarrow 1,
\end{equation}
where the quotient $J_a^\flat/J_a$ is supported on the finite closed subset $\bar X-U$.
The long exact cohomology sequence shows that, since $\operatorname{H}^1(\bar X,J_a^\flat/J_a)=0$, the map
$$
\operatorname{H}^1(\bar X,J_a)\longrightarrow \operatorname{H}^1(\bar X,J_a^\flat)
$$
is surjective.

\item Let $\widetilde X_a$ be the inverse image of the cover $\mathfrak{t}_D\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}_D$ via $h_a:\bar X\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}_D$.
Let $\widetilde X_a^\flat$ be the normalization of $\widetilde X_a$.
By Proposition~\ref{J a flat global}, the N\'eron model $J_a^\flat$ depends only on the cover $\widetilde X_a^\flat$.
More precisely, $J_a^\flat$ consists of the fixed points under the diagonal action of $W$ in the Weil restriction
$$
\prod_{\widetilde X_a^\flat/\bar X}\Bigl(T\times_{\bar X}\widetilde X_a^\flat\Bigr).
$$
It follows that up to isogeny, $\mathcal{P}_a^\flat$ is a factor of the Picard groupoid of $T$-torsors on $\widetilde X_a^\flat$,
which is isomorphic to the product of $r$ copies of $\operatorname{Pic}(\widetilde X_a^\flat)$.
Since $\widetilde X_a^\flat$ is a smooth projective curve (possibly disconnected), the neutral component
of $\operatorname{Pic}(\widetilde X_a^\flat)$ is the quotient of a product of Jacobians by a product of $\mathbb{G}_m$
acting trivially.

\item The last assertion also follows from the long exact sequence in cohomology arising from the short exact sequence~(\ref{suite courte}).
Since the kernel is defined as the category of $J_a$-torsors equipped with a trivialization of the induced $J_a^\flat$-torsor,
one need not worry about the $\operatorname{H}^0$ in the long exact sequence.
\end{enumerate}
This completes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}
Returning now to the second assertion of Proposition~\ref{A lozenge}:
Let $a\in\mathcal{A}^\diamondsuit(\bar{k})$. Then the cameral curve $\widetilde X_a$ is smooth, so the cokernel
of the homomorphism $J_a\rightarrow J_a^\flat$ is a finite group sheaf supported on a finite subscheme of $\bar X$.
It follows that $\mathcal{P}_a\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_a^\flat$ is an isogeny of Picard groupoids (i.e., a homomorphism that is essentially surjective
with finite kernel). Since $\mathcal{P}_a^\flat$ is an abelian stack, the same holds for $\mathcal{P}_a$.
\end{proof}

% [journal 4.8.4] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION.
\begin{lemme}\label{P champ abelien}
Let $C_{\tilde a}$ be a connected component of $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}$ and let $W_{\tilde a}$ be the subgroup of elements of $\bbW$ stabilizing $C_{\tilde a}$. If $\bbT^{W_{\tilde a}}$ is finite and unramified, then $\calP_a$ is a Deligne-Mumford abelian stack.
\end{lemme}

\subsection{Invariant $\delta_a$}

% [journal 4.9.1] the journal numbers this opening paragraph (which contains the numbered
% display (4.9.2)); leaving it unnumbered shifted every later number in 4.9.
\begin{numero}\label{invariant delta}
An important numerical invariant that one may attach to $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar k)$ is the delta invariant. For every $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, consider the kernel
\begin{equation}\label{R_a}
\mathcal{R}_a:=\ker\Bigl[\mathcal{P}_a \longrightarrow \mathcal{P}_a^\flat\Bigr].
\end{equation}
It classifies the $J_a$-torsors on $X$ whose induced $J_a^\flat$-torsor is trivialized.
This is an affine algebraic group of finite type which decomposes as a product
$$
\mathcal{R}_a=\prod_{v\in \bar X-U}\mathcal{R}_v(a),
$$
where $\mathcal{R}_v(a)$ is the group defined in \ref{R_v(a)}.

We define the \emph{invariant $\delta_a$} as the dimension of $\mathcal{R}_a$, that is,
$$
\delta_a:=\dim(\mathcal{R}_a)=\sum_{v\in \bar X-U}\delta_v(a).
$$
Combining Proposition~\ref{P a flat} and the dimension formula for the local symmetry group (see \ref{delta local}) yields the following formula for the global invariant $\delta$.

\end{numero}

% [journal 4.9.3] RESTORED: this numbered paragraph was absent, although the phrase
% "the dimension of the affine part of P_a" that it licenses is used repeatedly later.
\begin{numero}\label{partie affine P_a}
Recall that, on choosing a rigidifier \cf \ref{rigidificateur}, one has defined a smooth commutative algebraic group $\tilde{\calP}^0_a$ surjecting onto the neutral component $\calP^0_a$. By Chevalley's structure theorem one has a canonical exact sequence
$$1\rightarrow \tilde{\calR}_a\rightarrow \tilde{\calP}^0_a\rightarrow A_a\rightarrow 1,$$
where $A_a$ is an abelian variety. Under the hypothesis of \ref{P champ abelien} this is in fact an \'etale isogeny. There follows the formula
$$\dim(A_a)=d-\delta_a.$$
In this case, and in this case only, is it justified to call $\delta_a$ the dimension of the affine part of $\calP_a$.
\end{numero}

\begin{corollary}\label{delta global}
For every $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, the invariant $\delta_a$ defined above equals
$$
\delta_a=\dim\operatorname{H}^0\Bigl(\bar{X},\,\mathfrak{t}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}}}\Bigl(\frac{\pi_{a*}^\flat \mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}_a^\flat}}{\pi_{a*}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}_a}}\Bigr)\Bigr)^W.
$$
\end{corollary}

Again, there is another formula that expresses the global invariant $\delta$ in terms of the discriminant corrected by local monodromy invariants, as in Bezrukavnikov's formula.

Recall that the discriminant $\operatorname{discrim}_G$ is a homogeneous polynomial of degree $\sharp\,\Phi$ on $\mathfrak{c}$, where $\sharp\,\Phi$ is the number of roots in $\Phi$. Hence, for every $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, $a^*\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}$ is a divisor linearly equivalent to $D^{\otimes (\sharp\Phi)}$, so that
$$
\deg\bigl(a^*\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}\bigr)=\sharp\,\Phi\deg(D).
$$
Write
$$
a^*\operatorname{discrim}_{G,D}=d_1v_1+\cdots+d_nv_n,
$$
where $v_1,\ldots,v_n$ are pairwise distinct points of $\bar X$ and $d_i$ is the multiplicity at $v_i$.
For every $i=1,\ldots,n$, denote by $c_i$ the drop in the toric rank of $J_a^\flat$ at the point $v_i$.
The following proposition is an immediate consequence of \ref{dimension Bezru}.

\begin{proposition}\label{dimension Bezru globale}
We have the equality
$$
2\delta_a=\sum_{i=1}^n (d_i-c_i)=\sharp\,\Phi\deg(D)-\sum_{i=1}^n c_i.
$$
\end{proposition}

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
% [journal 4.10] RE-TRANSLATED FROM THE JOURNAL (pp. 71-73).
% The arXiv version defined W_atilde and I_atilde via a splitting rho-bar of the
% Out(G)-torsor and the normalization of the cameral cover. The journal defines them as
% the image of pi_atilde^bullet and the image of ker(pi_1(U,infty) -> pi_1(Xbar,infty)),
% after fixing the open A^infty and the maximal etale open U, via the diagram (4.10.1).
% Proposition 4.10.3 accordingly has ambient group W x| Out(G), not W x| Theta_rhobar.
% The arXiv text is preserved at ch4/OLD_410_arxiv.tex.
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{The Group $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_a)$}\label{subsection : pi_0(P_a)}

In this paragraph we shall describe the group of connected components of $\calP_a$ in the spirit of Tate-Nakayama duality. To do so it is necessary to make a certain number of choices and to fix some notation.

Fix a point $\infty\in X(\bar k)$. Consider the open subset $\calA^\infty$ of $\calA\otimes_k\bar k$ consisting of the points $a\in\calA(\bar k)$ such that $a(\infty)\in\bar{\frakc}^\rs_D$. It is an open subscheme of $\calA^\heartsuit\otimes_k\bar k$. If $\infty$ is defined over $k$, the open subset $\calA^\infty$ is also defined over $k$.

Fix a point $a\in\calA^\infty(\bar k)$. Let $U$ be the maximal open subset of $\bar X$ above which the cameral cover $\widetilde X_a\rightarrow\bar X$ is \'etale; by construction $\infty\in U$. Suppose that $G$ is defined by a continuous homomorphism
$$\rho_G^\bullet:\pi_1(X,\infty)\rightarrow\Out(\GG).$$
Having chosen a geometric point $\tilde\infty$ of the cameral curve $\widetilde X_a$ above $\infty$, we have, as in \ref{pi a J a 2}, a continuous homomorphism $\pi_{\tilde a}^\bullet$ fitting into a commutative diagram
\begin{equation}\label{diagramme 4.10.1}
\xymatrix{
\pi_1(U,\infty) \ar[d] \ar[r]^-{\pi_{\tilde a}^\bullet} & \bbW\rtimes\Out(\GG) \ar[d] \\
\pi_1(\bar X,\infty) \ar[r]_-{\rho_G^\bullet} & \Out(\GG)
}
\end{equation}
where $\tilde a=(a,\tilde\infty)$. We write $W_{\tilde a}$ for the image of $\pi_{\tilde a}^\bullet$ in $\bbW\rtimes\Out(\GG)$, and $I_{\tilde a}$ for the image of the kernel of $\pi_1(U,\infty)\rightarrow\pi_1(\bar X,\infty)$. The commutativity of the diagram above implies $I_{\tilde a}\subset\bbW$.

Let now $J_a^0$ be the open subgroup scheme of neutral components of $J_a$. Consider the Picard stack $\calP'_a$ of $J_a^0$-torsors on $\bar X$. The homomorphism of sheaves of groups $J_a^0\rightarrow J_a$ induces a homomorphism of Picard stacks $\calP'_a\rightarrow\calP_a$.
\begin{lemma}\label{P'_a P_a}
The homomorphism $\calP'_a\rightarrow\calP_a$ is surjective with finite kernel. The same holds for the homomorphism $\pi_0(\calP'_a)\rightarrow\pi_0(\calP_a)$ deduced from it.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We have a short exact sequence of sheaves
$$0\rightarrow J_a^0\rightarrow J_a\rightarrow \pi_0(J_a)\rightarrow 0,$$
where $\pi_0(J_a)$ is a sheaf with finite support whose fiber at a point $v\in\bar X$ is the group $\pi_0(J_{a,v})$ of connected components of the fiber $J_{a,v}$ of $J_a$. From it we deduce a long exact sequence
$$\rmH^0(\bar X,\pi_0(J_a))\rightarrow \rmH^1(\bar X,J_a^0)\rightarrow \rmH^1(\bar X,J_a)\rightarrow \rmH^1(\bar X,\pi_0(J_a))=0.$$
The vanishing of the last term results from the fact that $\pi_0(J_a)$ is supported on a scheme of dimension zero. We deduce the surjectivity of $\calP'_a\rightarrow\calP_a$, with kernel $\rmH^0(\bar X,\pi_0(J_a))$. The finiteness of this kernel comes from the finiteness of the fibers of $\pi_0(J_a)$. The assertion on $\pi_0(\calP'_a)\rightarrow\pi_0(\calP_a)$ follows immediately.
\end{proof}

Instead of the abelian groups $\pi_0(\calP'_a)$ and $\pi_0(\calP_a)$, it is more convenient to describe the dual diagonalizable groups. Dually, one has an inclusion of diagonalizable groups
$$\pi_0(\calP_a)^*\subset\pi_0(\calP'_a)^*,$$
where we have written
$$\pi_0(\calP_a)^*=\Spec(\Ql[\pi_0(\calP_a)]),\qquad \pi_0(\calP'_a)^*=\Spec(\Ql[\pi_0(\calP'_a)]).$$
We have used the exponent $(\_)^*$ to denote the duality between finitely generated abelian groups and diagonalizable groups of finite type over $\Ql$.

\begin{proposition}\label{pi 0 global}
For every $\tilde a=(a,\tilde\infty)$ as above, one has isomorphisms of diagonalizable groups
$$\pi_0(\calP'_a)^*=\hat\bbT^{W_{\tilde a}}$$
and
$$\pi_0(\calP_a)^*=\hat\bbT(I_{\tilde a},W_{\tilde a}),$$
where $\hat\bbT(I_{\tilde a},W_{\tilde a})$ is the subgroup of $\hat\bbT^{W_{\tilde a}}$ consisting of the elements $\kappa$ such that $W_{\tilde a}\subset(\bbW\rtimes\Out(\GG))_\kappa$ and $I_{\tilde a}\subset\bbW_\HH$, where $\bbW_\HH$ is the Weyl group of the neutral component of the centralizer of $\kappa$ in $\hat\GG$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By \cite[Cor.~6.7]{N} one has a canonical isomorphism
$$(\bbX_*)_{W_{\tilde a}}\longrightarrow \pi_0(\calP'_a)$$
from the group of $W_{\tilde a}$-coinvariants of $\bbX_*=\Hom(\GG_m,\bbT)$ onto the group of connected components of $\calP'_a$. This is essentially a particular case of a lemma of Kottwitz \cite[Lemma~2.2]{K-Iso1}. Dually, one has an isomorphism of diagonalizable groups
$$\pi_0(\calP'_a)^*=\hat\bbT^{W_{\tilde a}},$$
where $\hat\bbT$ is the $\Ql$-dual torus of $\bbT$.

Write $U=a^{-1}(\frakc^\rs_D)$. As in the proof of \ref{P'_a P_a}, we have an exact sequence
$$\rmH^0(\bar X,\pi_0(J_a))\rightarrow\pi_0(\calP'_a)\rightarrow\pi_0(\calP_a)\rightarrow 0,$$
where $\rmH^0(\bar X,J_a/J_a^0)=\bigoplus_{v\in\bar X-U}\pi_0(J_{a,v})$, with $\pi_0(J_{a,v})$ the group of connected components of the fiber of $J_a$ at $v$. For every $v\in\bar X-U$ one has an analogous local exact sequence
$$\pi_0(J_{a,v})\rightarrow\pi_0(\calP_v(J_a^0))\rightarrow\pi_0(\calP_v(J_a))\rightarrow 0$$
compatible with the global one. Consider the dual sequences of diagonalizable groups
$$0\rightarrow\pi_0(\calP_v(J_a))^*\rightarrow\pi_0(\calP_v(J_a^0))^*\rightarrow\pi_0(J_{a,v})^*.$$
The subgroup
$$\pi_0(\calP_a)^*\subset\pi_0(\calP'_a)^*$$
is then the intersection of the inverse images of the subgroups
$$\pi_0(\calP_v(J_a))^*\subset\pi_0(\calP_v(J_a^0))^*$$
for all $v\in\bar X-U$. The proposition now follows from \ref{pi 0 local}.
\end{proof}

% [journal 4.10.4 - 4.10.5] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION.
\begin{corollary}\label{pi 0 A diamond}
For $a\in\calA^\diamondsuit(\bar k)$ one has $\pi_0(\calP_a)=Z_{\hat\GG}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $\tilde a=(a,\tilde\infty)$ with $a\in\calA^\diamondsuit(\bar k)$, we have seen that $W_{\tilde a}=\bbW\rtimes\Theta$ and $I_{\tilde a}=\bbW$ \cf \ref{image pi a diamond}. The corollary therefore follows from \ref{pi 0 global}.
\end{proof}

\begin{numero}\label{A ani def}
Let $\calA^\ani(\bar k)$ be the subset of those $a\in\calA^\heartsuit(\bar k)$ for which $\pi_0(\calP_a)$ is finite. By \ref{pi 0 global}, this is equivalent to the finiteness of $\hat\bbT^{W_{\tilde a}}$. We shall show in \ref{I W croissant} that $\calA^\ani(\bar k)$ is the set of $\bar k$-points of an open subset $\calA^\ani$ of $\calA^\heartsuit$.
\end{numero}

\subsection{Automorphisms}\label{subsection : Automorphismes}

Let $(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{M}(\bar{k})$ be a point lying over $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$.
We now establish bounds for the group of automorphisms $\operatorname{Aut}(E,\phi)$ in terms of $a$.
Let $U$ be the open subset of $\bar X$ where $a$ is regular semisimple.

Consider the sheaf of automorphisms $\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(E,\phi)$ that associates to every $\bar{X}$-scheme $S$
the group $\operatorname{Aut}((E,\phi)|_S)$. This sheaf is represented by the group scheme
$$
I_{(E,\phi)}=h_{(E,\phi)}^* I,
$$
which is the pull-back of the centralizer $I$ on $\mathfrak{g}$ via the map $h_{(E,\phi)}:\bar X\rightarrow [\mathfrak{g}_D/G]$.
The restriction of $I_{(E,\phi)}$ to the open $U$ is a torus, but over $\bar X$ the group scheme $I_{(E,\phi)}$
is neither smooth nor flat. However, one may consider its smoothening in the sense of \cite{BLR}. According to loc. cit.,
there exists a unique smooth group scheme $I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis}$ over $\bar X$ such that for every smooth $\bar X$-scheme $S$, we have
$$
\operatorname{Aut}((E,\phi)|_S)=\operatorname{Hom}_X(S,I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis}).
$$
The tautological morphism $I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis}\rightarrow I_{(E,\phi)}$ is an isomorphism over $U$.
Note that by this characterization,
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{Aut}(E,\phi)=\operatorname{H}^0\Bigl(\bar X,I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis}\Bigr).
\end{equation}

Since $J_a$ is smooth, the canonical homomorphism $J_a\rightarrow I_{(E,\phi)}$
induces a homomorphism
$$
J_a\longrightarrow I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis},
$$
which is an isomorphism over $U$. By the universal property of the N\'eron model, there is a homomorphism
$$
I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis}\longrightarrow J_a^\flat,
$$
which is an isomorphism on $U$.

\begin{proposition}\label{borne automorphisme}
For every $(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{M}(\bar{k})$ lying over a point $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, we have canonical inclusions
$$
\operatorname{H}^0(\bar{X},J_a)\subset \operatorname{Aut}(E,\phi)\subset \operatorname{H}^0(\bar{X},J_a^\flat).
$$
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
It suffices to check that the maps $J_a\rightarrow I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis}$ and $I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis}\rightarrow J_a^\flat$
are injective as morphisms of sheaves for the \'etale topology. For this, it is enough to verify injectivity on the formal neighborhoods at each point
$v\in\bar X-U$. Denote by $\bar{\mathcal{O}}_v$ the formal completion of $\mathcal{O}_{\bar X}$ at $v$
and by $\bar{F}_v$ its fraction field. Then we have the inclusions
$$
J_a(\bar{\mathcal{O}}_v)\subset I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis}(\bar{\mathcal{O}}_v)\subset J_a^\flat(\bar{\mathcal{O}}_v)
$$
as subgroups of $J_a(\bar{F}_v)$.
\end{proof}

To describe explicitly the upper bound $\operatorname{H}^0(\bar{X},J_a^\flat)$, choose splittings as in the previous section.
In particular, we have a point $\infty\in X(\bar{k})$ with $a(\infty)\in \bar{\mathfrak{c}}_D^\mathrm{rs}$,
and choose a splitting $\bar{\rho}:\bar X_{\bar\rho}\rightarrow \bar X$ (see \ref{deploiement}). Then there is a finite flat cover
$$
\pi_{\bar\rho,a}:\widetilde X_{\bar\rho,a}\rightarrow \bar X,
$$
which is generically \'etale Galois with Galois group $\mathbb{W}\rtimes \Theta_{\bar\rho}$.
Choosing a point $\tilde\infty_{\bar\rho}\in \widetilde X_{\bar\rho,a}$ above $\infty$ defines a subgroup $W_{\tilde a}\subset \mathbb{W}\rtimes \Theta_{\bar\rho}$
and a normal subgroup $I_{\tilde a}\subset W_{\tilde a}$ as in \S\ref{subsection : pi_0(P_a)}.
Then, by \ref{J a flat global} and \ref{J pi_rho_G}, we have
$$
\operatorname{H}^0(\bar X,J_a^\flat)=\mathbb{T}^{W_{\tilde a}}.
$$
We thus deduce the following corollary.

\begin{corollary}
For every $(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{M}(\bar{k})$ lying over a point $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, $\operatorname{Aut}(E,\phi)$ is identified with a subgroup of $\mathbb{T}^{W_{\tilde a}}$.
\end{corollary}

Recent work of Frenkel and Witten suggests that this bound is not optimal.
In fact, one should have
$$
\operatorname{Aut}(E,\phi)\subset \mathbb{T}(I_{\tilde a},W_{\tilde a}),
$$
where $\mathbb{T}(I_{\tilde a},W_{\tilde a})$ is the subgroup of $\mathbb{T}^{W_{\tilde a}}$ defined as in Proposition~\ref{pi 0 global}
(with $\hat{\mathbb{T}}$ replaced by $\mathbb{T}$). Moreover, equality is expected to be achieved
at the most singular points of the fiber $\mathcal{M}_a$.

% [journal 4.11.4] The arXiv version re-defined A^ani here by finiteness of T^{W_atilde},
% duplicating (with a different condition) the definition already given in 4.10.5, and then
% announced an open subset that 4.10.5 has already produced. The journal instead DEDUCES
% finiteness over that open subset and draws the Deligne-Mumford conclusion.
\begin{numero}\label{ani DM}
Over the open subset $\calA^\ani$ \cf \ref{A ani def}, the group $\hat\bbT^{W_{\tilde a}}$ is finite. The same holds for $\bbT^{W_{\tilde a}}$. Assuming the order of $\bbW\rtimes\Theta$ prime to the characteristic, $\bbT^{W_{\tilde a}}$ will be finite unramified. The restriction of $\calM$ to $\calA^\ani$ is therefore Deligne-Mumford, by the corollary above.
\end{numero}

% translated at \ref{P Deligne-Mumford}. The two stated DIFFERENT hypotheses (this one
% dropped deg(D)>2g and the order-prime-to-p condition, and used W x| Theta_rhobar in
% place of Theta), so the document asserted the same result twice with conflicting
% hypotheses. Kept at ch4/OLD_automorphisme_P_a.tex.


%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
% [journal 4.12] "Module de Tate polarisable". NEW SUBSECTION in the journal version.
% The arXiv version proved polarizability inside chapter 7 (the Hitchin application);
% the journal moves the whole Weil-pairing argument here, as 4.12.1 and 4.12.2, and
% chapter 7 now merely cites it. The block below is that material, relocated verbatim.
