\subsection{Polarizable Tate Module}\label{subsection : module de Tate polarisable}

% [journal 4.12] Opening paragraphs, ABSENT from the arXiv version, which stated the
% proposition directly. They set up P^0 and the rigidifier decomposition, and are what
% make the canonical devissage of the Tate module available for a Picard STACK, where
% no Chevalley devissage exists.
Let us place ourselves under the hypothesis of \ref{P Deligne-Mumford}, so that $\calP$ is a smooth Deligne-Mumford Picard stack over $\calA^\heartsuit$. Write $\calP^0$ for the Picard substack of neutral components of $\calP$, and let $g:\calP^0\rightarrow\calA^\heartsuit$ be the structural morphism, which is smooth of relative dimension $d$. Consider the Tate module
$$\rmT_\Ql(\calP^0)=\rmH^{2d-1}(g_!\Ql),$$
which is a $\Ql$-sheaf on $\calA^\heartsuit$.

The more elementary way of presenting this sheaf is to use rigidifiers. As in \ref{rigidificateur}, we have defined over the open subset $\calA^\infty$ smooth group schemes with connected fibers $P^{-1}$ and $P^0$, with $P^{-1}$ affine, such that there is an exact sequence
$$1\rightarrow P^{-1}\rightarrow P^0\rightarrow \calP^0|_{\calA^\infty}\rightarrow 1.$$
There follows an exact sequence of $\Ql$-Tate modules
$$0\rightarrow \rmT_\Ql(P^{-1})\rightarrow \rmT_\Ql(P^0)\rightarrow \rmT_\Ql(\calP^0|_{\calA^\infty})\rightarrow 0.$$

For every geometric point $a\in\calA^\infty(\bar k)$, write $A_a$ for the maximal abelian quotient of $P^0_a$. Since $P^{-1}_a$ is affine, the arrow $\rmT_\Ql(P^0_a)\rightarrow \rmT_\Ql(A_a)$ factors through $\rmT_\Ql(\calP_a^0)$. From this we deduce an exact sequence
$$0\rightarrow \rmT_\Ql(R_a)\rightarrow \rmT_\Ql(\calP_a^0)\rightarrow \rmT_\Ql(A_a)\rightarrow 0,$$
which in fact does not depend on the choice of rigidifier. Thus, although no canonical Chevalley decomposition is available for the Deligne-Mumford Picard stack $\calP_a^0$, one does have a canonical decomposition of its $\Ql$-Tate module.

% [journal 4.12.1] The arXiv version stated this as "the Tate module of \calP^ani is
% polarizable in the sense of 7.1.4". The journal states it over \calA^\heartsuit (not
% just the anisotropic open) and spells the alternating form out explicitly. Restated.
\begin{proposition}\label{T ell P est polarisable}
There exists an alternating form
$$\psi:\rmT_\Ql(\calP^\heartsuit)\times \rmT_\Ql(\calP^\heartsuit)\rightarrow \Ql(-1)$$
such that at every geometric point $a\in\calA^\heartsuit(\bar k)$ the form $\psi_a$ vanishes on the affine part $\rmT_\Ql(R_a)$ and induces a perfect pairing on the abelian part $\rmT_\Ql(A_a)$.
\end{proposition}

The proof of this proposition is based on Weil pairing theory which we will recall for the reader's convenience. Let $S$ be a strictly Henselian local scheme. Let $c:C\rightarrow S$ be a proper flat morphism with geometrically reduced fibers of dimension one.

Suppose that $C$ is connected. Consider the Stein factorization $C\rightarrow S'\rightarrow S$ where $C\rightarrow S'$ is a proper morphism with connected non-empty fibers and $S'\rightarrow S$ is a finite morphism. Since the special fiber of $c$ is reduced, the morphism $S'\rightarrow S$ is finite and \'etale. Since $C$ is connected, $S'$ is also connected. Since we assumed that $S$ is strictly Henselian, we then have $S'=S$. In other words, the fibers of $c:C\rightarrow S$ are connected.

Consider the $S$-Artin stack $\Pic_{C/S}$ which associates to any $S$-scheme $Y$ the groupoid of invertible bundles on $C\times_S Y$. It is smooth over $S$. Consider its neutral component $\Pic^0_{C/S}$. For any point $L\in \Pic_{X/S}(Y)$, for any $y\in Y$, we can define the Euler-Poincar\'e characteristic $\chi_y(L)$ of the restriction of $L$ to $C_y$. If $Y$ is connected, this integer is independent of $y$ and we denote it $\chi(L)$. If $L\in \Pic^0_{C/S}$, we have $\chi(L)=\chi(\calO_C)$.

For any pair of $L,L'\in \Pic^0_{C/S}$, we define their Weil pairing by the formula
\begin{eqnarray*}
\langle L,L' \rangle_{C/S} = {\rm det}(\rmR c_*(L\otimes L'))\otimes {\rm det}(\rmR c_* L)^{\otimes-1} \\ \otimes {\rm det}(\rmR c_* L')^{\otimes-1} \otimes {\rm det}(\rmR c_* \calO_C)
\end{eqnarray*}
using the determinant of cohomology. If $t$ is an automorphism of $L$ which is then a scalar, $t$ acts on $\det(Rc_* L)$ by the scalar $t^{\chi(L)}$. Using the equalities
$$\chi(L\otimes L')=\chi(L)=\chi(L')=\chi(\calO_C)$$
for $L,L'\in \Pic^0_{X/S}$, we verify that for any pair of scalars $(t,t')$ the action of $t$ on $L$ and the action of $t'$ on $L'$ induce the identity on $\langle L,L' \rangle_{C/S}$.

If $N$ is an invertible integer on $S$ and $L$ is an invertible bundle equipped with an isomorphism $\iota_L:L^{\otimes N}\rightarrow\calO_C$, we have an isomorphism
$$\langle L,L' \rangle_{C/S}^{\otimes N}=\calO_S.$$
If furthermore $L'$ is also equipped with an isomorphism $\iota_{L'}:L'^{\otimes N}\rightarrow \calO_C$, we have another isomorphism $\langle L,L' \rangle_{C/S}^{\otimes N}=\calO_S$. The difference between these two isomorphisms defines an $N$-th root of unity. This root depends only on the isomorphism classes of $L$ and $L'$ by virtue of the discussion on the effect of scalars.


Suppose now that $C$ is a connected projective curve over an algebraically closed field $k$. The stack ${\rm Pic}_C^0$ is then the quotient of a connected commutative algebraic $k$-group ${\rm Jac}_C$ by $\GG_m$ acting trivially. The above construction defines an alternating form
$$\rmT_\Ql({\rm Jac}_C)\times \rmT_\Ql({\rm Jac}_C) \longrightarrow \Ql(-1)$$
which is non-degenerate when $C$ is smooth. To conclude this digression, we still need to consider the behavior of the Weil pairing with respect to normalization.

\begin{lemme}\label{normalisation Weil pairing}
Let $C$ be a reduced projective curve over an algebraically closed field $k$. Let $C^\flat$ be its normalization and $\xi:C^\flat \rightarrow C$ be the normalization morphism. Let $L,L'$ be two invertible bundles on $C$. Then there exists a canonical isomorphism of one-dimensional $k$-vector spaces
$$\langle L,L'\rangle_{C}=\langle \xi^*L,\xi^*L' \rangle_{C^\flat}.$$
\end{lemme}

\begin{proof} We have an exact sequence
$$0\longrightarrow \calO_C \longrightarrow \xi_* \calO_{C^\flat} \longrightarrow \mathcal{D} \longrightarrow 0$$
where $\mathcal{D}$ is an $\calO_C$-module of finite length supported on a finite collection of points $\{c_1,\ldots,c_n\}$ of $C$. Let $\mathcal{D}_i$ denote the direct factor of $\mathcal{D}$ supported on $c_i$ and $d_i$ its length. The multiplicativity of the determinant then provides us with an isomorphism
$${\rm det}(c^\flat_*\calO_{C^\flat})={\rm det}(c_*\xi_*\calO_{C^\flat})={\rm det}(c_*\calO_{C})\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^r \wedge^{d_i} \mathcal{D}_i$$
where $c:C\rightarrow\Spec(k)$ and $c^\flat:C^\flat\rightarrow \Spec(k)$ denote the structural morphisms.

Let $L$ be an invertible bundle on $C$. By using the projection formula $\xi_* \xi^* L=(\xi_*\calO)\otimes L$, we obtain the formula
$${\rm det}(c^\flat_*\xi^*L)={\rm det}(c_*L)\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^r (L_{c_i}^{\otimes d_i}\otimes \wedge^{d_i} \mathcal{D}_i)$$
where $L_{c_i}$ is the fiber of $L$ at $c_i$. By applying this formula to $L\otimes L'$, $L$ and to $L'$, we obtain the lemma.
\end{proof}

We are now able to prove Proposition \ref{T ell P est polarisable}.

\begin{proof}
Recall that for any point $a\in \calA^\heartsuit$, $\calP_a$ is the Picard stack of $J_a$-torsors on $X$. Let $\pi_a:\tilde X_a\rightarrow X$ be the cameral cover associated with $a$. According to the Galois description of the regular centralizer, we have a homomorphism of group sheaves \cf \ref{J J1}
$$J_a \rightarrow \pi_{a,*}(T\times_X \tilde X_a)$$
where $T$ is the maximal torus of $G$ in the fixed pinning. By considering a splitting $X_\rho\rightarrow X$ of $G$, we obtain a finite \'etale cover $\tilde X_{\rho,a}\rightarrow \tilde X_a$ by base change. Let $\pi_{\rho,a}:\tilde X_{\rho,a}\rightarrow X$ be the composed morphism. We thus have a homomorphism
$$J_a \rightarrow \pi_{\rho,a,*}(\bbT\times \tilde X_{\rho,a}).$$
where $\bbT$ is a split torus. The datum of an $\ell^n$-torsion point of $\calP_a$ thus induces an $\ell^n$-torsion point of ${\rm Pic}_{\tilde X_{\rho,a}}\otimes \bbX_*(\bbT)$. By choosing an invariant symmetric form on $\bbX_*(\bbT)\otimes\Ql$ and using the Weil pairing on ${\rm Pic}^0_{\tilde X_{\rho,a}}$, we obtain an alternating form
$$\rmT_\Ql(\calP_a^0)\otimes \rmT_\Ql(\calP_a^0)\longrightarrow \Ql(-1).$$
It remains to show that at each geometric point $a$, this symplectic form is zero on the affine part of $\rmT_\ell(\calP_a)$ and induces a non-degenerate pairing on its abelian part. This follows from Lemma \ref{normalisation Weil pairing}.
\end{proof}

% [journal 4.13] moved: in the arXiv version "Dimensions" came 4th, before the cameral
% curve; in the journal it is 4.13, after "Module de Tate polarisable".
\subsection{Dimensions}\label{subsection : dimension}
We will use the same notation $\mathfrak{c}_D$ to denote both the vector bundle over $X$
and the corresponding locally free $\mathcal{O}_X$-module. If $G$ is split, the $\mathcal{O}_X$-module $\mathfrak{c}_D$
has the explicit expression
$$
\mathfrak{c}_D=\bigoplus_{i=1}^r D^{\otimes e_i},
$$
where $e_1,\ldots,e_r$ are the natural numbers appearing in \ref{Chevalley}. In general, $\mathfrak{c}_D$
has this form after a finite \'etale Galois base change $\rho: X_\rho \rightarrow X$ that splits $G$.
By definition, the vector bundle $\mathfrak{c}_D$ is
$$
\mathfrak{c}_D=\operatorname{Spec}\Bigl(\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathcal{O}_X}(\mathfrak{c}_D^*)\Bigr),
$$
where $\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathcal{O}_X}(\mathfrak{c}_D^*)$ is the symmetric algebra sheaf of the dual
$\mathcal{O}_X$-module $\mathfrak{c}_D^*$.

\begin{lemma}\label{dim A}
If $\deg(D)>2g-2$, then $\mathcal{A}$ is an affine $k$-space of dimension
$$
\dim(\mathcal{A})=\frac{\sharp\Phi\,\deg(D)}{2}+r(1-g+\deg(D)),
$$
where $r$ is the rank of $\mathbb{G}$ and $\sharp\,\Phi$ is the number of its roots.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\rho:X_\rho\rightarrow X$ be the finite \'etale Galois cover that splits $G$. Then
$\rho^*\mathfrak{c}_D$ is isomorphic to the direct sum $\bigoplus_{i=1}^r \rho^*D^{\otimes e_i}$.
It follows that
$$
\deg(\mathfrak{c}_D)=(e_1+\cdots+e_r)\deg(D).
$$
By the Riemann--Roch theorem, we have
$$
% [journal 4.13] the journal has a MINUS (Euler characteristic); the arXiv text had a plus.
\dim \operatorname{H}^0 (X,\mathfrak{c}_D)-\dim\operatorname{H}^1(X,\mathfrak{c}_D)=(e_1+\cdots+e_r)\deg(D)+r(1-g).
$$
According to Kostant, the integers $e_i-1$ are the exponents of the root system $\Phi$, so that
$$
e_1+\cdots+e_r=r+\frac{\sharp\,\Phi}{2}.
$$
Thus, it remains only to prove that $\operatorname{H}^1(X,\mathfrak{c}_D)=0$. Since $\deg(D)>2g-2$
and $\rho$ is finite \'etale, we have
$$
\deg(\rho^* D)>\deg (\rho^*\Omega_{X/ k})=\deg(\Omega_{X_\rho/ k}),
$$
from which the vanishing of $\operatorname{H}^1(X_\rho, \rho^*D^{\otimes e_i})$ follows.
To prove the vanishing of $\operatorname{H}^1(X,\mathfrak{c}_D)$, note that $\operatorname{H}^1(X,\mathfrak{c}_D)$
is a direct summand of $\operatorname{H}^1(X_\rho, \rho^*\mathfrak{c}_D)$.
\end{proof}

Thus, if $\deg(D)$ is a fixed integer greater than $2g-2$, the dimension of the Hitchin base $\mathcal{A}$ does not depend on $D$
nor on the quasi-split form.

\begin{proposition}
For every $a\in\mathcal{A}(\bar{k})$, there is a canonical isomorphism
$$
\operatorname{Lie}(J_a)=\mathfrak{c}_D^*\otimes D.
$$
\end{proposition}

In the proof below, when $f:X\rightarrow Y$ and $\mathcal{L}$ is an $\mathcal{O}_Y$-module, we
simply write $\mathcal{L}$ to also denote its pull-back $f^*\mathcal{L}$. This abuse of notation
should not cause confusion since the scheme on which the module lives is always clear from context.

\begin{proof}
According to \ref{J=J'}, the homomorphism $J_a\rightarrow J^1_a$ induces an isomorphism
$\operatorname{Lie}(J_a) \rightarrow \operatorname{Lie}(J^1_a)$ on Lie algebras.
By the construction of $J^1$, $\operatorname{Lie}(J^1_a)$ can be computed using the cameral
cover $\pi_a:\widetilde X_a\rightarrow \bar X$ by the formula
$$
\operatorname{Lie}(J^1_a)=\Bigl((\pi_a)_*\mathfrak{t}\Bigr)^W.
$$
Recall that the cameral cover is defined by forming the cartesian diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
\widetilde X_a \ar[d]_{\pi_a} \ar[r] & \mathfrak{t}_D \ar[d]^{\pi} \\
\bar X \ar[r]_{a} & \mathfrak{c}_D.
}
$$
Since $\pi$ is finite and flat, the formation of $\pi_* \mathfrak{t}$ commutes with any base change,
in particular $(\pi_a)_*\mathfrak{t}=a^*\pi_* \mathfrak{t}$. Hence, it suffices to compute $(\pi_*\mathfrak{t})^W$.

Since $\mathfrak{c}_D$ is the invariant quotient of $\mathfrak{t}_D$ by the action of $W$, the same holds for
the total spaces of the tangent bundles $T_{\mathfrak{t}_D/X}$ and $T_{\mathfrak{c}_D/X}$. Thus,
$$
(\pi_* \Omega_{\mathfrak{t}_D/X})^W=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}_D/X}.
$$
Moreover, as $\mathfrak{t}_D$ is a vector bundle over $X$, we have $\Omega_{\mathfrak{t}_D/X}=\mathfrak{t}_{D^{-1}}$.
Similarly, $\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}_D/X}=\mathfrak{c}_D^*$. We ultimately obtain an equality of $\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{c}_D}$-modules
$$
(\pi_* \mathfrak{t}_{D^{-1}})^{W}=\mathfrak{c}_D^*,
$$
so that
$$
(\pi_*\mathfrak{t})^W=\mathfrak{c}_D^*\otimes D.
$$
Pulling back this equality via the section $a:\bar X\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}_D$, we obtain
$\operatorname{Lie}(J_a)=\mathfrak{c}_D^*\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_X} D$.
\end{proof}

In the case where $G$ is split, the lemma allows one to express $\operatorname{Lie}(J_a)$ in terms
of $D$ and the exponents of the root system $\Phi$. If $e_1,\ldots,e_r$ are the degrees of the homogeneous invariant
polynomials (see \ref{Chevalley}), then
$$
\operatorname{Lie}(J_a)=D^{-e_1+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus D^{-e_r+1}.
$$
If $G$ is not split, $\operatorname{Lie}(J_a)$ becomes isomorphic to the direct sum on a finite \'etale Galois cover of $X$ that
splits $G$. In particular,
$$
\deg(\operatorname{Lie}(J_a))=\sum_{i=1}^r (-e_i+1)\deg(D)=-\frac{\sharp\,\Phi\,\deg(D)}{2}.
$$

\begin{corollary}\label{dim fibre}
For every $a\in \mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$,
$$
\dim(\mathcal{P}_a)=\frac{\sharp\,\Phi\,\deg(D)}{2}+r(g-1).
$$
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
We have
$$
\dim(\mathcal{P}_a)=\dim\operatorname{H}^1(\bar X,\operatorname{Lie}(J_a))-\dim\operatorname{H}^0(\bar X,\operatorname{Lie}(J_a)),
$$
so that the stated equality follows from the Riemann--Roch formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{numero}
% [journal 4.13.4] the sentence defining d as the relative dimension of P over A was
% dropped, although d is used unexplained in 4.12 and 4.16.
We shall write $d=\sharp\,\Phi\deg(D)/2+r(g-1)$ for the relative dimension of $\calP$ over $\calA$. Comparing with the formula \ref{dim A}
$$
\dim(\mathcal{A})=\frac{\sharp\,\Phi\,\deg(D)}{2}+r(1-g+\deg(D)),
$$
we obtain
$$
\dim(\mathcal{P})=(r+\sharp\,\Phi)\deg(D).
$$
% [journal 4.13.4] the journal has a single closing paragraph here, not two.
In \ref{densite globale} we will show that $\mathcal{M}_a^\mathrm{reg}$ is dense in $\mathcal{M}_a$ so that one obtains the same dimension formulas for $\mathcal{M}$
$$
\dim(\mathcal{M}_a)=\frac{\sharp\,\Phi\,\deg(D)}{2}+r(g-1)
$$
and
$$
\dim(\mathcal{M})=(r+\sharp\,\Phi)\deg(D).
$$
\end{numero}

\subsection{Deformation Calculation}\label{subsection : deformation}

The deformations of Higgs bundles have been studied by Biswas and Ramanan in \cite{BR}.
For the reader's convenience we shall take up their computation again.

Recall first the standard computation of the deformations of a torsor under a smooth group.
Let $S$ be a $k$-scheme and $G$ a smooth group scheme over $S$. Let $\mathbf{B}G$ be the classifying stack of $G$.
The universal $G$-torsor $\mathbf{E}G$ is then $S$ over $[S/G]$. Denote
$$
\pi_{\mathbf{E}G}:\mathbf{E}G\longrightarrow \mathbf{B}G
$$
the tautological $G$-torsor. Consider the distinguished triangle of cotangent complexes
$$
\pi_{\mathbf{E}G}^* L_{\mathbf{B}G/S}\longrightarrow L_{\mathbf{E}G/S}\longrightarrow L_{\mathbf{E}G/\mathbf{B}G}\longrightarrow \pi_{\mathbf{E}G}^* L_{\mathbf{B}G/S}[1].
$$
Since $S=\mathbf{E}G$, the cotangent complex $L_{\mathbf{E}G/S}$ is zero,
while $L_{\mathbf{E}G/\mathbf{B}G}$ is the vector bundle $\mathfrak{g}^*$ placed in degree $0$.
It follows that
$$
L_{\mathbf{E}G/\mathbf{B}G}\cong \pi_{\mathbf{E}G}^* L_{\mathbf{B}G/S}[1].
$$
Hence, we deduce an isomorphism
$$
\mathfrak{g}^*[-1]\cong \pi_{\mathbf{E}G}^* L_{\mathbf{B}G/S},
$$
which, by descent along $\pi_{\mathbf{E}G}$, induces an isomorphism
$$
\Bigl(\mathbf{E}G\wedge^G\mathfrak{g}^*\Bigr)[-1]\cong L_{\mathbf{B}G/S}.
$$
Thus, the cotangent complex $L_{\mathbf{B}G/k}$ of the classifying stack of $G$ is the vector bundle obtained
by twisting $\mathfrak{g}^*$ by the torsor $\mathbf{E}G$, placed in degree $1$.

Therefore, for any $S$-scheme $X$ and any $G$-torsor $E$ on $X$ corresponding to a map $h_E:X\rightarrow \mathbf{B}G$,
the obstruction to deforming $E$ lies in
$$
\operatorname{H}^1\Bigl(X,\underline{\operatorname{RHom}}\bigl(h_E^*L_{\mathbf{B}G/S},\mathcal{O}_X\bigr)\Bigr)=\operatorname{H}^2(X,E\wedge^G \mathfrak{g}).
$$
If this obstruction vanishes, the deformations form a torsor under
$$
\operatorname{H}^0\Bigl(X,\underline{\operatorname{RHom}}\bigl(h_E^*L_{\mathbf{B}G/S},\mathcal{O}_X\bigr)\Bigr)=\operatorname{H}^1(X,E\wedge^G \mathfrak{g}),
$$
and the group of infinitesimal automorphisms is $\operatorname{H}^0(X,E\wedge^G \mathfrak{g})$.

Now let $V$ be a vector bundle on $S$ with a $G$-action and consider the quotient stack $[V/G]$.
The $G$-torsor $\pi_V:V\rightarrow [V/G]$ defines a morphism $[\nu]:[V/G]\rightarrow \mathbf{B}G$,
which fits into a cartesian diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
V \ar[d]_{\pi_V} \ar[r]^{\nu} & \mathbf{E}G \ar[d]^{\pi_{\mathbf{E}G}} \\
[V/G] \ar[r]_{[\nu]} & \mathbf{B}G.
}
$$
Consider the distinguished triangle of cotangent complexes
$$
\pi_V^* L_{[V/G]/S}\longrightarrow L_{V/S}\longrightarrow L_{V/[V/G]}\longrightarrow \pi_V^* L_{[V/G]/k}[1].
$$
Here, $L_{V/S}$ is a constant vector bundle of value $\nu^*V^*$ placed in degree $0$ (with $V^*$ the dual of $V$)
and $L_{V/[V/G]}=\nu^*L_{\mathbf{E}G/\mathbf{B}G}$ is the vector bundle $\nu^*\mathfrak{g}^*$ placed in degree $0$.
At each point $v\in V$, the $G$-action yields a linear map
$$
\alpha_v:\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow T_v V=V,
$$
whose dual is the map
$$
\alpha_v^*:(L_{V/S})_v=V^*\longrightarrow (L_{V/[V/G]})_v=\mathfrak{g}^*.
$$
This map descends to $[V/G]$ as
$$
\alpha_v^*\wedge^G \pi_V:
\pi_V\wedge^G V^*\longrightarrow \pi_V\wedge^G \mathfrak{g}^*,
$$
whose cone is isomorphic to $L_{[V/G]/S}$.

Apply the above to calculate the deformations of Hitchin pairs.
Recall the notations fixed at the beginning of Section~\ref{section : Fibres de Springer affines}.
In particular, $G$ is the reductive group scheme over the curve $X$ and $\mathfrak{g}$ is its Lie algebra,
equipped with the adjoint action of $G$ and with the $\mathbb{G}_m$-action by homothety.
The line bundle $D$ defines a $\mathbb{G}_m$-torsor $L_D$. Consider the stack $[\mathfrak{g}_D/G]$ obtained by twisting
$\mathfrak{g}$ by $L_D$ and then taking the quotient by $G$. Then the cotangent complex $L_{[\mathfrak{g}_D/G]/X}$ is identified with
$$
L_{[\mathfrak{g}/G]/X}\wedge^{\mathbb{G}_m} L_D,
$$
which is the cone of
$$
\Bigl(\pi_{D,\mathfrak{g}}\wedge^G\mathfrak{g}^*\Bigr)\otimes D^{-1}\longrightarrow \pi_{D,\mathfrak{g}}\wedge^G\mathfrak{g}^*,
$$
where $\pi_{D,\mathfrak{g}}$ is the obvious $G$-torsor over the quotient $[\mathfrak{g}_D/G]$.

Let $(E,\phi)$ be a Higgs bundle on $X$ with values in $\bar{k}$. It corresponds to a morphism
$$
h_{E,\phi}:\bar{X}\rightarrow [\mathfrak{g}_D/G].
$$
The deformation of $(E,\phi)$ is controlled by the complex
$$
\underline{\operatorname{RHom}}\Bigl(h_{E,\phi}^*\Bigl(L_D\wedge^{\mathbb{G}_m} L_{[\mathfrak{g}/G]/X}\Bigr),\mathcal{O}_X\Bigr),
$$
which in turn identifies with
$$
\operatorname{ad}(E,\phi):=[\operatorname{ad}(E)\longrightarrow \operatorname{ad}(E)\otimes D],
$$
where
\begin{itemize}
\item $\operatorname{ad}(E)$ is the vector bundle $E\wedge^G \mathfrak{g}$;
\item $\operatorname{ad}(E)$ is placed in degree $-1$, and $\operatorname{ad}(E)\otimes D$ is placed in degree $0$;
\item the differential is given by $x\mapsto [x,\phi]$.
\end{itemize}

Recall Proposition~5.3 of \cite{N}. (The reader will note a difference in the shift of $\operatorname{ad}(E,\phi)$ relative to that reference.)
We now give an alternative proof.

\begin{theoreme}\label{lisse}
Let $(E,\phi)\in \mathcal{M}(\bar{k})$ be a point lying over a point $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$.
Then the group $\operatorname{H}^1(X,\operatorname{ad}(E,\phi))$, in which the obstruction to deforming the pair $(E,\phi)$ lies,
vanishes in either of the following cases:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\deg(D)> 2g-2$,
\item $\deg(D)= 2g-2$ and $a\in \mathcal{A}^\mathrm{ani}(\bar{k})$.
\end{itemize}
If either hypothesis is satisfied, then $\mathcal{M}$ is smooth at $(E,\phi)$.
\end{theoreme}

\begin{proof}
Fix a nondegenerate, symmetric, invariant form on $\mathfrak{g}$. Then the dual of the complex $\operatorname{ad}(E,\phi)$
is identified with
$$
\operatorname{ad}(E,\phi)^*=[\operatorname{ad}(E)\otimes D^{-1}\longrightarrow \operatorname{ad}(E)],
$$
with the two nonzero terms placed in degrees $-1$ and $0$, and with differential $x\mapsto [x,\phi]$.
The $(-1)$-st cohomology of this complex is isomorphic to
$$
\operatorname{Lie}(I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis})\otimes D^{-1},
$$
where $I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis}$ is the smooth group scheme over $\bar X$ defined in \S\ref{subsection : Automorphismes}.
By Serre duality, $\operatorname{H}^1(X,\operatorname{ad}(E,\phi))$ is dual to
$$
\operatorname{H}^0\Bigl(\bar{X},\operatorname{Lie}(I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis})\otimes D^{-1}\otimes \Omega_{X/k}\Bigr).
$$
Since we have an injection $\operatorname{Lie}(I_{(E,\phi)}^{\rm lis})\rightarrow \operatorname{Lie}(J_a^\flat)$,
it suffices to prove that
$$
\operatorname{H}^0\Bigl(\bar{X},\operatorname{Lie}(J_a^\flat)\otimes D^{-1}\otimes \Omega_{X/k}\Bigr)=0.
$$
This reduces to proving the following lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{annulation J a flat}
For every $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\overline{k})$, one has
$$
\operatorname{H}^0\Bigl(\bar{X},\operatorname{Lie}(J_a^\flat)\otimes L\Bigr)=0
$$
for every line bundle $L$ of strictly negative degree.
The same conclusion holds under the hypothesis $a\in\mathcal{A}^\mathrm{ani}(\bar{k})$ when $\deg(L)\leq 0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose a splitting $\bar\rho:\bar{X}_{\bar\rho}\rightarrow \bar X$ of $G$ (see \ref{deploiement}).
Then there is a finite flat cover $\widetilde{X}_{\bar\rho,a}\rightarrow\bar X$ which is generically \'etale Galois with group
$\mathbb{W}\rtimes \Theta_{\bar\rho}$. Let $\widetilde{X}_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat$ be the normalization of $\widetilde{X}_{\bar\rho,a}$,
and denote by $\pi_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat$ its projection to $\bar X$. By \ref{J a flat global},
$$
\operatorname{Lie}(J_a^\flat)=(\pi_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat)_*\Bigl(\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat}\otimes \mathfrak{t}\Bigr)^{\mathbb{W}\rtimes \Theta_{\bar\rho}},
$$
so that
$$
\operatorname{Lie}(J_a^\flat)\otimes L=(\pi_a^\flat)_*\Bigl((\pi_a^\flat)^*L\otimes \mathfrak{t}\Bigr)^{\mathbb{W}\rtimes \Theta_{\bar\rho}}.
$$
If $\deg(L)<0$, then $(\pi_a^\flat)^*L$ is a line bundle of strictly negative degree on each connected component of $\widetilde{X}_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat$
and cannot have any nonzero global sections.
If $\deg(L)=0$, then $(\pi_a^\flat)^*L$ has nonzero global sections if and only if it is isomorphic to $\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat}$.
In that case,
$$
\operatorname{H}^0\Bigl(\widetilde{X}_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat,\,((\pi_{\bar\rho,a}^\flat)^*L\otimes \mathfrak{t})^{\mathbb{W}\rtimes \Theta_{\bar\rho}}\Bigr)=\mathfrak{t}^{W_{\tilde a}},
$$
where $W_{\tilde a}$ is the subgroup of $\mathbb{W}\rtimes \Theta_{\bar\rho}$ defined as in \S\ref{subsection : pi_0(P_a)}.
Under the hypothesis $a\in\mathcal{A}^\mathrm{ani}(\bar{k})$, the group $\mathfrak{t}^{W_{\tilde a}}$ vanishes.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Product Formula}

We now recall the link between the Hitchin fibers and the affine Springer fibers,
which are their local analogues.

Let $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, and let $U$ be the inverse image of the regular semisimple
locus $\mathfrak{c}^\mathrm{rs}$ under the map $a:\bar X\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}_D$.
The gluing with the Kostant section defines a morphism
$$
\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\mathcal{M}_v(a)\longrightarrow \mathcal{M}_a.
$$
Similarly, there is a group homomorphism $\prod_{v\in \bar X-U}\mathcal{P}_v(J_a)\longrightarrow \mathcal{P}_a$.
These induce a morphism
$$
\zeta:\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\mathcal{M}_v(a)
\wedge^{\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\mathcal{P}_v(J_a)} \mathcal{P}_a
\longrightarrow \mathcal{M}_a.
$$
According to Theorem~4.6 of \cite{N}, this morphism induces an equivalence on the category of $\bar{k}$-points.
(Notice that, compared to \cite{N}, the notations have been changed: what was denoted
by $\mathcal{M}_{v,a}^\bullet$ there is now $\mathcal{M}_v(a)$, and the notation $\mathcal{M}_{v,a}$ does not appear here.)

\begin{proposition}\label{produit}
For every $a\in\mathcal{A}^\mathrm{ani}(\bar{k})$, the quotient of
$$
\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\mathcal{M}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(a)\times \mathcal{P}_a
$$
by the diagonal action of $\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\mathcal{P}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(J_a)$ is a proper Deligne--Mumford stack.
Moreover, the morphism
$$
\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\mathcal{M}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(a)
\wedge^{\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\mathcal{P}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(J_a)} \mathcal{P}_a
\longrightarrow \mathcal{M}_a
$$
is a homeomorphism.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The homomorphism $\mathcal{P}_v(J_a)\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_a$ induces a map $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_v(J_a))\rightarrow \pi_0(\mathcal{P}_a)$ on connected components.
Since $a\in\mathcal{A}^\mathrm{ani}(\bar{k})$, $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_a)$ is finite,
and the kernel of this map is a subgroup of finite index in $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_v(J_a))$.
Thus, there exists a free abelian subgroup $\Lambda_v$ of finite index in $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_v(J_a))$ contained in the kernel.

Choose a lift $\Lambda_v\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_v(J_a)$.
Since $a$ is defined over a finite field, after possibly replacing $\Lambda_v$ by a subgroup of finite index,
we may assume that $\Lambda_v$ is contained in the kernel of $\mathcal{P}_v(J_a)\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_a$.

The group $\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\Lambda_v$ acts on $\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\mathcal{M}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(a)\times \mathcal{P}_a$
by acting freely on the first factor and trivially on the second. The quotient is
$$
\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\Bigl(\mathcal{M}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(a)/ \Lambda_v\Bigr)\times \mathcal{P}_a,
$$
where each $\mathcal{M}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(a)/ \Lambda_v$ is a projective $\bar{k}$-scheme (by Kazhdan--Lusztig; see \ref{quotient projectif}).

It remains to quotient by $\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\Bigl(\mathcal{P}_v(J_a)/ \Lambda_v\Bigr)$.
For each $v$, the homomorphism $\mathcal{R}_v(a)\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_v(J_a)/ \Lambda_v$ is injective with finite kernel.
Recall that we have a short exact sequence
$$
1\rightarrow \mathcal{R}_a\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_a\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_a^\flat\rightarrow 1,
$$
where $\mathcal{P}_a^\flat$ is a proper Deligne--Mumford stack.
Thus, the quotient of
$$
\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\Bigl(\mathcal{M}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(a)/ \Lambda_v\Bigr)\times \mathcal{P}_a
$$
by the diagonal action of $\mathcal{R}_a=\prod_v \mathcal{R}_v(a)$ is a locally trivial fibration over $\mathcal{P}_a^\flat$
with fibers isomorphic to
$$
\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\Bigl(\mathcal{M}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(a)/ \Lambda_v\Bigr).
$$
This quotient is then a proper Deligne--Mumford stack.

Finally, we must quotient by the finite group $\prod_v \mathcal{P}_v(J_a)/(\mathcal{R}_v(a)\times \Lambda_v)$.
The final quotient is also a proper Deligne--Mumford stack.

Since $\mathcal{M}_a$ is a Deligne--Mumford stack (in particular, separated), the morphism
$$
\zeta:\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\mathcal{M}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(a)
\wedge^{\prod_{v\in\bar X-U}\mathcal{P}_v^{\mathrm{red}}(J_a)} \mathcal{P}_a\longrightarrow \mathcal{M}_a
$$
is proper. Since it induces an equivalence on $\bar{k}$-points, it is a homeomorphism.
In particular, $\mathcal{M}_a$ is a proper Deligne--Mumford stack.
\end{proof}

One expects that the statement above extends to all $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$.

% [journal 4.15.2] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION. This is the statement cited twice
% in 7.8; without it those citations pointed at the product formula 4.15.1 instead.
\begin{corollary}\label{Ma projectif}
For every $a\in\calA^\ani(\bar k)$, $\calM_a$ is homeomorphic to a projective scheme. Moreover, for every $m\in\calM_a(\bar k)$, the stabilizer of $m$ in $\calP_a$ is an affine group.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{Density}\label{dense}

We now state and prove the global analogue of \ref{densite locale}.

\begin{proposition}\label{densite globale}
For every geometric point $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, the fiber $\mathcal{M}^\mathrm{reg}_a$ is dense in the fiber $\mathcal{M}_a$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The product formula (Proposition~\ref{produit}) implies that the closed complement of $\mathcal{M}^\mathrm{reg}_a$ in $\mathcal{M}_a$
has strictly smaller dimension than $\mathcal{M}^\mathrm{reg}_a$. Since the total Hitchin stack $\mathcal{M}$ is smooth over $k$ (by \ref{lisse}),
the Hitchin fibers $\mathcal{M}_a$ are locally complete intersections. In particular, they cannot have irreducible components of strictly smaller dimension than themselves.
This shows that $\mathcal{M}^\mathrm{reg}_a$ is dense in $\mathcal{M}_a$.
\end{proof}

The proof above is essentially the same as that of Altman, Iarrobino, and Kleiman in \cite{AIK} for the density of the Jacobian in the compactified Jacobian of a reduced,
irreducible projective curve with planar singularities.

\begin{corollary}
The regular part $\mathcal{M}_v^\mathrm{reg}(a)$ of the affine Springer fiber $\mathcal{M}_v(a)$ is dense.
\end{corollary}

One constructs a global situation from the local one as in \cf\ \ref{lemme fondamental}.
Then the local assertion follows from the global one via the product formula. (We omit further details as this corollary will not be used later in the article.)

\begin{corollary}
For every $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, $\mathcal{M}_a$ is equidimensional of dimension
$$
\frac{\sharp\,\Phi\,\deg(D)}{2}+r(g-1).
$$
Moreover, the set of irreducible components of $\mathcal{M}_a$ is identified with the group $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_a)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The dimension formula follows from Corollary~\ref{dim fibre}. The identification of the set of irreducible components of $\mathcal{M}_a$
with the group $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_a)$ is enabled by the Kostant section.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
If $\deg(D)>2g-2$, the morphism $f^\heartsuit:\mathcal{M}^\heartsuit\rightarrow \mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ is flat of relative dimension $d$.
Its fibers are geometrically reduced.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \ref{lisse}, both $\mathcal{M}^\heartsuit$ and $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ are smooth over $k$.
To prove that $f$ is flat, it suffices to show that the dimensions of the fibers satisfy
$$
\dim(\mathcal{M}_a)=\dim(\mathcal{M})-\dim(\mathcal{A}).
$$
This follows from the equalities $\dim(\mathcal{M}_a)=\dim(\mathcal{P}_a)$ and $\dim(\mathcal{M})=\dim(\mathcal{P})$,
and since for $\mathcal{P}$ smooth over $\mathcal{A}$ (see \ref{P lisse}) we have
$$
\dim(\mathcal{P}^\heartsuit)=\dim(\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit)+\dim(\mathcal{P}_a).
$$
As in the proof of Proposition~\ref{densite globale}, the fiber $\mathcal{M}_a$, being locally a complete intersection and admitting a dense smooth open subset,
must be reduced.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Case of Endoscopic Groups}\label{subsection : Fibration de Hitchin de groupes endoscopiques}

Let $(\kappa,\rho_\kappa)$ be an endoscopic datum for $G$ over $X$ (see \ref{donnee endoscopique}).
Let $H$ be the associated endoscopic group. As in \S\ref{subsection : Transfert des classes},
there is a morphism $\nu:\mathfrak{c}_H\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}$. Twisting by $D$, one obtains a morphism
$$
\nu:\mathfrak{c}_{H,D}\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}_D.
$$
Taking $X$-valued points, we obtain a morphism (still denoted by)
$$
\nu:\mathcal{A}_H\rightarrow \mathcal{A}.
$$
It is known (see \cite[7.2]{N}) that the restriction of this morphism to $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ is finite and unramified.

\begin{numero}\label{codim A_H}
Set
$$
r_H^G(D)=\frac{\bigl(|\Phi|-|\Phi_H|\bigr)\deg(D)}{2}.
$$
By Lemma~\ref{dim A}, we have
$$
\dim(\mathcal{A})-\dim(\mathcal{A}_H)=r_H^G(D),
$$
so that the image of $\mathcal{A}_H^{G-\heartsuit}=\nu^{-1}(\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit)$ in $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$
is a closed subscheme of codimension $r_H^G(D)$.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}
Over $\mathcal{A}_H$, there is the Hitchin fibration for the group $H$
$$
f_H:\mathcal{M}_H\rightarrow \mathcal{A}_H.
$$
There is also the Picard stack $\mathcal{P}_H\rightarrow \mathcal{A}_H$ acting on $\mathcal{M}_H$.
There is no direct relation between $\mathcal{M}$ and $\mathcal{M}_H$, but $\mathcal{P}$ and $\mathcal{P}_H$
are simply related. Let $a_H\in\mathcal{A}_H(\bar{k})$ with image $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$.
The homomorphism $\mu:\nu^* J\rightarrow J_H$ from \ref{J JH} induces a homomorphism $J_a\rightarrow J_{H,a_H}$ which
is generically an isomorphism. Thus, one obtains a surjective homomorphism
$$
\mathcal{P}_a\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_{H,a_H}
$$
with kernel
$$
\mathcal{R}_{H,a_H}^G=\operatorname{H}^0\Bigl(\bar X,\frac{J_{H,a_H}}{J_a}\Bigr),
$$
which is an affine group of dimension
$$
\dim(\mathcal{R}_{H,a_H}^G)=\dim(\mathcal{P}_a)-\dim(\mathcal{P}_{H,a_H}).
$$
According to Corollary~\ref{dim fibre}, we also have
$$
\dim(\mathcal{R}_{H,a_H}^G)=\frac{\bigl(|\Phi|-|\Phi_H|\bigr)\deg(D)}{2}=r_H^G(D).
$$
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}\label{delta-dellta_H}
Let $J_{H,a_H}^\flat$ be the N\'eron model of $J_{H,a_H}$. We have homomorphisms
$$
J_a\rightarrow J_{H,a_H}\rightarrow J_{H,a_H}^\flat,
$$
which are isomorphisms over a nonempty open of $\bar X$. Hence, $J_{H,a_H}^\flat$ is also the N\'eron model of $J_a$.
Combining with (\ref{R_a}), we obtain the exact sequence
$$
1\rightarrow \mathcal{R}_{H,a_H}^G\rightarrow \mathcal{R}_a\rightarrow \mathcal{R}_{H,a_H}\rightarrow 1.
$$
We deduce the dimension formula
$$
\delta_a-\delta_{H,a_H}=r_H^G(D),
$$
where $\delta_a=\dim(\mathcal{R}_a)$ and $\delta_{H,a_H}=\dim(\mathcal{R}_{H,a_H})$ are the $\delta$-invariants for $G$ and $H$, respectively.
\end{numero}

\subsection{The Case of Paired Groups}\label{Hitchin apparies}

Let $G_1$ and $G_2$ be two $X$-group schemes that are paired in the sense of \ref{apparies}.
Then there is an isomorphism $\mathfrak{c}_{G_1}\cong \mathfrak{c}_{G_2}$ (see \ref{c_G_1=c_G_2}).
This yields an isomorphism $\mathfrak{c}_{G_1,D}\cong \mathfrak{c}_{G_2,D}$ and thus an isomorphism between the bases of the Hitchin fibrations,
$$
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_1=\mathcal{A}_2.
$$
There is no direct relation between the fibrations
$$
f_1:\mathcal{M}_1\rightarrow \mathcal{A}_1 \quad\text{and}\quad f_2:\mathcal{M}_2\rightarrow \mathcal{A}_2
$$
of $G_1$ and $G_2$, but there is a relation between the associated Picard stacks $\mathcal{P}_1$ and $\mathcal{P}_2$.

\begin{proposition}\label{isogenie P1 P2}
There exists a homomorphism between the $\mathcal{A}$-Picard stacks
$$
\mathcal{P}_1\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_2,
$$
which induces an isogeny between their neutral components.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
As in the proof of \ref{c_G_1=c_G_2}, there is an isomorphism $\mathfrak{t}_1\cong \mathfrak{t}_2$
over the isomorphism $\mathfrak{c}_{G_1}\cong \mathfrak{c}_{G_2}$. By \ref{J=J'}, we deduce an isomorphism
between the neutral components $J_{G_1}^0\cong J_{G_2}^0$ of the regular centralizers of $G_1$ and $G_2$.
Since $J_{G_1}$ and $J_{G_2}$ are group schemes of finite type, by composing the isomorphism $J_{G_1}^0\cong J_{G_2}^0$
with multiplication by a sufficiently divisible integer $N$, one obtains a homomorphism $J_{G_1}^0\rightarrow J_{G_2}^0$ which extends to
a homomorphism $J_{G_1}\rightarrow J_{G_2}^0$ with finite kernel and cokernel. This yields a homomorphism between the Picard stacks
$$
\mathcal{P}_1\rightarrow \mathcal{P}_2,
$$
which induces an isogeny between their neutral components.
\end{proof}

% [journal chapter 5] the journal title is simply "Stratification".
\section{Stratification}
\label{section : stratification}

In the remainder of this paper we will need two stratifications of the Hitchin base $\mathcal{A}$, one relative to the group of connected components of the fiber $\mathcal{P}_a$ and the other relative to the invariant $\delta_a$, which is, in some sense, the dimension of the affine part of $\mathcal{P}_a$. These stratifications exist by general semicontinuity arguments. The purpose of this chapter is to understand how these two invariants relate to the cameral curve and to render the construction of these stratifications more explicit.

The basic observation is as follows: since these two invariants are completely determined by the cameral cover $\widetilde X_a\rightarrow \bar X$, in the case where the normalizations of the cameral curve vary in families the invariants $\delta_a$ and $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_a)$ are locally constant. We will therefore define the stratification by family normalization having, over each stratum, a cameral curve which normalizes in family after a radicial base change of the stratum. This stratification is then finer than the stratifications relative to $\delta_a$ and to $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_a)$. We will begin by discussing the case of $\operatorname{GL}(r)$, where one is led to the well-known problem of family normalization of curves on a surface.

A key result of this chapter is Proposition~\ref{codimension} which gives a lower bound for the codimension of the stratum $\mathcal{A}_\delta$ under a hypothesis on $\deg(D)$. In characteristic~$0$, one has a proof of this inequality based on the symplectic nature of the Hitchin fibration without resorting to the hypothesis on $\deg(D)$. We will present that argument in another paper where more emphasis is placed on characteristic~$0$. In positive characteristic that argument does not work. Here is how we will circumvent the obstacle. Goresky, Kottwitz, and MacPherson have computed similar codimensions in the local setting (see \cite{GKM-codim}). The global calculation reduces to the local calculation provided that $\deg(D)$ is large.

We will introduce an \'etale open subset $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ of $\mathcal{A}$ over which $\pi_0(\mathcal{P})$ becomes a quotient of a constant sheaf. With this extra rigidity, the endoscopic decomposition that will be studied in the next chapter will have a pleasant form over $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$.

\subsection{Family Normalizations of Spectral Curves}
In this chapter we introduce a stratification of the Hitchin base $\mathcal{A}$ adapted to the Picard stack $\mathcal{P}$. The general case will be modeled on the case of the linear group, which we now discuss.

In the case $G=\operatorname{GL}(r)$, one may associate to every point 
$$a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$$ 
a reduced curve $Y_a$ drawn on the total space of the line bundle $D$ (see \ref{Cas lineaire}). The symmetry group $\mathcal{P}_a$ is then the Picard stack $\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a)$ of invertible $\mathcal{O}_{Y_a}$-modules. The structure of $\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a)$ can be analyzed using the normalization of $Y_a$. Let 
$$\xi:Y_a^\flat\rightarrow Y_a$$ 
be the normalization of $Y_a$. The functor
$$\mathcal{L}\mapsto \xi^*\mathcal{L},$$
which assigns to every invertible $\mathcal{O}_{Y_a}$-module its pull-back via $\xi$, defines a homomorphism 
$$\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a)\rightarrow \operatorname{Pic}(Y_a^\flat).$$
The long exact sequence in cohomology associated with the short exact sequence of sheaves on $Y_a$
$$
1\rightarrow \mathcal{O}_{Y_a}^\times \rightarrow \xi_*\mathcal{O}_{Y_a^\flat}^\times \rightarrow \xi_*\mathcal{O}_{Y_a^\flat}^\times/\mathcal{O}_{Y_a}^\times\rightarrow 1
$$
provides the following information. The kernel of $\xi^*$, which consists of the category of invertible $\mathcal{O}_{Y_a}$-modules $\mathcal{L}$ equipped with a trivialization of $\xi^*\mathcal{L}$, is an algebraic group whose set of points is 
$$\operatorname{H}^0(Y_a,\xi_*\mathcal{O}_{Y_a^\flat}^\times/\mathcal{O}_{Y_a}^\times).$$ 
Moreover, the functor $\xi^*$ is essentially surjective.

One can attach two invariants to this situation:
\begin{itemize}
  \item the set $\pi_0(Y_a^\flat)$ of connected components of $Y_a^\flat$;
  \item the integer 
  $$\delta_a=\dim \operatorname{H}^0(Y_a,\xi_*\mathcal{O}_{Y_a^\flat}/\mathcal{O}_{Y_a}),$$ 
  called the Serre $\delta$-invariant.
\end{itemize}
By taking the degree on the components of $Y_a^\flat$, one obtains a homomorphism 
$$\pi_0(\operatorname{Pic}(Y_a))\rightarrow \mathbb{Z}^{\pi_0(Y_a^\flat)}$$ 
which is an isomorphism. The invariant $\delta$ measures the dimension of the affine group $\ker(\xi^*)$.

Teissier introduced in \cite{T} the moduli space of family normalizations of a family of plane curves. First, recall the definition of a family normalization.

\begin{definition}\label{normalisation en famille}
Let $y:Y\rightarrow S$ be a proper, flat morphism with reduced one-dimensional fibers. A family normalization of $Y$ is a proper birational morphism $\xi:Y^\flat\rightarrow Y$ that is an isomorphism over an open subset $U$ of $Y$, which is dense in every fiber of $Y$ over $S$, and such that the composite $y\circ \xi$ is a proper and smooth morphism.
\end{definition}

In a family normalization the invariants $\delta$ and $\pi_0(\mathcal{P})$ remain locally constant.

\begin{proposition}\label{localement constant}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item The direct image $y_*(\xi_*\mathcal{O}_{Y^\flat}/\mathcal{O}_Y)$ is a finitely generated locally free $\mathcal{O}_S$-module.
  \item There exists a sheaf $\pi_0(Y^\flat)$, locally constant for the \'etale topology on $S$, whose fiber at each geometric point $s\in S$ is the set of connected components of $Y^\flat_s$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $s$ be a geometric point of $S$. The restriction of $\xi_*\mathcal{O}_{Y^\flat}/\mathcal{O}_Y$ is supported on $Y_s-U_s$, which is a zero-dimensional scheme. It follows that
$$\operatorname{H}^1(Y_s,\xi_*\mathcal{O}_{Y^\flat}/\mathcal{O}_Y)=0$$
and that 
$$\dim \operatorname{H}^0(Y_s,\xi_*\mathcal{O}_{Y^\flat}/\mathcal{O}_Y)$$ 
is the difference between the arithmetic genus of $Y_s$ and that of $Y_s^\flat$. This is thus a function locally constant in $s$. It remains to apply the base change theorem for coherent sheaves \cite[5, Corollary 2]{Mum}.

The second assertion is a consequence of the fact that $\xi_*\mathbb{Q}_\ell$ is a locally constant sheaf.
\end{proof}

We now restrict ourselves to the case of spectral curves. Let $\mathcal{B}$ be the moduli space of family normalizations of the spectral curves $Y_a$. It associates to every $k$-scheme $S$ the groupoid $\mathcal{B}(S)$ of triples $(a,Y_a^\flat,\xi)$ where $a\in \mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(S)$ is an $S$-point of $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$, where $Y_a^\flat$ is a smooth projective curve over $S$, and where $\xi:Y_a^\flat\rightarrow Y_a$ is a family normalization of the spectral curve $Y_a$ associated to $a$. The functor $\mathcal{B}$ is representable by a $k$-scheme of finite type.

The forgetful morphism $\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ induces a bijection on $\bar{k}$-points. Indeed, for every $a\in \mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, the normalization $Y_a^\flat$ of $Y_a$ is uniquely determined. However, $\mathcal{B}$ has more connected components than $\mathcal{A}$. In fact, the two invariants $\pi_0(\widetilde Y_a)$ and $\delta_a$ are locally constant by Proposition~\ref{localement constant}. Let $\Psi$ be the set of connected components of $\mathcal{B}\otimes_k \bar{k}$. For every $\psi\in \Psi$, there exists an integer $\delta(\psi)$ equal to $\delta_a$ for every $a\in\mathcal{B}_\psi(\bar{k})$.

% [journal 5.1] REMOVED: this proposition has no counterpart in the journal's 5.1 (which
% has only the definition and one proposition) and is cited nowhere in the document.

Teissier \cite{T}, Diaz and Harris \cite{DH}, Fantechi, G\"ottsche, and Van Straten \cite{FGV}, and Laumon \cite{L} have proved the local analogue of this result. The passage from local to global currently requires the hypothesis on $\deg(D)$. We will use this result only as a guide. From the following section on, the letters $\mathcal{B}$, $\Psi$, and $\psi$ will take on slightly different meanings.

\subsection{Family Normalization of Cameral Curves}
\label{foncteur B}

In the case of classical groups one still has spectral curves \cite{H}, \cite{compagnon}. However, with these spectral curves one is forced to work case by case. We will in fact study the general case by examining the moduli space of normalizations of the cameral curves endowed with the $W$-action. This will even lead in the case of $\operatorname{GL}(r)$ to a different stratification, finer than that defined by the moduli space of normalizations of the spectral curves.

Let $S$ be a $k$-scheme. An $S$-point $a$ of $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ defines a morphism
$$a:X\times S\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}_D.$$ 
By taking the inverse image of the covering $\pi:\mathfrak{t}_D\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}_D$, one obtains a finite flat cover $\widetilde X_a$ of $X\times S$ which in each fiber is generically a $W$-torsor.

Consider the functor $\mathcal{B}$ which assigns to every $k$-scheme $S$ the groupoid of triples $(a,\widetilde X_a^\flat,\xi)$ where:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $a\in \mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(S)$ is an $S$-point of $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$.
  \item $\widetilde X_a^\flat$ is a proper smooth curve over $S$, endowed with a $W$-action and a morphism $\pi_a^\flat:\widetilde X_a^\flat\rightarrow X\times S$ that is finite, flat, and, fiberwise, generically a $W$-torsor.
  \item $\xi:\widetilde X_a^\flat\rightarrow \widetilde X_a$ is a $W$-equivariant birational morphism which is a family normalization (see Definition~\ref{normalisation en famille}) over an open subset $U$ of $Y$ that is dense in every fiber.
\end{itemize}

Let $b=(a,\widetilde X_a^\flat,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}(S)$ be a point of $\mathcal{B}$ over a connected $S$. Let $\operatorname{pr}_S:X\times S\rightarrow S$ be the projection. By Definition~\ref{normalisation en famille}, the direct image $(\operatorname{pr}_S)_*(\xi_*\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde X_a^\flat}/\mathcal{O}_{X_a^\flat})$ is a finitely generated locally free $\mathcal{O}_S$-module. The same holds for 
$$((\operatorname{pr}_S)_*(\xi_*\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde X_a^\flat}/\mathcal{O}_{X_a^\flat})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_X} \mathfrak{t})^W$$ 
under the hypothesis that the order of $\mathbb{W}$ is prime to the characteristic of $k$. Since $S$ is connected, this locally free $\mathcal{O}_S$-module has a constant rank which we denote by $\delta(b)$.

For every $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar{k})$, the cameral curve $\widetilde X_a$ has a unique normalization $\widetilde X_a^\flat$, which is then a smooth curve over $\bar{k}$. It follows that the forgetful morphism $\mathcal{B}\rightarrow \mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ induces a bijection on $\bar{k}$-points. Moreover, it induces an injection on points over any field by the uniqueness of normalization. In characteristic $p$ and over a non-perfect field, the normalization may fail to be smooth so that the above map may not be surjective when evaluated on a non-perfect field.

% [journal 5.2.1] this is a Proposition in the journal, not a Theorem
\begin{proposition}\label{representable}
The functor $\mathcal{B}$ defined above is representable by a $k$-scheme of finite type.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Consider the functor $\mathcal{B}'$ which assigns to every scheme $S$ the set of pairs $(\widetilde X_a^\flat, \gamma)$ where:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $\widetilde X_a^\flat$ is a proper smooth curve over $S$, endowed with a $W$-action and a morphism $\pi_a^\flat:\widetilde X_a^\flat\rightarrow X\times S$ as above.
  \item $\gamma:\widetilde X_a^\flat \rightarrow \mathfrak{t}_D \times S$ is a $W$-equivariant morphism such that the inverse image of the regular semisimple locus of $\mathfrak{t}_D$ is dense in each fiber.
\end{itemize}
Let $\mathcal{H}$ be the functor that assigns to every scheme $S$ the set of isomorphism classes of smooth projective curves $\widetilde X_a^\flat$ over $S$, endowed with a $W$-action and a morphism $\pi_a^\flat:\widetilde X_a^\flat\rightarrow X\times S$ as above. This functor is representable by a quasi-projective $k$-scheme. The morphism 
$$h:\mathcal{B}'\rightarrow \mathcal{H}$$ 
is also representable so that $\mathcal{B}'$ is representable by a quasi-projective $k$-scheme.

Moreover, there is a morphism $\mathcal{B}\rightarrow \mathcal{B}'$. It sends a point $b=(a,\widetilde X_a^\flat,\xi)\in\mathcal{B}(S)$ to the point $b'=(\widetilde X_a^\flat, \gamma)$, where $\gamma$ is the composition of $\xi:\widetilde X_a^\flat\rightarrow \widetilde X_a$ with the closed immersion $\widetilde X_a\rightarrow \mathfrak{t}_D\times S$. To prove the representability of $\mathcal{B}$, it suffices to verify the following assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
The morphism $\mathcal{B}\rightarrow \mathcal{B}'$ is an isomorphism.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
To show that the morphism $\mathcal{B}\rightarrow \mathcal{B}'$ is an isomorphism, we will construct an inverse. Let $b'=(\widetilde X_a^\flat, \gamma)\in\mathcal{B}'(S)$. Consider the $W$-invariant part of the push-forward $(\pi_a^\flat)_*\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde X_a^\flat}$. This is a finite $\mathcal{O}_{X\times S}$-algebra whose fiber in each $S$-fiber is generically isomorphic to $\mathcal{O}_X$. Since $X$ is normal, it follows that
$$((\pi_a^\flat)_*\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde X_a^\flat})^{W}=\mathcal{O}_{X\times S}.$$
Using the equality $k[\mathbb{t}]^{\mathbb{W}}=\mathbb{c}$ (see \ref{Chevalley}), the $W$-equivariant morphism $\gamma:\widetilde X_a^\flat\rightarrow \mathfrak{t}_D$ induces a morphism 
$$a:X\times S\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}_D.$$ 
Let $\widetilde X_a$ be the cameral curve associated to $a$. The morphism $\gamma$ then factors through a morphism 
$$\xi:\widetilde X_a^\flat\rightarrow \widetilde X_a,$$
which fiberwise over $S$ is a normalization of $X_a$. Thus we have constructed a point 
$$b=(a,\widetilde X_a^\flat,\xi)\in \mathcal{B}(S).$$ 
The morphism $\mathcal{B}'\rightarrow\mathcal{B}$ constructed in this way is inverse to the morphism $\mathcal{B}\rightarrow \mathcal{B}'$.
\end{proof}

Let $\Psi$ be the set of connected components of $\mathcal{B}\otimes_k \bar{k}$. For every $\psi\in\Psi$, let $\mathcal{B}_\psi$ be the reduced connected component of $\mathcal{B}\otimes_k \bar{k}$ indexed by $\psi$. The Galois group $\operatorname{Gal}(\bar{k}/k)$ acts on the finite set $\Psi$. For every $\psi\in \Psi$, let $\mathcal{A}_\psi$ be the image of $\mathcal{B}_\psi$ in $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit\otimes_k \bar{k}$. A priori, $\mathcal{A}_\psi$ is a constructible subset by Chevalley's theorem; in fact, it is a locally closed subset.

% [journal 5.2] The arXiv version closed 5.2 with a Zariski-main-theorem construction of a
% stratification of A^heartsuit indexed by Psi, plus two lemmas. The journal has no such
% material here: it builds the stratification on Atilde in 5.3 instead (now translated
% there). Those three statements were cited nowhere and clashed with 5.3 over the symbol
% Psi, so they are removed; they are kept at ch5/OLD_52_arxiv_extras.tex.

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
% [journal chapter 5] The arXiv order was 5.1, 5.2, delta-constant, radicial valuations,
% etale open Atilde, monodromic invariants, pi_0(P). The journal order is
% 5.1, 5.2, 5.3 etale open Atilde, 5.4 monodromic invariants, 5.5 pi_0(P),
% 5.6 delta-constant, 5.7 radicial valuations. Reordered accordingly.
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
% [journal 5.3 - 5.5] RE-TRANSLATED FROM THE JOURNAL (pp. 93-99).
% The arXiv version built these three paragraphs on a different apparatus: a chosen
% reduction rho : X_rho -> X under a discrete group Theta_rho, an open A^infty, and a
% descent of Pi, Pi' to A^infty. The journal instead works with rho_G^bullet :
% pi_1(X,infty) -> Out(G), with Theta the image of the GEOMETRIC pi_1, and defines the
% monodromic invariants (I_atilde, W_atilde) directly. The whole anisotropic thread
% (5.4.7, and the last sentence of 5.5.4) was absent from the arXiv text.

\subsection{Stratification of the \'Etale Open Subset $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$}
\label{subsection : tilde A}

In the remainder of the article we shall not work directly on the Hitchin base $\mathcal{A}$, but on an \'etale open subset of it. This choice detracts from our claim to geometrize the stabilization of the trace formula \ref{Formule globale de stabilisation}, but it rids us of a number of incidental difficulties.

\begin{numero}\label{tilde A def}
Let $\infty\in X(\bar k)$. Consider the \'etale open subset $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ of $\mathcal{A}\otimes_k\bar k$ whose points are the pairs $(a,\tilde\infty)$ with $a\in\mathcal{A}$ such that the cameral cover $\widetilde X_a\rightarrow X$ is \'etale above $\infty$, and where $\tilde\infty$ is a point of $\widetilde X_a$ above $\infty$. Note that if $\infty\in X(k)$, then $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ has an evident $k$-structure.

The construction above can be reformulated diagrammatically as follows. The choice of the point $\infty\in X(\bar k)$ defines a morphism $\mathcal{A}\otimes_k\bar k\rightarrow \mathfrak{c}_{D,\infty}$, where $\mathfrak{c}_{D,\infty}$ is the fiber of $\mathfrak{c}_D$ above $\infty$, which assigns to $a\in\mathcal{A}$ the point $a(\infty)$. Let $\mathcal{A}^\infty$ denote the inverse image of the regular semisimple open subset $\mathfrak{c}^\rs_{D,\infty}$ of $\mathfrak{c}_{D,\infty}$. We then in fact have a cartesian diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
\tilde{\mathcal{A}} \ar[d] \ar[r] & \mathfrak{t}_{D,\infty} \ar[d] \\
\mathcal{A}^\infty \ar[r] & \mathfrak{c}_{D,\infty}
}
$$
which makes $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ a $\mathbb{W}_\infty$-torsor over $\mathcal{A}^\infty$, where $\mathbb{W}_\infty$ is the fiber above $\infty$ of the finite \'etale $X$-group scheme $\mathbb{W}$.
\end{numero}

\begin{lemma}\label{connexe tilde A}
Suppose that $\deg(D)>2g$. Then $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ is smooth and geometrically irreducible.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Under the hypothesis $\deg(D)>2g$, the linear map $\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathfrak{c}_{D,\infty}$ in the diagram above is surjective \cf \ref{deg(D)>2g}. We deduce that the upper arrow of the diagram is a smooth morphism with connected fibers. Since $\mathfrak{t}_{D,\infty}$ is a vector space, $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ is smooth and geometrically irreducible.
\end{proof}

We now form the cartesian diagram
$$\tilde{\mathcal{B}}=\mathcal{B}\times_{\mathcal{A}}\tilde{\mathcal{A}},$$
where $\mathcal{B}$ is the moduli space of family normalizations of cameral curves \cf \ref{representable}. This morphism defines a bijection at the level of geometric points. The image of a connected component of $\tilde{\mathcal{B}}\otimes_k\bar k$ in $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\otimes_k\bar k$ is a constructible subset. A constructible subset is by definition a finite union of irreducible locally closed subsets. Let $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}'$ be one of these irreducible locally closed subsets and $\tilde{\mathcal{B}}'$ its inverse image. The morphism $\tilde{\mathcal{B}}'\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{A}}'$ induces a bijection at the level of geometric points; in particular it is quasi-finite. Applying Zariski's main theorem, one sees that there exists a dense open subset $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}''$ of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}'$ above which the morphism $\tilde{\mathcal{B}}'\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{A}}'$ is finite and radicial. Subdividing further and proceeding by noetherian induction, one obtains a stratification into irreducible locally closed subsets
\begin{equation}\label{stratification tilde A}
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\otimes_k\bar k=\bigsqcup_{\psi\in\Psi}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi
\end{equation}
such that, if $\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_\psi$ denotes the inverse image of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ in $\tilde{\mathcal{B}}$, the morphism $\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_\psi\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ is a finite radicial morphism.

Subdividing yet further if necessary, we may assume that the closure of a stratum $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ is a union of other strata. This makes it possible to define a partial order relation on the set $\Psi$ of strata. Since $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ is geometrically irreducible, the set $\Psi$ admits a maximal element, which we shall denote by $\psi_G$.

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Monodromic Invariants}\label{subsection : stratification monodromique}

We shall now construct a monodromic invariant attached to each stratum of the stratification \ref{stratification tilde A}.

Recall that the form $G$ of $\mathbb{G}$ is given by a homomorphism $\rho_G^\bullet:\pi_1(X,\infty)\rightarrow\Out(\mathbb{G})$. Let $\Theta$ denote the image of the geometric fundamental group $\pi_1(\bar X,\infty)$ in $\Out(\mathbb{G})$, which is a subgroup of finite order.

\begin{numero}\label{WIPHI}
Let $\tilde a=(a,\tilde\infty)\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\bar k)$. Let $U$ be the maximal open subset of $\bar X$ above which the cameral cover $\widetilde X_a\rightarrow\bar X$ is \'etale; it contains $\infty$ in particular. By \ref{deploiement}, we have a commutative diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
\pi_1(U,\infty) \ar[d] \ar[r]^-{\pi_a^\bullet} & \mathbb{W}\rtimes\Out(\mathbb{G}) \ar[d] \\
\pi_1(\bar X,x) \ar[r]_-{\rho_G^\bullet} & \Out(\mathbb{G})
}
$$
Let $W_{\tilde a}$ denote the image of $\pi_a^\bullet$ in $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Out(\mathbb{G})$, and let $I_{\tilde a}$ be the image of the kernel of the homomorphism $\pi_1(U,\infty)\rightarrow\pi_1(\bar X,\infty)$. By construction, $W_{\tilde a}$ is contained in the finite subgroup $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$, and $I_{\tilde a}$ is a normal subgroup of $W_{\tilde a}$ contained in $W_{\tilde a}\cap\mathbb{W}$.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}\label{WI revetements}
Let us restate the definition above in the language of covers. Let $X_\rho\rightarrow\bar X$ be the connected finite \'etale Galois cover with Galois group $\Theta$ corresponding to the surjective homomorphism
$$\rho_G^\bullet:\pi_1(\bar X,\infty)\rightarrow\Theta.$$
By construction it carries a point $\infty_\rho$ above $\infty$. Form the cartesian product
$$\widetilde X_{\rho,a}=\widetilde X_a\times_X X_\rho$$
of the cameral curve $\widetilde X_a$ with the finite \'etale cover $X_\rho\rightarrow X$. The curve $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}$ is then equipped with an action of $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$, and so is its normalization $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}^\flat$. Let $C_{\tilde a}$ be the connected component of $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}^\flat$ containing $\tilde\infty_\rho=(\tilde\infty,\infty_\rho)$. The group $W_{\tilde a}$ is then the subgroup of $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ consisting of the elements that leave this component stable. The group $I_{\tilde a}$ is then the subgroup generated by the elements of $W_{\tilde a}$ admitting at least one fixed point in $C_{\tilde a}$. Since the projection of $C_{\tilde a}$ onto $X_\rho$, on which the group $\Theta_\rho$ acts freely, $I_{\tilde a}$ is contained in the kernel of the projection $W_{\tilde a}\rightarrow\Theta$. It follows also that $I_{\tilde a}\subset W_{\tilde a}\cap\mathbb{W}$.
\end{numero}

\begin{proposition}\label{IW constant}
% [journal 5.4.3] the journal prints "sur chaque strate \tilde A_\rho"; the stratification
% \ref{stratification tilde A} is indexed by \psi, so this must read \tilde A_\psi.
The map $\tilde a\mapsto (I_{\tilde a},W_{\tilde a})$ is constant on each stratum $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ of the stratification \ref{stratification tilde A}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By construction of the stratification \ref{stratification tilde A}, the morphism $\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_\psi\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ is a finite radicial morphism. Above the connected scheme $\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_\psi$, the normalization $\widetilde X_a^\flat\rightarrow\widetilde X_a$ of the cameral curve goes over into families, as does the whole construction preceding the statement of this proposition. We therefore have a proper smooth relative curve
$$\widetilde X_{\rho,\psi}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_\psi$$
equipped with an action of $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ and with a section $\tilde\infty_\rho$. As in Lemma \ref{localement constant}, there exists a locally constant sheaf $\pi_0(\widetilde X_{\rho,\psi}/\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_\psi)$ for the \'etale topology of $\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_\psi$ which interpolates the sets of irreducible components of the fibers of $\widetilde X_{\rho,\psi}/\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_\psi$. The group $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ acts on this sheaf and induces a transitive action on its fibers. The datum of the section deduced from $\tilde\infty_\rho$ implies that this sheaf is constant. The proposition follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{numero}\label{stratification tilde A I W}
It follows from the preceding proposition that there is a map
$$\psi\mapsto (I_\psi,W_\psi)$$
defined on the set $\Psi$ of strata of the stratification \ref{stratification tilde A}, compatible with the map $\tilde a\mapsto (I_{\tilde a},W_{\tilde a})$ defined at the level of geometric points.
\end{numero}

\begin{lemma}\label{I W croissant}
Consider the set of pairs $(I_1,W_1)$ consisting of a subgroup $W_1$ of $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ and a normal subgroup $I_1$ of $W_1$, and the partial order on it defined by $(I_1,W_1)\leq (I_2,W_2)$ if and only if $W_1\subset W_2$ and $I_1\subset I_2$. Then the map $\psi\mapsto (I_\psi,W_\psi)$ is an increasing map.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $S=\Spec(R)$ be a formal trait with generic point $\eta=\Spec(k(\eta))$ and geometric closed point $s=\Spec(k(s))$. Let $\tilde a:S\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ be a morphism with $\tilde a(\eta)\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ and $\tilde a(s)\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\psi'}$. We must show that
$$(I_{\psi'},W_{\psi'})\leq (I_\psi,W_\psi).$$

Consider the cover $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}$ of $X\times S$ defined as the inverse image under $a$ of the cover $X_\rho\times\mathfrak{t}_D\rightarrow\mathfrak{c}_D$. Consider the normalization $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}^\flat$ of $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}$. After possibly making a radicial base change of the trait, we may assume that the generic fiber $(\widetilde X_{\rho,a}^\flat)_\eta$ is a smooth curve over $k(\eta)$, so that $(I_\psi,W_\psi)$ can be computed from this generic fiber. By contrast, the special fiber $(\widetilde X_{\rho,a}^\flat)_s$ is not normal in general, and one must take its normalization $(\widetilde X_{\rho,a}^\flat)_s^\flat$ in order to compute $(I_{\psi'},W_{\psi'})$.

The point $\tilde a$ defines a section of $(\widetilde X_{\rho,a}^\flat)_\eta$. Let $C_{\tilde a}$ be the connected component of $(\widetilde X_{\rho,a}^\flat)_\eta$ containing this section. The group $W_\psi$ is then the subgroup of $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ formed by the elements that leave this component stable. Let $C_{\tilde a(s)}$ be the connected component of $(\widetilde X_{\rho,a}^\flat)_s^\flat$ containing the point defined by $\tilde a(s)$. The group $W_{\psi'}$ is the subgroup of $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ formed by the elements that leave $C_{\tilde a(s)}$ stable. Since $C_{\tilde a(s)}$ is a connected component of the normalization of the special fiber of $C_{\tilde a}$, we have the inclusion $W_{\psi'}\subset W_\psi$. An element of $W_{\psi'}$ having a fixed point in $C_{\tilde a(s)}$ necessarily has a fixed point in $C_{\tilde a}$, whence the second inclusion $I_{\psi'}\subset I_\psi$.
\end{proof}

\begin{numero}\label{stratification monodromique}
For every pair $(I_-,W_-)$ consisting of a subgroup $W_-$ of $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ and a normal subgroup $I_-$ of $W_-$ contained in $\mathbb{W}$, it follows from this lemma that the union of the strata $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ with $W_\psi\subset W_-$ and $I_\psi\subset I_-$ is a closed subscheme of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$. It follows also that the union of the strata $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ with $W_\psi=W_-$ and $I_\psi=I_-$ is an open subset of the closed subscheme just mentioned. We shall write $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{(I_-,W_-)}$ for this locally closed subscheme of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$. We then have the stratification
$$\tilde{\mathcal{A}}=\bigsqcup_{(I_-,W_-)}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{(I_-,W_-)}.$$
Recall that if $\infty\in X(k)$, then $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ is defined over $k$, but the stratification above is not necessarily defined over $k$.
\end{numero}

\begin{numero}\label{ouvert ani tilde A}
% [journal 5.4.7] ABSENT from the arXiv version. This is what identifies the anisotropic
% open subset with the locus where the invariants of the Lie algebra vanish, and it is
% cited from 4.10.5 and from chapter 6.
It follows also from Lemma \ref{I W croissant} that the union of the strata $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ with $\psi\in\Psi$ such that $\mathfrak{t}^{W_\psi}=0$ is an open subset of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$. By \ref{pi 0 global}, a point $\tilde a=(a,\tilde\infty)\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\bar k)$ lies in this open subset if and only if $a$ belongs to the set $\mathcal{A}^\ani(\bar k)$ defined in \ref{A ani def}. The stratification of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ induces on $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^\ani$ a stratification
$$\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^\ani=\bigsqcup_{\psi\in\Psi^\ani}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi,$$
where $\Psi^\ani$ is a subset of $\Psi$. If $\infty\in X(k)$, then $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^\ani$ is defined over $k$, but the stratification above is not necessarily defined over $k$.
\end{numero}

\begin{lemma}\label{I W psi_G}
Suppose that $\deg(D)>2g$. Let $\psi_G$ be the maximal element of $\Psi$. Then
$$(I_{\psi_G},W_{\psi_G})=(\mathbb{W},\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta).$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By construction, $W_\psi\subset\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ and $I_\psi\subset\mathbb{W}$. It therefore suffices to show that this bound is attained. We shall show that it is attained at a point $\tilde a=(a,\tilde\infty)$ with $a$ in the open subset $\mathcal{A}^\diamondsuit$, which we have seen is non-empty under the hypothesis $\deg(D)>2g$ \cf \ref{A diamond non vide}.

By \ref{image pi a diamond} we already know that $W_{\tilde a}=\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ if $a\in\mathcal{A}^\diamondsuit$. The subgroup $I_{\tilde a}$ is then a normal subgroup of $\mathbb{W}$. Since the curve $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}$ meets transversally all the walls of $h_a$ in $X_\rho\times\mathfrak{t}$ associated with the roots $\alpha$ of $\mathfrak{g}$, the group $I_{\tilde a}$ is a normal subgroup of $\mathbb{W}$ containing all the reflections $s_\alpha$ associated with the walls $h_\alpha$. It follows that $I_{\tilde a}=\mathbb{W}$.
\end{proof}

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Description of $\pi_0(\mathcal{P})$}\label{subsection : description pi_0(P)}

In this paragraph we shall describe the sheaf $\pi_0(\mathcal{P})$ along the strata $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{(I_-,W_-)}$ of the stratification \ref{stratification monodromique}. Let us recall the definition of this sheaf. Since the Picard stack $\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ is smooth, there exists a unique sheaf $\pi_0(\mathcal{P})$ for the \'etale topology of $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ whose fiber at a point $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar k)$ is the group $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_a)$ of connected components of $\mathcal{P}_a$. This is a consequence of a theorem of Grothendieck \cf \cite[15.6.4]{EGA4}; see also \cite[6.2]{N}.

We shall also consider the intermediate problem of determining the sheaf $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}')$ of connected components of the Picard stack $\mathcal{P}'$ whose fiber at each point $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar k)$ classifies the $J_a^0$-torsors on $\bar X$. The surjective homomorphism $\mathcal{P}'\rightarrow\mathcal{P}$ induces a surjective homomorphism $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}')\rightarrow\pi_0(\mathcal{P})$. Let $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}$ and $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}'$ denote the restrictions of $\mathcal{P}$ and $\mathcal{P}'$ to $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$.

By \ref{pi 0 global}, for every point $\tilde a\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\bar k)$ the fiber of $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}'_{\tilde a})$ admits the description
$$\pi_0(\mathcal{P}'_a)=(\hat{\mathbb{T}}^{W_{\tilde a}})^*,$$
where the exponent $(\_)^*$ denotes the duality between finitely generated abelian groups and diagonalizable groups of finite type over $\Ql$. Likewise, $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}_a)$ is identified with the quotient of $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}'_a)$ dual to the subgroup $\hat{\mathbb{T}}(I_{\tilde a},W_{\tilde a})$ defined in \ref{pi 0 global}. The following statement makes it possible to determine the sheaves $\pi_0(\tilde{\mathcal{P}}')$ and $\pi_0(\tilde{\mathcal{P}})$ completely.

\begin{proposition}\label{surjectif}
The surjective arrows of Proposition \ref{pi 0 global}
$$\mathbb{X}_*\rightarrow\pi_0(\mathcal{P}'_a)=(\mathbb{X}_*)_{W_{\tilde a}},$$
defined for every $\tilde a=(a,\tilde\infty)\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\bar k)$, interpolate into a canonical surjective homomorphism from the constant sheaf $\mathbb{X}_*$ onto $\pi_0(\tilde{\mathcal{P}}')$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $\tilde a=(a,\tilde\infty)\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\bar k)$. The geometric point $\tilde\infty_\rho=(\tilde\infty,\infty_\rho)$ of $\widetilde X_{\rho,a}$ makes it possible to identify the fiber of $J_a$ at $\infty$ with the fixed torus $\mathbb{T}$ \cf \ref{J=J'}. By \cite[6.8]{N}, this identification defines a homomorphism from the constant sheaf $\mathbb{X}_*$ into $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}'$, and hence a homomorphism
$$\mathbb{X}_*\times\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\pi_0(\tilde{\mathcal{P}}').$$
Fiber by fiber this is the surjective homomorphism of \ref{pi 0 global}.
\end{proof}

\begin{numero}\label{petits ouverts}
By means of this lemma one obtains an explicit description of the restrictions of $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}')$ and of $\pi_0(\mathcal{P})$ to $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$. For every \'etale open subset $U$ of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$, the stratification \ref{stratification monodromique} induces on $U$ a stratification
$$U=\bigsqcup_{(I_-,W_-)}U_{(I_-,W_-)}.$$

For every pair $(I_1,W_1)$ consisting of a subgroup $W_1$ of $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ and a normal subgroup $I_1$ of $W_1$, we say that $U$ is a {\em small open subset of type $(I_1,W_1)$} if $U_{(I_1,W_1)}$ is the unique non-empty closed stratum in the stratification above. It is clear that the small open subsets of the various types form a base for the \'etale topology of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$, in the sense that every open subset can be covered by a family of small open subsets. In order to define a sheaf for the \'etale topology of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$, it therefore suffices to specify its sections on the small open subsets together with the transition arrows.

Consider the sheaves $\Pi'$ and $\Pi$, which are quotients of the constant sheaf $\mathbb{X}_*$, defined as follows. For a small open subset $U_1$ of type $(I_1,W_1)$ we set
$$\Gamma(U,\Pi')=(\hat{\mathbb{T}}^{W_1})^*=(\mathbb{X}_*)_{W_1},$$
$$\Gamma(U,\Pi)=\hat{\mathbb{T}}(I_1,W_1)^*.$$
Let $U_2$ be a small \'etale open subset of $U_1$ of type $(I_2,W_2)$. Since $U_{(I_1,W_1)}$ is the unique non-empty closed stratum of $U_1$, we have the inequality
$$(I_1,W_1)\leq (I_2,W_2).$$
We then have an evident inclusion of the subgroups of invariants of $\hat{\mathbb{T}}$ under $W_1$ and $W_2$
$$\hat{\mathbb{T}}^{W_2}\subset\hat{\mathbb{T}}^{W_1},$$
whence the transition arrow
$$\Gamma(U_1,\Pi')\rightarrow\Gamma(U_2,\Pi').$$
The definition of the transition arrows of the sheaf $\Pi$ follows from the lemma below.
\end{numero}

\begin{lemma}\label{T(I,W) inclusion}
If $(I_1,W_1)\leq (I_2,W_2)$, then one has the inclusion
$$\hat{\mathbb{T}}(I_2,W_2)\subset\hat{\mathbb{T}}(I_1,W_1).$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\kappa$ be an element of $\hat{\mathbb{T}}$, let $\hat{\mathbb{G}}_\kappa$ be its centralizer in $\hat{\mathbb{G}}$ and let $\hat{\mathbb{H}}$ be the neutral component of the latter. Let $(\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta)_\kappa$ be the centralizer of $\kappa$ in $\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta$ and let $\mathbb{W}_\HH$ be the Weyl group of $\hat{\mathbb{H}}$. If $\kappa\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}(I_2,W_2)$, then $I_2\subset\mathbb{W}_\HH$ and $W_2\subset(\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta)_\kappa$. We deduce that $I_1\subset\mathbb{W}_\HH$ and $W_1\subset(\mathbb{W}\rtimes\Theta)_\kappa$.
\end{proof}

The following statement is an immediate consequence of \ref{surjectif} and \ref{pi 0 global}.

\begin{corollary}\label{pi}
The surjective homomorphism \ref{surjectif} induces, on passing to the quotient, isomorphisms $\pi_0(\mathcal{P}')|_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}=\Pi'$ and $\pi_0(\mathcal{P})|_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}=\Pi$.

Above the open subset $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^\ani$ of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$ \cf \ref{ouvert ani tilde A}, these are sheaves of finite abelian groups.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{Stratification with Constant $\delta$}

% [journal 5.6] The arXiv version stated this paragraph over A^heartsuit, indexing by the
% connected components of B. The journal states it over Atilde, indexing by the strata of
% the stratification 5.3.3. Converted, so that the four journal lemmas below (5.6.1, 5.6.3,
% 5.6.4, 5.6.6) sit in the setting they are stated in.
For every $a\in\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit(\bar k)$ we defined in \ref{delta global} an integer $\delta(a)$. This induces a function $\tilde a\mapsto\delta(a)$ for $\tilde a=(a,\tilde\infty)\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\bar k)$.

% [journal 5.6.1] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION, which asserted this in passing.
% The journal states and proves it.
\begin{lemma}\label{delta constant sur strate}
The function $\tilde a\mapsto\delta(a)$ is constant on each stratum $\tilde\calA_\psi$, for every $\psi\in\Psi$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
After the finite radicial base change $\tilde\calB_\psi\rightarrow\tilde\calA_\psi$, the restriction of the cameral curve to $\tilde\calB_\psi$ admits a family normalization. Write $\pi_\psi:\widetilde X_\psi\rightarrow\tilde\calB_\psi$ for the restriction of the cameral curve to $\tilde\calB_\psi$, and $\xi:\widetilde X_\psi^\flat\rightarrow\widetilde X_\psi$ for the family normalization. By \ref{localement constant}, the sheaf
$$\pi_{\psi,*}(\xi_*\calO_{\widetilde X_\psi^\flat}/\calO_{\widetilde X_\psi})$$
is a locally constant $\calO_{\tilde\calB_\psi}$-module of finite type. This module is equipped with an action of $\bbW$. Since the order of $\bbW$ is prime to the characteristic,
$$(\calO_{\tilde\calB_\psi}\otimes\frakt)^\bbW$$
is likewise locally constant. We conclude by the formula \ref{delta global}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{delta decroit}
Let $\psi,\psi'\in \Psi$ with $\psi\geq \psi'$. Then $\delta(\psi)\leq \delta(\psi')$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $S=\operatorname{Spec}(R)$ be a formal trait with generic point $\eta=\operatorname{Spec}(k(\eta))$ and special point $s=\operatorname{Spec}(k(s))$ (with $k(s)$ algebraically closed). Let $a:S\rightarrow \mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ be an $S$-point of $\mathcal{A}^\heartsuit$ whose generic point lies in $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ and whose special point lies in $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\psi'}$. Let 
$$\widetilde X_a \rightarrow S\times X$$ 
be the associated cameral cover.

After possibly replacing $\eta$ by an inseparable extension and $S$ by its normalization in that extension, we may assume that the morphism $a:\eta\rightarrow \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi$ factors through $\mathcal{B}_\psi$. Then the normalization $\widetilde X_{a,\eta}^\flat$ of the generic fiber $\widetilde X_{a,\eta}$ is a smooth curve.

Consider the normalization $\widetilde X_a^\flat$ of $\widetilde X_a$ in $\widetilde X_{a,\eta}^\flat$ and denote by $\xi$ the morphism
$$\xi:\widetilde X_a^\flat\rightarrow \widetilde X_a.$$ 
The $R$-module 
$$\operatorname{H}^0(\widetilde X_a,\xi_*\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde X_a^\flat}/\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde X_a})$$ 
is finite and flat over $R$ (since $R$ is a discrete valuation ring, flatness is equivalent to being torsion-free). Denote it by $M$, which is a finite flat $R$-module endowed with an action of $W$. By decomposing $M$ into irreducible representations of $\mathbb{W}$ (since the order of $\mathbb{W}$ is prime to the characteristic), we have
$$\dim_{k(\eta)}\Bigl((\mathfrak{t}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_X} M_{\eta})^{W}\Bigr) =
\dim_{k(s)}\Bigl((\mathfrak{t}\otimes_k M_s)^{W}\Bigr).$$

The special fiber of $\widetilde X_{a,s}^\flat$ of $\widetilde X_a^\flat$ is a partial normalization of $\widetilde X_a$. To compute the invariant $\delta(\psi')$, one must take the full normalization $(\widetilde X_{a,s}^\flat)^\flat$ of $\widetilde X_{a,s}^\flat$. The $k(s)[\mathbb{W}]$-module $M_s$ is then a direct summand of 
$$\operatorname{H}^0\Bigl(\mathcal{O}_{(\widetilde X_{a,s}^\flat)^\flat}/\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde X_{a,s}}\Bigr).$$
We deduce that
$$\delta(\psi')\geq \dim_{k(s)}\Bigl((\mathfrak{t}\otimes_k M_s)^{W}\Bigr),$$
which implies the lemma.
\end{proof}

% [journal 5.6.3] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION. The arXiv text merely cited \cite{SGA7}
% for the semi-continuity of delta; the journal proves it here, and 7.1.5 cites it.
\begin{lemma}\label{delta semicontinu}
Let $P\rightarrow S$ be a smooth commutative group scheme of finite type. The function $s\mapsto \tau_s$, which assigns to a geometric point $s$ the dimension of the abelian part of $P_s$, is lower semi-continuous. Conversely, the function $s\mapsto \delta_s$, which assigns to a geometric point $s$ the dimension of the affine part of $P_s$, is upper semi-continuous.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We may assume that $P$ has only connected fibers, and we may also assume that $S$ is strictly henselian. Choose a prime number $\ell$ prime to the residue characteristic of $S$. The kernel $P[\ell]$ of multiplication by $\ell$ is representable by an \'etale quasi-finite subgroup scheme. For every geometric point $s$ of $S$, the length of $P_s[\ell]$ is given by the formula
$$\lg(P_s[\ell])=\mu_s\ell+\tau_s\ell^2,$$
where $\mu_s$ is the dimension of the multiplicative part of $P_s$ and $\tau_s$ is the dimension of its abelian part. If $s_0$ denotes the geometric closed point of $S$ and $s_1$ the geometric generic point, one has the inequality
$$\lg(P_{s_0}[\ell])\leq \lg(P_{s_1}[\ell]),$$
since, $P[\ell]$ being \'etale over the henselian base $S$, every point in the special fiber extends to an $S$-section. The inequality
$$\mu_{s_0}\ell+\tau_{s_0}\ell^2\leq \mu_{s_1}\ell+\tau_{s_1}\ell^2$$
holding for every prime number $\ell$ prime to the residue characteristic of $S$, we obtain
$$\tau_{s_0}\leq \tau_{s_1}.$$
The other inequality follows, since $\delta_s+\tau_s=\dim(P_s)$ does not depend on the point $s$.
\end{proof}

% [journal 5.6.4] MISSING FROM THE arXiv VERSION.
\begin{lemma}\label{delta psi_G nul}
Suppose that $\deg(D)>2g$. Then $\delta(\psi_G)=0$, where $\psi_G$ is the maximal element of $\Psi$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This is immediate from \ref{delta global}.
\end{proof}

For every integer $\delta\in\mathbb{N}$, the union
$$
\overline{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_\delta:=\bigsqcup_{\delta(\psi)\geq \delta} \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\psi
$$
is a closed subset of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$. We then define 
$$\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_\delta:=\overline{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_\delta-\overline{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\delta+1},$$
which is then a locally closed subscheme of $\tilde{\mathcal{A}}$. Thus, we obtain a stratification
